It hasn’t developed property before and that’s why Earlmist was developing, Earlmist is a developer, it’s got the financial capacity to develop the second stage of the shopping centre.

  24. The issue of who the developer was is further complicated by the absence of any agreement between the owner of the land, Westpoint Management, and Earlmist, that appoint Earlmist to carry out the development. The following exchange is relevant (transcript/126):
    MR DAVIES: And you say that Earlmist is the one carrying out the development, but you are not able to point to any document that is an agreement between Westpoint Management – the owner of the land – and Earlmist, that has the consequence that the development of Westpoint Management’s land would be carried out by Earlmist?---

    MR CAREY: What I’m saying to you is, as I’ve said before, as the managing director of Earlmist, managing director of Westpoint Management, managing director of Westpoint Constructions, managing director of Westpoint Realty, in control of entities, that was what was agreed. And Earlmist was carrying out the development process with the assistance of Westpoint Constructions, Westpoint Realty, to facilitate the development of stage 2 of the shopping centre.

    MR DAVIES: ... Mr Carey, that’s a story, is it not, that’s been invented since 1 November?---

    MR CAREY: Mr Davies, I’m telling you of my own knowledge – I was there at the time, I was the decision maker of these entities, I was the architect of the project, I was the one directing the development process – that that is the case. That is my evidence.

  25. This exchange highlights a fundamental problem for Earlmist and the other Applicants which we identified earlier in these reasons. Mr Carey controlled all the entities and made decisions for each of them – often following “negotiations” that he effectively conducted with himself. Where the documents are missing, incomplete or point to inconvenient conclusions, the Applicant in each case relies on Mr Carey’s recollections of what he said, did or intended to fill in gaps in the documentation. Those omissions and inconsistencies make it difficult to ascertain what was really going on, including whether any creditable acquisitions were made and the entity they were made to.
  26. The Commissioner submitted the ‘Deed of Assignment of Option’ to Earlmist was a fabrication for the purpose of these proceedings. Mr Carey apparently intended to rely on it in these proceedings because he had it stamped on 19 October 2021. It was curious that he would stamp a document that he knew had been cancelled, although Mr Carey’s evidence was that his lawyers told him to have the document stamped (transcript/124).
  27. We do not think the evidence goes so far as to logically permit us to draw an inference that the Deed of Assignment of Option to Earlmist was fabricated. However, the conflicting evidence in Richstar about Bowesco being the developer, the absence of a deed cancelling the Deed of Assignment of Option to Earlmist, the late disclosure of the Bowesco Deed of Assignment of Option in a statement filed on 1 November 2021 after these proceedings commenced on 3 November 2021, the late stamping of the Deed of Assignment of Option to Earlmist when Mr Carey knew it had been cancelled, and the absence of any document whereby Earlmist was appointed by the owner of the land, Westpoint Management, to undertake the development, raise doubts about Earlmist’s involvement in the development and whether it made creditable acquisitions in the relevant period.
  28. We also note (a) the correspondence with the Western Australian Planning Commission and the firm of town planners who worked on the development application for lots 909 and 913, and (b) the Application for Approval to commence development refer to Westpoint Management as being the owner and contain no reference to Earlmist developing the land (TB 006.51, pdf 9-30). On the other hand, we note there are other agreements (in particular the Agency Agreements with Westpoint Realty that we will discuss in due course) which do name Earlmist as the owner and developer.
  29. However, even if we ignore these doubts and accept Mr Carey’s evidence in full that Earlmist was carrying out the development, there are other concerns raised by the evidence which further support our finding that Earlmist has not met its burden under s 14ZZK of the TAA.

What was supplied by Westpoint Constructions?

  1. The evidence does not establish whether any work was carried out under the building and construction contracts, being the Warnbro Fair D & C Contract and the Warnbro Commercial D & C Contract at the relevant time, and if it was, what that work was.
  2. The Warnbro Fair D & C Contract (described above), executed on 22 September 2005, was for the extension and redevelopment of the Warnbro Fair Shopping Centre including the design and construction of a Big W store, 50 specialty shops and the integration of the existing shopping centre and a new underground carpark for a contract sum of $31,964,000 on lots 909 and 913. However, when Landgate satellite images of the Warnbro Fair Shopping Centre as at 12 December 2004 and 16 March 2006 are compared, they are substantially the same. Those images do not show visible work that has taken place for Stage 2 on lot 913 (as well as none that is visible for lot 909) between those dates (TB 006.54).
  3. The Warnbro Commercial D & C Contract (also described above), executed on 2 October 2005, was for the design and construction of a service station, convenience centre, fast food outlet, two retail shops and car parking for a contract sum of $6,254,640 on lots 914 and 915 (which were subdivided from lot 912) and lot 911. Again, Landgate satellite images of those lots as at 12 December 2004 and 16 March 2006 are substantially the same and also show no visible work. Mr Carey’s evidence at the hearing was that the work undertaken on these lots was “design work and preliminaries” and that nothing physical happened on the land (transcript/108).
  4. Earlmist relied upon the Construction Works Summaries – Stage 2 (TB 013.20 and TB 013.21) and the Construction Works Summaries – Commercial (TB 013.18 and TB 013.19) to support the tax invoices issued by Westpoint Constructions and, specifically, to evidence work being completed for Earlmist under the Warnbro Fair D & C Contract and the Warnbro Commercial D & C Contract.
  5. However, under cross-examination, Mr Carey acknowledged the summaries included charges for work that had been carried out under the 1997 Contract and for work carried out by Westpoint Constructions (and Westpoint Realty) from 2002 through to August 2005, which pre-dated the Warnbro Fair D & C Contract and the Warnbro Commercial D & C Contract (transcript/159-160; A3/[30]). It is therefore unclear as to what, if any, work was carried out under the Warnbro Fair D & C Contract and the Warnbro Commercial D & C Contract.
  6. Mr Carey appeared to be of the view there was a somewhat fluid arrangement whereby Westpoint Constructions could charge for the work they carried out under the 1997 Contract under subsequent contracts. Mr Carey stated (transcript/159):
    The agreement with construction was that they’d carry out the work required to subdivide the land, and they would recover that from each of the design and construct building contracts, as and when those contracts arose.
  7. That evidence is not consistent with the terms of the Warnbro Fair D & C Contract and the Warnbro Commercial D & C Contract. Those contracts are not expressed to have retrospective application. They do not refer to the 1997 Contract, nor do they impose any liability on Earlmist to pay for work under that earlier contract.
  8. Also, the work under the 1997 Contract was completed sometime in 1998 (transcript/158) and so it is surprising work completed under that contract would be included in construction works summaries from 20 December 2005 and 14 January 2006 and charged for under subsequent contracts.
  9. In his statement dated 5 July 2021 (A3/[18] and [31]), Mr Carey says the amounts in the Construction Works Summaries – Stage 2 for works up to 14 January 2006 and the Construction Works Summaries – Commercial for works up to 14 January 2006 were both verified by another related entity he controlled, the Westpoint Consulting Group Pty Ltd (‘Westpoint Consulting’). Westpoint Consulting assessed the value of the work undertaken by Westpoint Constructions and recommended payment in letters dated 14 January 2006 for Warnbro Shopping Centre – Stage 2 (TB 015.4) and 15 January 2006 for Warnbro Commercial Centre (TB 015.2). We note there is a generally expressed list of the works completed in these letters and that the amounts in the valuations match the amounts in both Construction Works Summaries (being $1,218,800 and $984,775). However, it is not clear which of the works were carried out under the 1997 Contract and what work was undertaken before the Warnbro Fair D & C Contract and the Warnbro Commercial D & C Contract commenced. The letters were signed but it is not clear who the signatory was. The costs are stated, but apart from reference to a site visit it is also unclear how those costs were assessed. We therefore agree with the Commissioner’s submission that these two letters are of limited probative weight.
  10. In summary, we cannot tell what work, if any, was carried out for Earlmist under the Warnbro Fair D & C Contract and the Warnbro Commercial D & C Contract. There is insufficient evidence of what was supplied, if anything, by Westpoint Constructions to Earlmist pursuant to the tax invoices issued by Westpoint Constructions.

What did Westpoint Realty supply to Earlmist?

  1. The Agency Agreements and Management Agreements relied upon by Earlmist are between Westpoint Realty and Earlmist.
  2. The Introduction to the Agency Agreements states that, “The Principal owns and develops property in Western Australia, more specifically described in Annexure A”. Thus, Earlmist is represented as the owner and developer of that property, being the Warnbro Fair Shopping Centre Stage 2 development (lots 909 and 913) and the Warnbro Commercial Land (lots 911, 914 and 915) (TB 006.10, pdf 98, 111).
  3. The Management Agreements provide that Earlmist is or will be the owner of the respective properties (Recital A) (TB 006.19, pdf 58, 77).
  4. There is a problem for Earlmist with respect to the Warnbro Fair Shopping Centre Stage 2 development (lots 909 and 913). As we have outlined above, on 8 November 1999, lots 909 and 913 were transferred to Westpoint Management. Mr Carey’s evidence was that the assignment to Earlmist was cancelled and replaced with an assignment to Bowesco dated 7 October 2005. Based on Mr Carey’s evidence, Earlmist did not become the owner of lots 909 and 913, nor did it have any interest under the option because it had been cancelled. Thus, there is apparently no legal basis upon which Earlmist could appoint agents and managers for the Warnbro Fair Shopping Centre Stage 2 development because they were not the owner, nor were they the prospective owner of the land. The basis on which Earlmist could charge Westpoint Realty for agency or management fees under those agreements is unclear. It is therefore curious that the project cost summaries for the Warnbro Fair Shopping Centre Stage 2 land show Westpoint Realty charged a “Commission – Big W (ATL) [agreement to lease]” of $725,000, shown in the project cost summary dated 20 December 2005 and $575,000 shown in the project cost summary dated 16 January 2006 (TB 006.19, pdf 92-93). We do not have a copy of this agreement to lease, so we have no confirmation that it was ever entered into, which is a precondition for being able to charge that commission under clause 4.2.1 of the Management Agreement which refers to the execution of an agreement for lease before the entitlement to the full leasing fee arises. In any event, it would be the registered proprietor who would have to consent to the agreement for lease, and not Earlmist who had no interest in the land. In this regard, we note a draft agreement for lease with Big W where the details of the Landlord on the first and execution pages are blank (TB 006.20, pdf 26 and 36).
  5. Regarding the Warnbro Fair Shopping Centre Stage 2, to the extent that marketing, legal and administration services are stated in the project cost summaries, it is unclear to us what exactly was supplied to Earlmist in December 2005 and January 2006. There is no documentation before us to substantiate how those specific sums were arrived at.
  6. There is, however, some undated documentation relied upon by Earlmist to support the existence of marketing costs for the Warnbro Commercial Centre. These are:
    (a) ‘Warnbro Commercial Centre Marketing Strategy and Leasing Plan’ (TB 015.1, pdf 73-85). Although this document is undated, the header on the last two pages, pages 12 and 13 of the document is ‘WMPC Marketing and Sales Plan 2017’ which suggests this document was created over a decade after the tax invoices.

    (b) A two page ‘Westpoint Realty Warnbro Commercial Centre For Sale and Lease’ Brochure (TB 015.1, pdf 86-87).

    (c) A document titled, ‘Warnbro Commercial Centre Leasing Opportunity’ (TB 015.1, pdf 88-98).

  7. However, these documents are general in nature. They do not specifically refer to, nor do they provide evidence of what was supplied by Westpoint Realty to Earlmist in December 2005 and January 2006 for the Warnbro Commercial Centre. Like the Warnbro Fair Shopping Centre Stage 2, it is unclear what exactly was supplied for the amounts of legal and administration services that were stated in the project cost summaries for the Warnbro Commercial Centre.
  8. The project cost summaries for Warnbro Commercial Centre refer to a leasing “Commission – Service Station Complex (ATL) [agreement to lease]” for $650,000 on 20 December 2005 (TB 006.19, pdf 73) and for $535,000 on 16 January 2006 (TB 006.19, pdf 74). On 7 December 2005, Earlmist (with a wrongly stated ACN) entered into an agreement for lease with Raeside Pty Ltd (ACN 008 960 923) with respect to lots 911, 914 and 915 (TB 006.20, pdf 46). The agreement for lease provided Westpoint Constructions would construct a building on the land and that Raeside would be allowed to occupy the service station complex. According to our reading of clause 4.2.1(i)(ii) of the Management Agreement for the Warnbro Fair Commercial Centre, Westpoint Realty was entitled to a leasing fee of 12% of the first term gross income if an agreement for lease was signed (TB 006.19, pdf 65). According to Annexure B of the lease attached to the agreement for lease, the term of the lease was 10 years, and the annual rent was $988,000 (TB 006.20, pdf 86). Thus, the total first term rent was $9,880,000, and 12% of this sum is $1,185,600. The leasing fees claimed in the two tax invoices total $1,185,000. This evidence would ordinarily support a finding that Westpoint Realty made this supply. However, the problem is, to whom was the supply made? Earlmist was not the owner of the land, nor the prospective owner, and was not capable of entering into a lease (or an agreement to lease). It is unclear what (if anything) was supplied to Earlmist.

Issues regarding the payment of the tax invoices via set-off

  1. Earlmist alleges the tax invoices were paid by way of a set-off recorded in book entries. It relies on unstamped loan agreements between each of the entities, drawdown notices, journal entries and trial balances.
  2. In his witness statement dated 26 April 2016 (A1, paras [47]-[49]), Mr Carey explained Westpoint Corporation operated as a central banker to the other entities in the group. He also explained how work was paid for. We have summarised this evidence, using Earlmist to illustrate how the process worked:
    (a) Westpoint Corporation would advance loan funds pursuant to a loan agreement entered into with the entity undertaking the development (in this application, Earlmist).

    (b) Earlmist would then advance loan funds pursuant to a loan agreement with Westpoint Constructions to carry out design and construction work pursuant to the design and construct agreement for the Project.

    (c) Westpoint Constructions issued tax invoices for the work it undertook under the design and construct agreement.

    (d) Payment of the Westpoint Constructions tax invoices was made by the Westpoint Constructions loan being repaid by the amount of the tax invoice.

    (e) Earlmist would also advance loan funds pursuant to a loan agreement with Westpoint Realty to carry out the marketing, selling, leasing and property management work pursuant to the agency agreement for the project.

    (f) Westpoint Realty issued tax invoices for the services they provided pursuant to the agency agreement.

    (g) Payment of the Westpoint Realty tax invoices was made by the Westpoint Realty Loan being repaid by the amount of the tax invoice.

  3. Mr Carey explained the loans were effected by drawdown notices executed by the borrowing entities. He said the transactions were recorded in the accounts of the entities and the Westpoint group, including journal entries, general ledger, and trial balance records.
  4. We first turn our attention to the specific loan agreements and drawdown notices relied upon by Earlmist.
  5. The loan agreements provide for a maximum sum that can be borrowed (the commitment). When the borrower wanted to borrow money, the borrower had to give the lender a drawdown notice specifying the sum to be borrowed. Each of the loan agreements states the wrong ACN, which belongs to another company, Eastlands Pty Ltd, on the cover page, however the correct ACN for the Earlmist Company is in the Schedule.
  6. All the loan agreements were executed by Mr Carey as Director of both entities and Mr Rundle as Company Secretary of both entities. All the drawdown notices were signed by Mr Carey as “Authorised Officer”.
  7. The specific loan agreements and corresponding drawdown notices were:
    (a) A (hitherto unstamped) loan agreement dated 12 June 2002 between Westpoint Corporation as lender and Earlmist as borrower for a maximum commitment of $18,000,000 (TB 006.19, pdf 43).
On 31 October 2005 Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating that Earlmist wished to draw $14,311,779.27 on 31 October 2005 (TB 006.19, pdf 55).
(b) A loan agreement dated 4 July 2005 between Earlmist as lender and Westpoint Realty as borrower for a maximum commitment of $6,000,000 (TB 006.15, pdf 19). This agreement was executed by Mr Carey as Director of both entities and Mr Rundle as Company Secretary of both entities.

On 31 October 2005 Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating that Westpoint Realty wished to draw $4,841,918 on 31 October 2005 (TB 006.17, pdf 9).

(c) A loan agreement dated 5 July 2005 between Earlmist as lender and Westpoint Constructions as borrower for a maximum commitment of $12,000,000 (TB 006.15, pdf 7).

On 31 October 2005 Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating that Westpoint Constructions wished to draw $9,487,615 on 31 October 2005 (TB 006.17, pdf 8).

  1. The Commissioner submitted the evidence does not establish loan funds were in fact provided pursuant to the drawdown notices (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [173]).
  2. Westpoint Corporation’s bank account that was used for its central treasury function in 2004 was identified by Mr Carey in his witness statement (A1, para [46]). However, the bank statement for that account does not show a drawdown of $14,311,779.27 on or around that time. Indeed, the statement for the period 31 October 2005 to 4 November 2005 has an opening balance of $184,138.67 and a closing balance of $4,041,328.60 (TB 006.63, pdf 125).
  3. Likewise, the bank statement for Westpoint Constructions has an opening balance of $611,561.13 as at 31 October 2005 and does not show Westpoint Constructions received a deposit of $4,841,918 (TB 006.62, pdf 1). The bank statement for Westpoint Realty has a balance of $134,184.86 on 31 October 2005 and does not show Westpoint Realty received a deposit of $9,487,615 (TB 006.71, pdf 23-25).
  4. The Commissioner also submitted there are no corresponding records from Westpoint Corporation, Westpoint Constructions or Westpoint Realty, including accounting records, to show loan funds were provided to Earlmist under the drawdown notices (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [176]).
  5. Earlmist argues the Commissioner’s submission shows a misunderstanding of the way in which the Westpoint group operated (Applicant’s closing submissions in reply, para [64]) and that evidence of cash transfers is not required. That argument is consistent with Goods and Services Tax Determination GSTD 2004/4 Goods and services tax: can consideration for a supply be provided or received without transferring money (such as where the parties only make book entries recording their agreement that the supply is paid for)? (GSTD 2004/4) which suggests a set-off can be made by a book-entry in journals, ledgers, or other accounting records of the entity and that a book entry does not need to extend so far as to include the deposit of monies in a bank account. (GSTD 2004/4 is not binding on us, but we accept its analysis of the law is sound and it provides useful guidance as to what would ordinarily be expected when a set-off is claimed.) The existence of bank deposits would certainly be beneficial for Earlmist in meeting the burden under s 14ZZK of the TAA, but the absence of that evidence is not fatal. There are other expectations for a set-off by an associated entity that are explained in GSTD 2004/4 that have not been satisfied by Earlmist. We will return to this in a moment, but first it is relevant to consider the specific journal and general ledger accounts relied upon.
  6. Each of the journal entries relied upon by Earlmist are handwritten on a “Journal Entry Form” template. Each describes the “Journal Details” as “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices”. Each describes the “Entity” as “(24) Earlmist”. The forms are incomplete. On each form, several important boxes have been left blank. These are for the “journal number”, “date prepared”, “prepared by”, “entered by”, “authorised by” and “date entered”. They are:
    (a) A journal entry form with an “effective journal date” of 20 December 2005. Each of the amounts in the four tax invoices dated 20 December 2005 are set-off against the respective loan accounts of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty (TB 006.10, pdf 132).

    (b) A journal entry form with an “effective journal date” of 14 January 2006. The amounts in the two 14 January 2006 tax invoices are set-off against the loan account of Westpoint Constructions (TB 006.10, pdf 133).

    (c) A journal entry form with an “effective journal date” of 16 January 2006. The amounts in the two 16 January 2006 tax invoices are set-off against the loan account of Westpoint Realty (TB 006.10, pdf 133).

  7. Earlmist relies on a ‘GL [General Ledger] Account Year to Date Transaction Report’ of Earlmist for ‘Loan – Westgem Unit Trust’ showing:
    (a) A “Loan drawdown” on 31 October 2005 of $4,841,918 by the Westgem Unit Trust (TB 006.15, pdf 32-33).

    (b) Two “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices” on 20 December 2005 showing the amounts payable in the tax invoices ($720,500 and $821,150) being set-off against the loan account for Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem (TB 006.15, pdf 34).

    (c) Two “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices” on 16 January 2006 showing the amounts payable in the tax invoices ($578,050 and $642,400) being set-off against the loan account for Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem (TB 006.15, pdf 35).

  8. Earlmist also relies upon a ‘GL Account Year to Date Transaction Report’ of Earlmist for ‘Loan – Westpoint Constructions’ showing:
    (a) A “Loan drawdown” on 31 October 2005 of $9,487,615 by Westpoint Constructions (TB 006.15, pdf 38-39).

    (b) Two “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices” on 20 December 2005 showing the amounts payable in the tax invoices ($1,479,500 and $1,595,000) being set-off against the loan account for Westpoint Constructions (TB 006.15, pdf 40).

    (c) Two “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices” on 14 January 2006 showing the amounts payable in the tax invoices ($984,775 and $1,218,800) being set-off against the loan account for Westpoint Constructions (TB 006.15, pdf 41).

  9. Further, a trial balance for Earlmist as at 31 October 2005 shows:
    (a) A credit of $14,311,779.27 (TB 006.15, pdf 44) for “Loan – Westpoint Corporation P/L” whereby, according to Mr Carey, Westpoint Corporations advanced loan funds to Earlmist pursuant to the loan agreement dated 12 June 2002 (A1, para [134(a)]).

    (b) Debit entries showing the loan drawdowns of $4,841,918 by the Westgem Unit Trust and $9,487,615 by Westpoint Constructions (TB 006.15, pdf 44).

  10. Another trial balance for Earlmist as at 31 December 2005 (TB 006.15, pdf 46) shows:
    (a) A debit of $4,225,038.15 with the notation “WIP Earlmist”. Earlmist describes this sum as the GST-exclusive amounts payable by Earlmist to Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty in respect of the tax invoices dated 20 December 2005 (opening submissions, para [57(b)] of Annexure 4 Earlmist). (We note in passing that these numbers do not add up. The total of the 20 December 2005 tax invoices is $4,616,150. When we subtract the GST component of $419,650, the amount comes to $4,196,500. It may be that nothing turns on this discrepancy.)

    (b) A debit of “GST paid” of $419,650.35, being the amounts of GST under the 20 December 2005 invoices.

    (c) A credit towards the “Loan – Westpoint Constructions” of $3,074,500 being a reduction in the loan to pay the invoices issued by Westpoint Constructions on 20 December 2005.

    (d) A credit towards the “Loan – Westgem Unit Trust” of $1,541,650 being a reduction in the loan to pay the invoices issued by Westpoint Realty dated 20 December 2005.

  11. Another trial balance for Earlmist as at 31 January 2006 (TB 006.15, pdf 47) shows:
    (a) A debit of $3,112,750 with the notation “WIP Earlmist”, which was also described by Earlmist in their opening submissions as the GST exclusive amounts payable by Earlmist to Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty in respect of the tax invoices dated 14 January 2006 and 16 January 2006. This sum does add up. The total of the tax invoices is $3,424,025, less the GST component of $311,275 is $3,112,750.

    (b) A debit of “GST paid” of $311,275, being the amounts of GST under the 14 and 16 January 2006 invoices.

    (c) A credit towards the “Loan – Westpoint Constructions” of $2,203,575 being a reduction in the loan to pay the invoices issued by Westpoint Constructions on 14 January 2006.

    (d) A credit towards the “Loan – Westgem Unit Trust” of $1,220,450 being a reduction in the loan to pay the invoices issued by Westpoint Realty dated 16 January 2006.

  12. The Commissioner submitted that no records could be extracted by an independent information technology expert from the Triumph database produced by ASIC to corroborate the accounting entries we have just outlined above. The Commissioner also observes that when the specific account numbers were searched, they did not show the loan drawdowns and the amounts payable under the tax invoices (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [178]; TB 006.59, pdf 3 to 10).
  13. Further, while there are no corresponding accounts for Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty showing the transactions, importantly, there is no evidence the companies accepted the mutual set-off liabilities relied upon by Earlmist (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [179]). This, in our view, is a significant problem for Earlmist for reasons that we will now explain.
  14. The GSTD 2004/4 provides useful guidance with respect to set-off and associated entities. The relevant part provides (see para [29]-[30]):
    ... the act of making book entries indicates the provision and receipt of consideration for GST purposes if:
  • an entity (Entity A) has an obligation to provide consideration to another entity (Entity B) for a supply;
  • entities A and B are associates of one another within the meaning of the GST Act;
  • entities A and B have a binding agreement, whether express or implied, that, instead of Entity A making any payment (whether monetary or non-monetary) in respect of the obligation to Entity B, the entities will dispense with the obligation by making book entries in each entity’s books of account;
  • the agreement contemplates that, once the entries are made, the obligation to pay for the supply will no longer be recorded as an obligation in either entity’s books of account and the statement of financial position of each entity at the end of the relevant financial year will reflect that the obligation has been replaced with an obligation to repay an equal amount lent to A (that is, the obligation to pay is not treated as forgiven or otherwise discharged for less than its original amount); and
  • the book entries made in each entity’s books of account reflect the carrying into effect of the agreement and correspond with each other.
    (Our emphasis.)
  1. As this guidance indicates, one would ordinarily expect evidence of an express or at least implied agreement between the entities that set-off can be used. There is no clear evidence of such an understanding in this case, including in the loan agreements. Also, one would expect each entities’ end of year financial statements would reflect that an obligation to pay has been replaced with the set-off arrangement. There is no evidence of that either.
  2. The timing of the tax invoices, intercompany loans, and set-offs is also unfortunate. We have already noted there was not enough money in the relevant accounts to loan and history tells us the companies were facing imminent collapse. On 2 February 2006 a receiver was appointed to Westpoint Constructions. A liquidator was appointed on 22 February 2006 (TB 006.40, pdf 5). On 20 April 2006 a receiver and manager was appointed to Westpoint Realty. An administrator was appointed on 16 January 2007, and then a liquidator on 13 April 2007 (TB 006.42, pdf 1 and 6).
  3. Additionally, the Commissioner points out reports as to affairs made by the liquidators do not show these inter-company debts and mutual set-offs alleged by Earlmist (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [182]). Specifically, a report as to affairs of the Earlmist Company dated 29 May 2006 was prepared by receiver and manager Mr Oren Zohar (TB 006.44, pdf 1). It specified “sundry debtors as detailed in schedule B” as $388,680.68 (TB 006.44, pdf 6). These debtors included loan debtors. This sum was comprised of a debt for $13,747.60 for “ATO – GST” and a debt for $374,933.08 for “Related Party Loans” (TB 006.44, pdf 8). This latter sum is substantially less than the amounts owing under the inter-company loans.
  4. The Commissioner has also referred to other reports prepared during the administration and liquidation of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty that do not correspond to the amounts Earlmist alleges were owing under the loan arrangements. These included:
    (a) A report prepared by the administrators of Westpoint Realty dated 2 February 2007 listed monies owed by Westpoint Realty to other entities, however Earlmist was not listed, indicating that no money was owed to Earlmist (TB 006.47 pdf 1, 17 and 22).

    (b) A report as to affairs of Westpoint Constructions by the liquidator, which was verified by Mr Graeme Rundle on 9 August 2006 (TB 006.46, pdf 2), did not list Earlmist as an unsecured creditor (TB 006.46, pdf 9). There is also a list of work in progress as at 22 February 2006 which does not include the Warnbro Fair Shopping Centre or the Warnbro Commercial Land (TB 006.46, pdf 11).

    (c) A report as to affairs of Westpoint Corporation dated 27 February 2006 (TB 031.A1, pdf 1) certified by Mr Carey and Mr Graeme Rundle on 23 February 2006, and Mr Nairn (TB 031.A1, pdf 5) shows that Earlmist was owed $374,933.08 (TB 031.A1, pdf 59). This is substantially different to the trial balance for Earlmist as at 31 January 2006 which shows that Earlmist owed Westpoint Corporation $17,308,299.25 (TB 006.15, pdf 47).

  5. Mr Carey claimed the records of Earlmist and the reports as to affairs were inconsistent because the receivers and liquidators did not have all the information and were working off financial accounts that had not been updated. At the hearing he explained ASIC had seized all the books and records of the group so there were no current records to work on (transcript/173). He estimated the balances in the 27 February 2006 report as to affairs were probably balances as at 30 June 2005 that had not been updated because of the records being seized by ASIC. Mr Carey said (transcript/173) that he signed the report as to affairs of Westpoint Corporation dated 27 February 2006 with a declaration that stated (A11):
    As a result of ASIC’s intervention with KPMG’s audit of Westpoint entities 30/6/2005 financial accounts these accounts were not fully updated. In January 2006 ASIC seized Westpoint entities books and records. In these circumstances we have made our best reasonable endeavours to fill out the RATA for Westpoint Corporation Pty Ltd dated 23/02/2006.
  6. However, there is no mention in any of the reports as to affairs by the receivers and liquidators that they were lacking up-to-date accounting information. We also note Mr Rundle was not called as a witness in these proceedings. Indeed, Mr Rundle could have shed light on some of the issues and transactions before us given he was the Company Secretary who executed the agency agreements, loan agreements and design and construct contracts relied upon by Earlmist. The receivers and liquidators could also have been called to identify the information before them, but they were not.

Other evidence that might assist

  1. The GST reporting by Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty is also inconsistent with those entities having made taxable supplies to Earlmist.
  2. Westpoint Constructions lodged BASs for the relevant periods:
    (a) 1 October 2005 to 31 December 2005, lodged on 2 March 2006 (TB 006.34, pdf 1).

    (b) 1 January 2006 to 31 January 2006, lodged on 2 March 2006 (TB 006.35, pdf 1) after Westpoint Constructions went into external administration on 2 February 2006.

  3. Westpoint Realty lodged BASs for the relevant periods:
    (a) 1 October 2005 to 31 December 2005, lodged on 27 February 2006 (TB 006.37, pdf 1-2) completed by a person named Tracey Fox.

    (b) 1 January 2006 to 31 March 2006, (TB 006.38, pdf 1), also completed by Tracey Fox. This BAS was lodged on 2 August 2006 after Westpoint Realty had a receiver and manager appointed on 20 April 2006.

  4. The Commissioner points out these BASs do not record any corresponding taxable supplies to Earlmist in accordance with the tax invoices issued to Earlmist. Mr Carey’s evidence was that the taxable supplies may not have been correctly reported because the companies were wound up or had receivers and liquidators appointed (transcript/90) and because ASIC had seized documents from January 2006 (transcript/99). That may be the case, but it does not help the evidentiary problem faced by Earlmist that there is no corroborating evidence of creditable acquisitions by Earlmist in the BASs of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty.

Failure to stamp documents

  1. Except where we have stated otherwise, none of the documents relied upon by Earlmist have been stamped. An organisation with a pattern of failing to stamp documents raises questions over that organisation’s commitment to regular and orderly record-keeping. This raises doubts about its business practices if only because those practices may not be properly documented. At a minimum, the routine failure to stamp documents suggests a casual approach to legal obligations. Those shortcomings are ultimately the responsibility of Mr Carey. On his own evidence, Mr Carey effectively controlled all the entities in a multi-million-dollar corporate group and played a central role in their operations. He should have been aware, and his companies’ lawyers at the relevant times should have been aware, that there is a legal requirement to stamp many documents, such as agency agreements, construction and design contracts and loan agreements. The failure to stamp documents also compounds an evidentiary problem: where documents are stamped in the ordinary course, the stamping process would help establish when the document was created. A routine failure to stamp documents raises concerns over the regularity of the records and the transactions they purport to record, the credibility of the person seeking to rely on those documents, and the timing of their creation. This, in our view, adds further doubt into the application.

Conclusion for Earlmist

  1. We are not satisfied Earlmist has established on the evidence that it made creditable acquisitions from Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty in the period under review. In summary, our reasons for that conclusion include:
    (a) It is unclear who made the alleged creditable acquisitions because it is unclear whether the developer was Earlmist or Bowesco. In this regard, we note the conflicting evidence Mr Carey gave to the Federal Court in Richstar.

    (b) It is also unclear what, if any, work was carried out by Earlmist under the building and construction contracts.

    (c) There was no clear legal basis upon which Earlmist could charge Westpoint Realty for agency or management fees given the doubts over whether Earlmist was the owner or prospective owner of the land.

    (d) It is unclear exactly what (if anything) was supplied by Westpoint Realty to Earlmist for the amounts of marketing, legal and administration services stated in the project cost summaries.

    (e) The journal entry forms which are key documents relied upon by Earlmist to evidence the set-off of the tax invoices are incomplete, with several important boxes having been left blank.

    (f) There is a lack of evidence of any implied or express binding agreement between Earlmist and Westpoint Constructions and Earlmist and Westpoint Realty that the entities would dispense with the obligation to pay for the tax invoices by mutual book entries.

    (g) A search of the accounts of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty do not show the corresponding loan drawdowns and the amounts payable under the tax invoices as reflected in the documentation Earlmist relies upon.

    (h) There is an absence of mutual book entries for the associated entities showing the set-off.

    (i) The reports of affairs are inconsistent with the existence of inter-company debts, the amounts claimed to be owed to Earlmist under the relevant invoices and Mr Carey’s evidence that they were prepared based on a lack of updated financial information.

    (j) The failure to contemporaneously stamp documents raises concerns about their provenance and the persons who seek to rely upon them, in this instance, Mr Carey.

  2. There are so many doubts raised by the evidence before us that we are not persuaded Earlmist has discharged the burden of proving its net amount under for the December 2005 and January 2006 tax periods is less than zero. Earlmist has not established it made creditable acquisitions from Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty whereby Earlmist was entitled to input tax credits for GST of $419,650 and $311,275 as stated in those tax invoices.
  3. We therefore find that the Earlmist Reviewable Decision should be affirmed.

SILKCHIME

The entity

  1. Mr Carey’s evidence was that the Silkchime Unit Trust (Silkchime Trust) was established on 31 October 1994 to develop the Warwick Commercial Park project (A1, paras [155] and [157]).
  2. Silkchime Pty Ltd (ACN 066 849 429) (Silkchime Company) was the Trustee of the Silkchime Trust at all relevant times.
  3. We will refer to the Applicant entity, Silkchime Pty Ltd as Trustee for the Silkchime Unit Trust, as Silkchime.
  4. Mr Carey has been a Director of the Silkchime Company since December 1994, together with Ping Kuen Ho and Peng Phui Ho (TB 012.29, pdf 2). Mr Carey was appointed Company Secretary from December 1994. Mr Rundle was a Company Secretary from July 1997 to January 2006 (TB 012.29, pdf 3).
  5. According to Mr Carey, the unitholders of the Silkchime Trust were Rompride Pty Ltd as trustee for the Erley Unit Trust and Greenleaf Holdings Pty Ltd as Trustee for the Greenleaf Unit Trust. Mr Carey’s said he was a beneficiary of the Erley Unit Trust but the beneficiaries of the Greenleaf Unit Trust were two Singaporean brothers, Patrick and Steven Ho (who appear to be the other directors of the Silkchime Company, Ping Kuen Ho and Peng Phui Ho) (transcript/87).
  6. Receivers were first appointed to the Silkchime Company on 24 January 2006 (TB 012.29, pdf 3).

The assessment

  1. Silkchime Company was registered to account for GST from 1 July 2000 (TB 012.30, pdf 1) on a quarterly and a cash basis (TB 012.6, pdf 3).
  2. On 13 November 2012, Silkchime lodged a BAS for the quarterly tax period 1 October 2005 to 31 December 2005 (TB 012.3, pdf 1). Silkchime claimed it was entitled to a refund of $596,150 on the basis it was entitled to input tax credits on non-capital purchases of $6,557,650 (TB 012.3, pdf 2).
  3. Again, following the receipt of this BAS, the Commissioner initiated a review. As with the review of Earlmist, this was likely because in the previous tax periods Silkchime had lodged BASs claiming far lesser amounts of input tax credits of between $1,300 and $11,800, except for the quarter ended 30 June 2005 where the largest amount of $49,283 was claimed (TB 012.4, pdf 1).
  4. On 22 November 2012, the Commissioner decided to revise the BAS to nil which reflected the Commissioner’s view that Silkchime was not entitled to any input tax credits (TB 012.6, pdf 1 and 3). The Commissioner duly issued a notice of assessment on 23 November 2012 whereby Silkchime’s net amount was changed to nil (TB 012.7, pdf 1).

The Reviewable Decision

  1. Silkchime made a late objection to the notice of assessment on 1 September 2014 (TB 012.8). On 4 September 2015, Silkchime’s tax advisors submitted a notice under s 14ZYA of the TAA requiring the Commissioner to make an objection decision (TB 012.22).
  2. On 29 September 2015, the Commissioner agreed to an extension of time (TB 012.24).
  3. The Commissioner did not make a decision within 60 days of the s 14ZYA notice (by 3 November 2015) and so was taken to have disallowed the objection (TB 012.1, pdf 2). We will refer to this as the ‘Silkchime Reviewable Decision’.
  4. On 5 November 2015, Silkchime sought review of the Silkchime Reviewable Decision in this Tribunal (TB 012.1).

The tax invoices

  1. In Silkchime’s objection dated 1 September 2014, it was contended Silkchime operated a “property development enterprise and was involved in the Warwick Project” (TB 012.8, pdf 7). The Warwick Project was described as follows (TB 012.8, pdf 8):
    Project 1 – Warwick Commercial Park (“the Warwick Project”)

    Design and construction of five buildings within the Warwick Commercial Park set out below:

    Lot 961 Professional centre comprising 1,200m2 of professional offices in a two storey building with adjacent car parking

    Lot 944 Amusement centre of 700 m2

    Lot 965 Commercial units of 1,350 m2 with adjacent car parking

    Lot 956 Service station incorporating a convenience store and a fast food outlet and adjacent car parking

    Lot 946 Video store of 650 m2 with adjacent car parking

    The tenancies were to be leased and sold on a strata-titled basis.

  2. Like the Earlmist objection, the Silkchime objection claimed Silkchime had engaged Westpoint Constructions to design and construct the project and Westpoint Realty to “carry out the marketing and sales of the project” (TB 012.8, pdf 8).
  3. The following tax invoices issued to Silkchime for the tax period 1 October 2005 to 31 December 2005, were relied upon by Silkchime (TB 012.8):
    (a) ‘Tax Invoice – Number 0629 – 01 Warwick Commercial Park’ dated 30 November 2005 issued by Westpoint Constructions in the sum of $2,601,500 for “value of work completed to date” including GST of $236,500 (pdf 72).

    (b) ‘Tax Invoice – Number 0629 – 02 Warwick Commercial Park’ dated 20 December 2005 issued by Westpoint Constructions in the sum of $2,413,400 for “value of work completed to date” including GST of $219,400 (pdf 74).

    (c) Tax invoice numbered 071-01 dated 30 November 2005 issued by Westpoint Realty to the Silkchime Company for “Marketing, Selling and Administration Fees” in the sum of $840,400 including GST of $76,400 (pdf 73).

    (d) Tax invoice numbered 071-02 dated 20 December 2005 issued by Westpoint Realty to the Silkchime Company for “Marketing, Selling and Administration Fees” in the sum of $702,350 including GST of $63,850 (pdf 75).

Documentation relied upon by Silkchime

  1. We will provide an overview of the documentation relied upon by Silkchime to establish it made creditable acquisitions from Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty.
  2. All the contracts and agreements relied upon were signed by Mr Carey as Director and Mr Rundle as Company Secretary of each entity.
  3. As was the case with Earlmist, Mr Carey claimed he sent a letter dated 3 September 2009 to the Deputy Commissioner of Taxation (TB 012.8, pdf 102). The letter was worded the same as the Earlmist letter we have already discussed but referred to Silkchime being entitled to the “substantial GST refunds”.
  4. Again, we were told there was no record of this letter being received, and again, there is a statutory declaration dated 24 July 2014 from Ms Gray where she recalls typing the letter for Mr Carey, Mr Carey signing it, and the letter being posted that day. We draw the same conclusions from this letter as for the Earlmist letter – namely, that we accept the letter was sent. It is worth reiterating that, although the despatch of the letter referred to in the statutory declaration is consistent with (a) the Applicant’s apparent belief at the time that the seizure of records made it difficult to accurately complete the BAS, and (b) Mr Carey’s contemporaneous belief a “substantial refund” was due to Silkchime, that evidence does not of itself demonstrate whether creditable acquisitions were made by Silkchime from any particular entity, or what (if anything) was supplied.

Documents said to establish taxable supplies were made by Westpoint Constructions

  1. In its objection, Silkchime claimed the tax invoices related to supplies made to it by Westpoint Constructions under a ‘Design & Construct – Lump sum contract’ dated 7 August 2005 (TB 012.8, pdf 18-19) between Silkchime and Westpoint Constructions for a contract sum of $11,325,500 (TB 012.8, pdf 52) (‘Warwick D & C Contract’). (We note this contract appears to have been based on the same template used by Earlmist.) Under the Warwick D & C Contract for the design and construction of the Warwick Project, Silkchime engaged Westpoint Constructions to design and construct five buildings within the Warwick Commercial Park including a professional centre, amusement centre, commercial units, service station incorporating a convenience store and fast-food outlet and a video store.
  2. In addition, Silkchime now relies upon an agreement dated 17 September 1997 between Silkchime and Westpoint Constructions (‘1997 Building Contract’) which Mr Carey only referred to in his statement dated 5 July 2021 (A3, paras [52] and [55]-[56]). Under this contract Silkchime purportedly engaged Westpoint Constructions to subdivide an original super lot (TB 015.6, pdf 1-2). The subdivision work included design, preliminaries, demolition, earthworks, siteworks, the set out of footings and retention for the basement and ground floor and the provision of services including water, sewerage, and electricity (TB 015.6, pdf 14). Silkchime says that work was carried out under this contract and the charges were recorded by Westpoint Constructions in the 30 November 2005 and 20 December 2005 tax invoices.
  3. Silkchime also relies upon two documents named, ‘progress payment – construction works summary Warwick Commercial Park’ to substantiate taxable supplies were made to it. We will collectively refer to these as the Construction Works Summaries – Warwick. These showed progress payments:
    (a) for works up to 30 November 2005 with “this payment value” being a total of $2,601,500, marked progress claim number one (TB 013.24); and

    (a) for works up to 20 December 2005 with “this payment value” being a total of $2,413,400, marked progress claim number two (TB 013.25).

Documents said to establish taxable supplies were made to Westpoint Realty

  1. In their objection, Silkchime relies on an Agency Agreement made on 16 June 2005 between Silkchime and Westpoint Realty whereby Silkchime, as the owner, appointed Westpoint Realty to market and sell the properties in the Warwick Commercial Park development (TB 012.8, pdf 58-60). Silkchime relied on the Agency Agreement as the basis of charging sales commission (TB 012.15, pdf 9). This Agency Agreement is in substantially the same terms as the Agency Agreement for Earlmist.
  2. On approximately 19 December 2014, Silkchime’s tax advisors provided another agreement to the Commissioner (TB 012.15, pdf 1). It was a ‘Property Management Service Agreement Warwick Commercial Park’ between Silkchime and Westpoint Realty, executed on 8 June 2005 (‘Management Agreement’). In this Management Agreement, Silkchime, as the owner of the property, appointed Westpoint Realty to carry out management services for Warwick Commercial Park (TB 012.15, pdf 11-29). Silkchime relied on the Agency Agreement as the basis for charging leasing fees and the on-charge of expenses (TB 012.15, pdf 9). This Management Agreement is also in substantially the same terms as the Management Agreement for Earlmist, with the same term of five years and the same fees and charges.
  3. Silkchime also relies upon two project cost summaries for the Warwick Commercial Park project. Each has a breakdown of the costs in the tax invoices issued to Westpoint Realty. The costs are for marketing (with numerous sub-categories), selling, leasing and administration. They are titled ‘Westpoint Realty – Project Cost Summary’ and are (TB 012.15):
    (a) dated 30 November 2005 for a total (including GST) of $840,400 (pdf 28); and

    (b) dated 20 December 2015 (sic) for a total (including GST) of $702,350 (pdf 29).

Has Silkchime established it made creditable acquisitions?

  1. Like Earlmist, there are issues and concerns with the documentation relied upon by Silkchime that suggest Silkchime has not discharged its burden under s 14ZZK of the TAA. While Mr Carey sought to fill the gaps with his recollections, there is too much in doubt for us to be satisfied on the evidence that Silkchime made creditable acquisitions as it contends. Our reasons are as follows.

Who made the creditable acquisitions?

  1. Silkchime became the registered proprietor of the land on 27 January 1995 (TB 012.13, pdf 12) and the land was subsequently subdivided. There are no issues, such as there were for Earlmist, with respect to the entity that received any taxable supplies.

What was supplied by Westpoint Constructions?

  1. Clause 1 of the 1997 Building Contract provided that the builder, Westpoint Constructions, was to complete the works by 30 June 1998 (TB 015.6, pdf 1). Mr Carey’s evidence at the hearing was that most of the work was carried out by this time, but that there was still some work carried out in the following two to three years due to a change to the subdivision. Mr Carey later said in his evidence at the hearing that some of the work carried out under this 1997 Building Contract was subcontracted, and that Westpoint Constructions paid the subcontractors. However, it was never clearly explained why Westpoint Constructions would not have sought to recoup that expenditure from Silkchime until up to eight years later in 2005 (see transcript/203). The documentary evidence suggests to us that all the works under the 1997 Building Contract were completed by approximately mid-2001. It is therefore unlikely that any work carried out under the 1997 Building Contract would be invoiced as late as November and December 2005. Mr Carey’s evidence that the invoices raised in November and December 2005 included work carried out prior to June 1998 (transcript/202) and that the Construction Works Summaries – Warwick included the recovery of costs incurred by Westpoint Constructions under the 1997 Building Contract (A3, para [56]) is a convenient explanation if true, but it is impossible to square with the documentary evidence.
  2. We also agree with the Commissioner’s submission that the Warwick D & C Contract does not appear to impose any liability for work undertaken prior to the contract being entered into. In particular, we note there is no reference to an obligation to pay for work carried out under the 1997 Building Contract. However, despite the express terms of the Warwick D & C Contract, our attention was drawn to another document titled ‘Minutes of Meeting’ of the Warwick Commercial Park Project Control Group. The meeting was apparently attended by Mr Carey as General Manager and Construction Manager for Westpoint Constructions. The minute is dated 15 December 2005 (TB 041.11). It purported to outline the status of the project as at that date and referred to the 1997 works being incorporated into the Warwick D & C Contract. The relevant part of the minute stated (TB 041.11, pdf 2):
    2. Design and construct

    2.1. Status as at 15th December 2005

    2.1.1 Design and contract dated 07/08/2005 signed by Silkchime Pty Ltd and provided to Westpoint Constructions. Subdivision work set out in agreement dated 17/09/1997 is incorporated in D&C contract. Compliance checklist to be finalised.

    ...

  3. Despite this statement, as we have said above, the express terms of the actual Warwick D & C Contract do not incorporate the 1997 Building Contract. Where the parties have clearly recorded the terms of their agreement in a written contract that appears to be entire, that contract is usually taken to be the complete agreement of the parties. It is not clear why a minute stating that the 1997 Building Contract is incorporated into the Warwick D & C Contract is enough to add to or alter the terms of the Warwick D & C Contract which otherwise appears complete on its face.
  4. The more plausible scenario is that the 1997 Building Contract work was completed by sometime in mid-2001, and that even if some works occurred after that time, they were likely completed in tax periods before the tax invoices were issued in November and December 2005. By that time there was a new agreement in place, namely the Warwick D & C Contract entered into on 7 August 2005 – and the evidence does not suggest that agreement was retrospective. We do not think the 1997 Building Contract supports a finding that the taxable supplies recorded in the November and December 2005 invoices were made to Silkchime by Westpoint Constructions.
  5. It is in any event unclear what work was carried out under the Warwick D & C Contract by Westpoint Constructions for Silkchime. We note there was very little time for any work to be undertaken under that agreement. It was entered into on 7 August 2005 and, as we have said above, receivers were first appointed to the Silkchime Company on 24 January 2006. Further, we note the Warwick D & C Contract was for the lump sum of $11,325,500. If the contract (which we have found was not retrospective) was only operational for five months and only included “preliminaries” with no visible building works (transcript/201) (discussed further below), it is difficult to appreciate how taxable supplies were made ($2,601,500 and $2,413,400, being a total of $5,014,900) which amounted to approximately 44 percent of the contract price.
  6. Our misgivings about the work being carried out are increased when we have regard to the history of the developments. For example, the Warwick D & C Contract includes the design and construction of a 700 square metre amusement centre on lot 944. However, a record of the building applications for lot 944 indicates the building had been built prior to 1999 (TB 103), as did a Landgate photograph as of 12 December 2004 which shows the amusement centre had been constructed by that time (TB 012.55, pdf 1). No further construction work is discernible from another aerial photograph as of 26 February 2016 (TB 012.55, pdf 3). Mr Carey agreed to this under cross-examination (transcript/193-194), but he claimed the structure was “dilapidated” and that the work undertaken under the Warwick D & C Contract was “to substantially remodel and renovate the existing building and to do a quite substantial fit out for the new amusement centre” (transcript/195). This evidence is perplexing. If the building was completed in the late 1990s, it would be unusual if it fell into such a state of disrepair that it required work involving such a large amount of expenditure. In addition, there is no evidence of any remodelling works, nor documentation that evidences Westpoint Constructions undertaking any such works.
  7. A letter from Silkchime’s town planners dated 18 May 2004 (TB 012.51, pdf 2-3) says the development took place in stages, and the timing of the construction was dependent on interest from suitable tenants or purchasers. It explains lots 946, 956, 961 and 965 had still not been constructed and the development approval was due to expire on 21 May 2004. They applied for a new development approval, but we have no evidence it was granted. Mr Carey said there was no need to renew the development approval because there was “substantial commencement” of the development with six of the buildings already having been built (transcript/198-199), but that is inconsistent with a further development application being made. It is difficult for us to determine what, if any, work was undertaken on these lots after 18 May 2004 and the absence of a development approval, in our view, adds to this uncertainty.
  8. Mr Carey was shown satellite images of lots 946, 956, 961 and 965. He conceded no buildings were constructed on the lots but insisted there had been earthworks and siteworks on the lots (transcript/196-197). However, he also conceded those earthworks and siteworks were done prior to 12 December 2004 when the subdivision was being done (transcript/197). In addition, Mr Carey also conceded the work included in the Construction Works Summaries – Warwick included work undertaken before the Warwick D & C Contract was entered into and work undertaken under the 1997 Building Contract (transcript/201). This adds further doubt as to what work, if any, was carried out under the Warwick D & C Contract.
  9. In summary, the documentation raises more questions than it answers – and the gaps are not adequately filled by the explanations proffered by Mr Carey. It is unclear as to what work (if any) was carried out under the Warwick D & C Contract. We find it is more likely that any work in the form of “preliminaries” (transcript/201) occurred under the 1997 Building Contract before the introduction of GST. Even if it occurred in the early 2000s, it is unclear why tax invoices issued approximately five years later would cover this work. Additionally, as we have explained, the Warwick D & C Contract was not retrospective, and it was only in operation for approximately five months before external managers were first appointed and any work ceased.

What did Westpoint Realty supply to Silkchime?

  1. It is also unclear as to what, if anything, Westpoint Realty supplied to Silkchime. We first turn to the example of sales commission purportedly charged under the Agency Agreement.
  2. In the project cost summary for the Warwick Commercial Park dated 30 November 2005 there is a selling commission for the professional centre for $250,000 and legal costs of $7,256 (TB 012.15, pdf 28). Similarly, in the project cost summary dated 20 December 2005 (incorrectly stated as 2015) there is another selling commission for the professional centre for $250,000 and legal costs of $2,345 (TB 012.15, pdf 29). These commissions and fees were purportedly charged under the Agency Agreement with respect to a contract of sale of lot 961 dated 18 November 2005 between Silkchime and Rold Corporation Pty Ltd (ACN 009 358 276). Rold Corporation was controlled by one of Mr Carey’s brothers (transcript/208). The contract provided for a purchase price of $6,600,000 with a deposit of $660,000. Clause 6(c) provided, in summary, that the contract was conditional upon the written approval from all relevant authorities (TB 012.17, pdf 176). Mr Carey conceded that not all approvals were obtained (transcript/208). The Contract provided the deposit “shall be paid within 14 days of acceptance to be held by See Annexure A”. Clause 15(a) in Annexure A provided:
    The deposit of $660,000 forms part of the Purchasers [sic] $1.5million investment in mezzanine finance currently held by Renaissance Mezzanine Pty Ltd (ACN 110 978 491). Within fourteen (14) days of notice of settlement, this $660,000 is to be provided for settlement.
  3. Mr Carey explained his brother had made a cash investment of $1.5 million in Renaissance Mezzanine Pty Ltd and received $1.5 million in promissory notes which were redeemable back to cash so his brother could redeem the full amount, or just the amount of the deposit when it was required for settlement (transcript/209). However, Mr Carey agreed the payment of the deposit by that method was never implemented and there was never a notice of settlement (transcript/209).
  4. We agree with the Commissioner’s submission that the preconditions to Westpoint Realty being entitled to remuneration under clauses 5.1 and 5.2 were not satisfied. Those clauses stated commission was payable on the “sale of a property” and 100% of the commission was payable when a deposit has been paid by the purchaser or a “Deposit Power Bond has been received by the Principal or the Agent on account of the deposit paid by the Purchaser” (TB 12.8, pdf 64). A “Deposit Power Bond” is not defined. It is unclear whether it would include a promissory note.
  5. Silkchime submitted the payment of commission by Silkchime could be categorised as voluntary (Applicant’s closing submissions in reply, para [75]). They say this is contemplated by s 9-15(2) of the GST Act which provides “It does not matter whether the payment, act or forbearance was voluntary, or whether it was by the recipient of the supply”. However, the problem for Silkchime is that s 9-15(1) of the GST Act contemplates that “the payment, act of forbearance” in question must be “in connection with or be a response to or an inducement for a supply”. According to clauses 5.1 and 5.2 of the Agency Agreement, nothing was supplied or could possibly be supplied because the preconditions in respect of the services that formed part of the tax invoices were not satisfied.
  6. We next turn to leasing and other fees charged under the Management Agreement. Again, two separate commissions for each property are included in the project cost summary for agreements to lease for each of the following lots (TB 012.15, pdf 28-29):
    (a) $215,000 for the professional centre (lot 961) on 30 November 2005 and $145,000 for the same on 20 December 2005 (totalling $360,000);

    (b) $110,000 for the amusement centre (lot 944) on 30 November 2005 and $75,000 for the same on 20 December 2005 (totalling $185,000);

    (c) $105,000 for the commercial units (lot 965) on 30 November 2005 and $85,000 for the same on 20 December 2005 (totalling $190,000); and

    (d) $70,000 for a video store (lot 946) on 30 November 2005 and another $70,000 for the same on 20 December 2005 (totalling $140,000).

  7. These amounts are less than those Silkchime was legally entitled to receive according to calculations supplied to the Commissioner by Silkchime’s tax advisors (see Table at TB 012.17, pdf 3) which were:
Lot no

  1. Mr Carey explained in his witness statement dated 26 April 2016 (A1, para [179]) that the amounts of lease commission in the project cost summaries “represented partial payment of the total commission”, and that “the balance was to be invoiced and recovered progressively over subsequent months”. However, he said this was not possible due to the appointment of the receivers (see A1, para [179] (b)(vii), (ix), (xi)).
  2. There is one amount of legal fees stated for the combined properties of $12,199 in both the 30 November 2005 project summary and of $11,100 in the 20 December 2005 project summary. There is no evidence that explains how these amounts were calculated.
  3. We were told agreements for lease were entered into between Silkchime as lessor and the following parties as lessee:
    (a) Tarana Holdings Pty Ltd (ACN 075 364 617) on 3 November 2005 regarding lot 944 (TB 012.17, pdf 48);

    (b) Arcane Pty Ltd (ACN 009 324 076) on 8 November 2005 regarding lot 961 (TB 012.17, pdf 6);

    (c) Kingrose Pty Ltd (ACN 075 697 095) on 17 November 2005 regarding lot 965 (TB 012.17, pdf 90); and

    (d) Indietro Pty Ltd (ACN 009 460 897) on 23 November 2005 for lot 946 (TB 012.17, pdf 132).

  4. We note a fixed and floating charge was entered into by Silkchime and St George Bank Limited on 3 September 1999 over all property held by Silkchime (TB 012.74). The charge document contained a prohibition on dealing with the mortgaged property, including granting any interest in the property (clause 8.1(a)(i), pdf 25) although Silkchime was not precluded from selling any mortgaged property over which the charge was floating provided the sale occurred in the ordinary course of business (clause 2.5(a), pdf 14). It follows Silkchime was in breach of clause 8.1(a)(i) by entering into the agreements to lease without obtaining the mortgagee’s consent. It may be that the mortgagee would be entitled to ignore any interest under the agreements for lease to which it had not consented.
  5. We also note clause 3 of each agreement for lease provides “The Lessor shall prior to the date of Practical Completion, do all things necessary to obtain the consent of any mortgagee of the Premises to the arrangements set out in this Agreement and the grant of a lease to the Lessee pursuant to clause 6” (TB 012.17, pdf 14, 98 and 140). However, it is not expressed to be a precondition to the agreements to lease or to otherwise affect their validity. The agreements for lease, in clause 5, provide for the lessee to rescind the agreement if the lessor’s works are not completed (012.17, pdf 15, 99 and 141). It is helpful to consider this clause in full:
    5.1 Subject to the obligations of the parties to each other, provided for under this Agreement, if the Lessor’s Works have not reached the stage of Practical Completion on or before twenty four (24) months from the date of this Agreement then the Lessee may, by not less than one (1) month’s notice in writing to the Lessor (and if the Lessor’s Works are not completed by exploration of the one (1) month’s notice), terminate this Agreement subject to the rights of either party against the other for any antecedent breaches or obligations contained herein.

    5.2 In the event that the Lessee rescinds this Agreement by reason of the events set out in Clause 7.1 then the Lessor shall reimburse to the Lessee all reasonable costs and expenses incurred by the Lessee in relation to its proposed use of the premises including its reasonable legal costs and expenses and such other reasonable costs and expenses which have been paid by the Lessee in connection with this Agreement.

  6. The constructions works were not carried out and no leases were ever entered into (transcript/210-211; see references in Respondent’s closing submissions, page 78, footnote 318).
  7. Clause 7.1 of the agreements to lease provided for a right to terminate if a receiver and/or manager, liquidator or administrator was appointed (clause 7.2(e), TB 012.17, pdf 16, 100 and 142). Again, it is helpful to review the relevant parts of that clause:
    7.1 If:

    ...

(e) a receiver, a manager or a receiver and manager or an administrator is appointed in respect of any of the business affairs or undertaking of the Lessor;
then in any one or more of such events the Lessee may determine in this Agreement by notice in writing to that effect given to the Lessor whereupon this Agreement will determine but without prejudice to any action or other right or remedy that the Lessee has or may or otherwise can have for any such breach or default or the loss or damage suffered by the Lessee as a result of any such breach or default or any other of such events and upon giving such notice the Lessee will be freed and discharged from any action suit to claim or demand by or obligation to the Lessor under or by virtue of this Agreement.
  1. The Commissioner says the agreements for lease were not agreements to which clause 4.2 of the Management Agreement authorised payment because, relying on clause 5 and clause 7.1 of the agreements to lease, there was compelling evidence that the agreements were rescinded (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [255]). We are not persuaded by this submission. The rescission contemplated by clause 5.1 and 5.2, on our reading, is not rescission ab initio. This is demonstrated by clause 5.1, which maintains the right of the parties to sue each other for antecedent breaches. Similarly, clause 7.1 provides that the Lessee may give written notice to determine the agreement if, for example, receivers and managers are appointed with respect to the lessor, but the lessee’s rights to claim for loss or damage from a breach or default is retained. The effect is not to restore the parties to the position as if the agreements for lease were never entered into. Further, as we found above for Earlmist, clause 4.2.1(i)(ii) of the Management Agreement provides Westpoint Realty was entitled to a leasing fee of 12% of the first term gross income if an agreement for lease was signed, which they were for lots 961, 944, 965 and 946. Under the terms of the Agency Agreement, payment of the commission was only dependent on an agreement for lease being entered into even if the lease itself was never concluded. However, this finding does not inevitably mean Silkchime is entitled to input tax credits for the GST payable on these commissions, particularly as there are so many doubts that arise from the evidence as a whole.
  2. Our conclusions regarding marketing, legal and administration fees purportedly charged under the Agency and the Management Agreement are substantially the same as for Earlmist. Again, to the extent those costs are listed in the project cost summaries, it is unclear to us as to what exactly was supplied that comprised those sums. Again, there is insufficient documentation to support or substantiate how those specific sums were arrived at.
  3. Like Earlmist, other undated marketing documentation relied upon by Silkchime does little to substantiate the stated marketing costs. These documents included:
    (a) ‘Warwick Professional Centre Marketing Strategy and Leasing Plan’, undated (TB 015.6, pdf 77);

    (b) A three page ‘Westpoint Realty Warwick Commercial Park’ brochure (TB 015.6, pdf 90-92); and

    (c) Documents titled, ‘Warwick Professional Centre Leasing Opportunity’ for lots 961, 944, 965 and 946 (TB 015.6, pdf 93-104; 107-118; 121-132; 135-146).

  4. As we have stated for Earlmist above, these marketing documents for Silkchime are too general in nature to substantiate the specific amounts claimed for marketing costs. We note Mr Boon Leng Low (TB 016) refers to a ‘Warwick Professional Centre Leasing Opportunity’ document for lot 961 (TB 016/016.8, pdf 2), but he does not explain the details of the marketing work undertaken.
  5. We accept there is some evidence in the form of signed agreements to lease which suggests Westpoint Realty may have supplied services to Silkchime under the Agency Agreement but the evidence, on the whole, raises more questions than it answers. We will return to this issue below.

Issues regarding the payment of the tax invoices via set-off

  1. Like Earlmist, Silkchime alleges the tax invoices were paid by way of a set-off of book entries. Also, like Earlmist, Silkchime relies on unstamped loan agreements between each of the entities, drawdown notices, journal entries and trial balances.
  2. We refer to the evidence of Mr Carey that we outlined above as to how Westpoint Corporation operated as a central treasury, with the loans being effected by drawdown notices and the transactions being recorded in the accounts of the entities.
  3. The loan agreements appear to use the same template as those for Earlmist, with the borrower being required to give the lender a drawdown notice to borrow the funds.
  4. The specific loan agreements and corresponding drawdown notices were:
    (a) A loan agreement dated 7 February 2002 between Silkchime as borrower and Westpoint Corporation as lender for a maximum commitment of $18,000,000 (TB 013.23).

Mr Carey’s evidence was that in September 2005, Silkchime drew down the amount of $11,487,044.51 as a loan from Westpoint Corporation pursuant to the loan agreement (A1, para [185](a)). This is shown as a credit against the trial balance for “Loan – Westpoint Corporation P/L” as at 30 September 2005 (TB 012.11, pdf 45).

(b) A loan agreement dated 26 May 2005 between Westpoint Realty as borrower and Silkchime as lender for a maximum commitment of $6,000,000 (TB 012.11, pdf 19-30).

On 30 September 2005 Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Westpoint Realty wished to draw $3,241,436.25 on 30 September 2005 (TB 012.13, pdf 10).
(c) A loan agreement dated 3 June 2005 between Westpoint Constructions as borrower and Silkchime as lender for a maximum commitment of $12,000,000 (TB 012.11 pdf 7).

On 30 September 2005 Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Westpoint Constructions wished to draw $8,100,852.88 on 30 September 2005 (TB 012.13, pdf 9).

  1. Consistent with the approach he adopted in relation to Earlmist, the Commissioner submitted the evidence does not establish loan funds were provided pursuant to the drawdown notices (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [261]).
  2. As was the case in relation to Earlmist, Westpoint Corporation’s bank account that was used for its central treasury function does not show a corresponding drawdown. The closing account balance as at 30 September 2005 was $5,128,441.95 falling short of the $11,487,044.51 drawdown amount (TB 012.65, pdf 96). As we concluded for Earlmist, the existence of bank deposits would be beneficial for Silkchime in meeting the burden under s 14ZZK of the TAA, but the absence of such evidence is not necessarily fatal. The evidence must be viewed as a whole. Therein lies the problem, because – as with Earlmist - there are further issues with the remaining documentation that Silkchime relies upon to substantiate the set-off which causes us to doubt whether it occurred.
  3. The journal entries relied upon by Silkchime are like those for Earlmist. They are handwritten on a “Journal Entry Form” template. Each describes the “Journal Details” as “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices”. Each describes the “Entity” as “(29) Silkchime”. The forms are incomplete. As with Earlmist, several important boxes have been left blank on each form for the “journal number”, “date prepared”, “prepared by”, “entered by”, “authorised by” and “date entered”. They are:
    (a) A journal entry form with an “effective journal date” of 30 November 2005 (TB 012.8, pdf 78). The amounts in the two tax invoices dated 30 November 2005 are set-off against the respective loan accounts of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty.

    (b) A journal entry form with an “effective journal date” of 20 December 2005 (TB 012.8, pdf 79). The amounts in the two 20 December 2005 tax invoices are set-off against the respective loan accounts of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty.

  4. Silkchime also relies on a ‘GL [general ledger] account year to date transaction report’ of Silkchime for ‘Loan – Westgem Unit Trust’ showing:
    (a) A “Loan drawdown” on 30 September 2005 of $3,241,436.25 by the Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem Unit Trust (TB 012.11, pdf 38) which Mr Carey claimed was pursuant to the loan agreement dated 26 May 2005 to carry out work under the Management Agreement and the Agency Agreement (A1/[185](c)).

    (b) The entry “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices” on 30 November 2005 for tax invoice 071-01 of 30 November 2005 for $840,400 being set-off against the loan account for the Westgem Unit Trust (TB 012.11, pdf 40).

    (c) Another entry, “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices” on 31 December 2005 for tax invoice 071-02 for $702,350 being set-off against the loan account for the Westgem Unit Trust (TB 012.11, pdf 41).

  5. Silkchime also relies on a ‘GL [general ledger] account year to date transaction report’ of Silkchime for ‘Loan – Westpoint Constructions’ showing:
    (a) A “Loan drawdown” on 30 September 2005 of $8,180,852.88 by Westpoint Constructions (TB 012.11, pdf 32) which Mr Carey attested was pursuant to the Loan Agreement dated 3 June 2005 to carry out design and construction work pursuant to the Warwick D & C Contract (A1/[185](b)).

    (b) The entry, “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices” on 30 November 2005 for tax invoice 0629-01 of 30 November 2005 for $2,601,500 being set-off against the loan account for Westpoint Constructions (TB 012.11, pdf 34).

    (c) Another entry, “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices” on 20 December 2005 for tax invoice 0629-02 for $2,413,400 being set-off against the loan account for Westpoint Constructions (TB 012.11, pdf 35).

  6. Silkchime also relies on a trial balance as at 30 September 2005, which in addition to showing the $11,487,044.51 loan to Silkchime by Westpoint Corporation that we referred to above (TB 012.11, pdf 45), also records:
    (a) A debit of $3,241,436.25 to “Loan – Westgem Unit Trust”, being the loan by Silkchime to Westpoint Realty (TB 012.11, pdf 45).

    (b) A debit of $8,180,852.88 to “Loan – Westpoint Constructions”, being the loan by Silkchime to Westpoint Constructions (TB 012.11, pdf 45).

  7. A trial balance as at 30 November 2005 also shows (TB 012.11, pdf 49):
    (a) A debit of $3,129,000 for “WIP Silkchime”, which was described in Silkchime’s opening submissions as the GST exclusive amounts payable by Silkchime to Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty in respect of the tax invoices dated 30 November 2005 (para [34(b)(i)] of Annexure 5 Silkchime).

    (b) A debit of $312,900 for “GST Paid”, which Silkchime describes in its opening submissions as the GST component of the tax invoices dated 30 November 2005 (para [34(b)(ii)] of Annexure 5 Silkchime).
