    (c) A credit of $2,601,500 for “Loan – Westpoint Constructions” being a reduction in the loan to pay the invoices issued by Westpoint Constructions dated 30 November 2005.

    (d) A credit of $840,400 for “Loan – Westgem Unit Trust” being a reduction in the loan to pay the invoices issued by Westpoint Realty dated 30 November 2005.

  8. The trial balance of Silkchime as at 31 December 2005, contains the following entries (TB 012.11, pdf 51):
    (a) A debit of $2,832,500 for “WIP Silkchime”, which was described in Silkchime’s opening submissions as the GST exclusive amounts payable by Silkchime to Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty in respect of the tax invoices dated 20 December 2005 (para [34(c)(i)] of Annexure 5 Silkchime).

    (b) A debit of $283,250 for “GST Paid”, which Silkchime describes in its opening submissions as the GST component of the tax invoices dated 20 December 2005 (para [34(c)(ii)] of Annexure 5 Silkchime).

    (c) A credit of $2,413,400 for “Loan – Westpoint Constructions” being a reduction in the loan to pay the invoices issued by Westpoint Constructions dated 20 December 2005.

    (d) A credit of $702,350 for “Loan – Westgem Unit Trust” being a reduction in the loan to pay the invoices issued by Westpoint Realty dated 20 December 2005.

  9. However, as was the case with Earlmist, no records could be extracted by an independent information technology expert from the Triumph database produced by ASIC to corroborate these accounting entries. When the specific account numbers were searched, they did not show the loan drawdowns and the amounts payable under the 30 November 2005 or the 20 December 2005 tax invoices (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [267]; TB 012.61, pdf 3-8).
  10. As with Earlmist, there are no corresponding accounts for Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty showing the transactions. Again, there is no evidence that the companies accepted the mutual set-off liabilities relied upon by Silkchime. As we explained for Earlmist, GSTD 2004/4 suggests there should be an express or implied agreement between the entities that set-off can be used. There is no evidence of this in Silkchime’s case, nor any evidence of whether the companies end-of-year financial year statements reflect that an obligation to pay has been replaced with a set-off arrangement.
  11. The timing of the invoices in November and December 2005 is also questionable. The Westpoint group was on the verge of collapse. As we mentioned in relation to Earlmist, there was not enough money in the Westpoint Corporation’s central treasury account for the loan drawdown and receivers were appointed to the Silkchime Company on 24 January 2006. Transactions occurring at the 11th hour in this context inevitably invite questions.
  12. Also, like Earlmist, the reports as to affairs made by the liquidators are inconsistent with these inter-company debts and mutual set-offs alleged by Silkchime. A report as to affairs up to 18 May 2006 by receiver and manager Mr Oren Zohar specified “sundry debtors as detailed in schedule B” being “related party loans” as $4,762,838.13 (TB 012.41, pdf 5 and 7). This amount is significantly less than the amounts that would have been owing under the loan agreements after the set-offs.
  13. Other reports prepared during the administration and liquidation of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty do not support that a set-off occurred, or that the amounts claimed were owed to Silkchime. For example, the report by the administrators of Westpoint Realty dated 2 February 2007 stated the amount owed to Silkchime in a related party loan was $37,859 (TB 006.47, pdf 17).
  14. A report as to affairs as at 22 February 2006 for Westpoint Constructions, verified by Mr Rundle on 9 August 2006, does not list Silkchime as an unsecured creditor, nor the Warwick Commercial Centre as a work in progress (TB 012.42, pdf 9 and 11).
  15. A report as to affairs by official liquidator Mr Simon Andrew Read submitted to ASIC on 27 February 2006 stated Westpoint Corporation owed Silkchime $403,432.08 (TB 031.A1, pdf 60). However, the 31 December 2005 trial balance said Westpoint Corporation owed Silkchime $7,923,745.40 (TB 012.11, pdf 51).
  16. As for Earlmist, Mr Carey claimed the authors of these reports were lacking up-to-date financial information. We make the same comments as for Earlmist, including that there is no mention in any of the reports as to affairs that the receivers and managers or any liquidators were lacking up-to-date information, and that they were not called as witnesses to identify the information before them or be questioned about any gaps.

Other evidence that may assist

  1. As for Earlmist, the GST reporting by Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty is also inconsistent with those entities having made taxable supplies to Silkchime.
  2. Westpoint Constructions lodged the following BASs for the following relevant periods. They each have a net amount of nil and do not report any taxable supplies:
    (a) BAS for the monthly period 1 November 2005 to 30 November 2005, lodged on 2 March 2006 (TB 012.31); and

    (b) BAS for an overlapping quarterly period, 1 October 2005 to 31 December 2005, also lodged on 2 March 2006 (TB 012.32).

  3. Westpoint Realty lodged a BAS for the period 1 October 2005 to 31 December 2005 on 27 February 2006 which reported total sales of $302,711 and non-capital purchases of $785,046 (TB 012.34).
  4. We agree with the Commissioner’s submission that these BASs do not show taxable supplies which correspond with the acquisitions Silkchime claims to have made.
  5. The Commissioner points out Westpoint Realty’s BAS, lodged on 27 February 2006, occurred before the receiver and manager was appointed to Westpoint Realty on 20 April 2006 (TB 012.38, pdf 1). If we accept Mr Carey’s evidence that the books of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty were seized by ASIC in January 2006, there is still a lack of any corroborating evidence from the perspective of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty that they made taxable supplies to Silkchime.

Conclusion for Silkchime

  1. We are not satisfied Silkchime has established on the evidence that it made creditable acquisitions from Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty. In summary, those reasons are similar to those we gave for Earlmist and include:
    (a) It is unclear what work was carried out by Silkchime under the Warwick D & C Contract, given the short time the Warwick D & C Contract (which was not retrospective) was operative, and there is some doubt about whether any preliminary work that may have been done prior to that agreement was in fact completed under the earlier 1997 Building Contract.

    (b) We have found that entry into an Agreement for Lease entitled Westpoint Realty to the payment of commission under the Agency Agreement. However, it is otherwise unclear exactly what was supplied by Westpoint Realty to Silkchime for the amounts of marketing, legal and administration services stated in the project cost summaries.

    (c) Like Earlmist, the journal entry forms which are key documents relied upon by Silkchime to evidence the set-off of the tax invoices are incomplete, making it difficult to know what we should make of them.

    (d) Again, the evidence does not suggest any implied or express binding agreement between Silkchime and Westpoint Constructions and Silkchime and Westpoint Realty to the effect that the entities would dispense with the obligation to pay for the tax invoices by mutual book entries.

    (e) Also, like Earlmist, search of the accounts of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty do not show the corresponding loan drawdowns and the amounts payable under the tax invoices as reflected in the documentation Silkchime relies upon. Likewise, there is an absence of mutual book entries for the associated entities showing the set-off.

    (f) Like Earlmist, the reports of affairs are inconsistent with the existence of inter-company debts and there is no indication in those reports that the writers, who were not called to give evidence about the information they relied on, lacked up-to-date financial information.

    (g) The failure to contemporaneously stamp the Warwick D & C Contract, Agency Agreement and Management Agreement raises concerns about their integrity and the persons who seek to rely upon them, in this instance, Mr Carey for similar reasons we discussed in relation to Earlmist – namely, the failure to comply with stamping obligations reflects a lack of commitment to complying with legal requirements and obligations with respect to record-keeping. The free-wheeling approach to business that Mr Carey described in which documents of uncertain pedigree sometimes meant what they said - but did not in other circumstances - only serves to obscure the reality of whatever was going on.

  2. Although we have observed the terms of the Agency Agreement entitled Westpoint Realty to the payment of commission when agreements for lease were entered into, that is not enough for us to be satisfied Silkchime made the creditable acquisitions in the amounts they claimed, given the numerous doubts that are raised on the evidence that we have summarised above, including there being no evidence of inter-company agreements and financial statements in support that a mutual set-off was agreed and inconsistent GST reporting from counterparties.
  3. There are, in our view, so many doubts raised by the evidence before us that we find Silkchime has not discharged the burden of proving that its net amount in the quarterly tax period ending 31 December 2005 is less than zero. Silkchime has not established it made creditable acquisitions under s 11-5 of the GST Act from Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty which entitled Silkchime to input tax credits of GST of $596,150, in whole or in part, in the 30 November 2005 and 20 December 2005 tax invoices. In other words, Silkchime has not discharged the burden of proving that the assessment was excessive or otherwise incorrect and what it should have been.
  4. We therefore find the Silkchime Reviewable Decision should be affirmed.

CINEMA CITY

The entity

  1. The ‘Cinema City Development Trust’ was established by a Deed signed on 31 October 2002 between Cinema City Development Pty Ltd (ACN 091 824 298) (‘CCD Company’) as Trustee and a company called Quartz Nominees Pty Ltd (ACN 008 859 103) (‘Quartz Company’) who is described as The Original Unit Holders (TB 031.25). The Quartz Company held all units in the CCD Trust (TB 031.25, pdf 48-49). We will refer to this trust as the ‘CCD Trust’. The Trust Deed was stamped on 13 June 2003 (TB 031.25, pdf 5).
  2. Mr Carey was the sole Director of the CCD Company from 1 March 2000 until it was deregistered on 24 July 2011. Mr Rundle was the Company Secretary from 1 March 2000 to 27 January 2006 (TB 031.30, pdf 3). Mr Carey was also a director and company secretary of the Quartz Company when the CCD Trust was established. His brother, Allan Carey, was also a director. Mr Grant Dodd also became a Company Secretary from 28 October 2003 (TB 031.A6, pdf 3-4). Mr Rundle was Company Secretary from 30 June 1997 to 27 January 2006 (TB 031.A6, pdf 3-5). (We note the Quartz Company changed its name to ACN 008 859 103 Pty Ltd on 3 May 2020 (TB 031.A6, pdf 1)).
  3. Mr Carey (as Director) and Mr Rundle (as Company Secretary) executed the CCD Trust Deed on behalf of both entities.
  4. This application concerns the December 2005 and January 2006 tax periods. At those times, the CCD Company was the Trustee of the CCD Trust.
  5. The CCD Company is not a party to these proceedings because it was deregistered in 2011. The Applicant in this case is actually Heca Nominees. Heca Nominees came to be involved because it claims it was appointed an additional trustee of the Cinema City Development Trust. To that end, the Applicant provided a ‘Deed of Appointment of Additional Trustee: Cinema City Development Trust’ (‘Additional Trustee Deed’) dated 10 October 2006 (TB 022.20) which purports to appoint Heca Nominees as an additional trustee to the CCD Trust from the date of execution (that is, 10 October 2006). The Additional Trustee Deed is not stamped. If it was stamped, that would have provided some confirmation as to when it was created, which was an issue because the document was only produced during these proceedings. The parties to the Additional Trust Deed are the Quartz Company as the Unit Holder and Heca Nominees as the additional trustee.
  6. The Commissioner argued the late-breaking evidence of Heca Nominee’s appointment as an additional trustee should not be accepted. To begin with, the Commissioner says it was difficult to square the evidence of an appointment with what occurred – and what did not occur – under the terms of the CCD Trust. For example: clause 19.6(b) of the CCD Trust states that if a new trustee is appointed, the “continuing Trustee shall take such action as is necessary to cause title to any assets of the Trust Fund to be amended to include any new or additional trustee” (TB 031.25, pdf 38). The Commissioner pointed out no step was ever taken to vest the trust property in Heca Nominees as a Trustee, and indeed Heca Nominees never held any assets on trust pursuant to the CCD Trust (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [295]). Mr Carey confirmed that was so during his evidence at the hearing. He said he did not recall taking any action to transfer the assets of the trust into the joint names of CCD Company and Heca Nominees (transcript/272). He was also not aware of the existence of an additional trustee being brought to the attention of French J in the Federal Court proceedings that wound up the CCD Trust nor was he aware whether the receivers and managers had ever been informed an additional trustee had been appointed (transcript/273). One would expect news of an appointment would have been communicated to the Court and the external managers, and the fact it was not calls into question whether the appointment occurred as alleged.
  7. The Applicant sought to buttress its case by producing a document titled ‘Deed of Ratification for the Cinema City Development Trust’ dated 31 August 2021 between the Quartz Company (notwithstanding that the company had already changed its name to ACN 008 859 103 Pty Ltd) as Unit Holder, and Heca Nominees as the additional trustee (‘Ratification Deed’) (TB 041.19). The Ratification Deed sought to confirm and ratify the appointment of Heca Nominees as Trustee of the CCD Trust. It also sought to confirm and ratify all acts or omissions of Heca Nominees done in its capacity as a Trustee of the CCD Trust since the date of its appointment as additional trustee (that is, on 10 October 2006).
  8. The Ratification Deed was not stamped. This failure to contemporaneously stamp the Ratification Deed, together with this document coming into existence some 15 years after the Additional Trustee Deed and after these proceedings had been commenced raises questions about the probative value of this document and the transaction it purports to record.
  9. Heca Nominees argues the Additional Trustee Deed and the Ratification Deed entitle it to be treated as the Trustee for the Cinema City Development Trust. Because Heca Nominees is a party to the other proceedings discussed in these reasons, we will refer to the company in the reasons which follow in these proceedings as ‘Cinema City’ to make clear we are discussing the position of Heca Nominees in this case. Where the relevant documentation refers to “The Trustee for the Cinema City Development Trust”, we will refer to them as ‘The Trustee’.
  10. The case for Cinema City (that is, Heca Nominees in these proceedings) becoming an additional trustee is fraught with difficulty. As at 31 August 2021 when the Ratification Deed was entered into, there was nothing left to ratify. The only apparent act of Cinema City was to claim input tax credits under the relevant invoices. The CCD Company went into liquidation on 6 December 2006 (TB 031.30, pdf 1). Joint and several receivers were also appointed for the purpose of preserving the assets of the CCD Trust and to sell or otherwise realise the assets (Australian Securities & Investments Commission v Eastlands Pty Ltd ACN 009 349 053 (No 3) [2006] FCA 1702, para [17]). The liquidator retired on 12 May 2011, and the CCD Company was deregistered on 24 July 2011 (TB 031.30, pdf 1).
  11. Consequently, by the retirement of the liquidator in May 2011, there was no CCD Trust and its property had been realised and distributed to creditors. We agree with the Commissioner’s submission that there is no persuasive evidence to suggest the assets of the CCD Trust were not all distributed and realised (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [414]). Mr Davies submitted in his opening submissions, and we agree, that any entitlement to a GST refund would form part of the CCD Trust and therefore the claims are not properly made by Cinema City. Rather, it would be a matter for the liquidators to persuade the Tribunal as to why Cinema City should be entitled to a GST refund in circumstances where they did not find such an entitlement (transcript/74-75). This, and the issues we have identified above with respect to the Additional Trustee Deed and the Ratification Deed, is a further reason why we say Cinema City (again, Heca Nominees) has not met its burden under s 14ZZK of the TAA.

The assessment

  1. Cinema City was registered for GST on a non-cash accruals basis (TB 022.1, pdf 8, para [24]) from 31 October 2002 until 6 December 2006 (TB 022.9; 031.27). It lodged its BAS monthly (TB 022.1, pdf 8, para [24]).
  2. Cinema City did not lodge a BAS for the December 2005 and January 2006 tax periods by the due dates of 23 January 2006 and 21 February 2006 respectively (TB 022.3 and 022.4). Neither did it lodge a BAS before Cinema City was de-registered for GST on 6 December 2006.
  3. Mr Carey’s said this was because the entity lacked the records and documentation to do so because those records had been seized by ASIC, and the relevant staff who worked for Cinema City and the other entities in the Westpoint group had their employment terminated when the receiver was appointed (A4, para [73]).
  4. Mr Carey added that, as in Earlmist and Silkchime, he sent a letter dated 3 September 2009 to the Deputy Commissioner of Taxation. The letter was worded the same as the other letters and referred to Cinema City (to be clear, Heca Nominees) being entitled to the “substantial GST refunds” for December 2005 and January 2006 (TB 022.21).
  5. Again, there is a statutory declaration from Ms Gray attesting that she recalled typing and posting the letter (TB 022.22). Again, although Ms Gray was not called as a witness, we accept the letter was sent to the Deputy Commissioner of Taxation, even though they have no record of receiving it. As we noted before, the letter may demonstrate Mr Carey held the view when the letter was sent that substantial refunds were due, but it does not of itself qualify as evidence that substantiates creditable acquisitions were made by Cinema City.
  6. In an application dated 15 August 2014, The Trustee’s tax advisors applied for a private tax ruling. They asked the Commissioner to confirm they were entitled to input tax credits for the December 2005 and January 2006 tax periods, and that their entitlement had not ceased (TB 013.10).
  7. On 26 November 2014, the Commissioner requested further evidence to confirm that creditable acquisitions occurred (TB 031.A5).
  8. On 22 August 2018, the Commissioner declined to rule on the application because the Commissioner concluded The Trustee had been unable to provide the information the Commissioner required to progress the private ruling (TB 022.24).
  9. On 27 November 2018, The Trustee wrote to the Commissioner to request that the Commissioner issue a nil assessment for the December 2005 tax period (TB 022.24).
  10. On 7 March 2019, the Commissioner declined to issue the nil assessment and stated that the appropriate way to generate review rights under Part IVC of the TAA would be to lodge a BAS and request the Commissioner make an assessment (TB 022.24).
  11. On 13 December 2019, nil BASs were lodged for Cinema City for the December 2005 and January 2006 tax periods (TB 022.3, pdf 3; 022.4, pdf 3).
  12. On 18 December 2019, the Commissioner issued a notice of assessment to the Trustee for the December 2005 and January 2006 tax periods, with the assessed net amount being nil for both periods (TB 022.25).

The Reviewable Decision

  1. The Trustee’s tax advisors made an objection dated 31 January 2020 (TB 022.6) to the 18 December 2019 notice of assessment. The grounds for the objection were that The Trustee was entitled to input tax credits of $1,109,095 for the December 2005 tax period, $904,971 for the January 2006 tax period, and that The Trustee’s entitlements to refunds or credits had not ceased (TB 022.7).
  2. The Commissioner disallowed the objection in full on 22 May 2020 (TB 022.26; 022.2). We will refer to this as the ‘Cinema City Reviewable Decision’.
  3. On 27 May 2020, The Trustee sought review of the Cinema City Reviewable Decision in this Tribunal (TB 022.1).

The tax invoices

  1. In a letter dated 30 January 2020 which accompanied the objection of 31 January 2020, The Trustee’s tax advisors described the Cinema City project (TB 022.7, pdf 2) as follows:
    The taxpayer held the rights to develop and then acquire a site in the Perth CBD located at the corner of Hay and Barrack Street, Perth. This property contained the Cinema City complex. The Taxpayer had the right to re-develop the building to include a 19 storey apartment complex comprising of 175 residential apartments, 190 serviced apartments and two retail units. The apartments were to be pre-sold on a strata-titled basis.
  2. This was also how Mr Carey described the project (A1, para [75]).
  3. In an earlier Sales and Marketing Agreement dated 11 December 2003 between CCD Company as trustee for Cinema City Development Trust and Westpoint Realty, the project is described as “a hotel and residential development” (TB 022.13, pdf 4).
  4. As with Earlmist and Silkchime, the letter dated 30 January 2020 explained The Trustee had engaged Westpoint Constructions to “design and construct the project” and Westpoint Realty was engaged “to carry out the marketing, selling and/or leasing of the project” (TB 022.7, pdf 2).
  5. Cinema City relies upon the following tax invoices issued to “Cinema City Developments Pty Ltd ATF Cinema City Development Trust” for the December 2005 and January 2006 tax periods:
    (a) Invoice Number 085 – 02 dated 20 December 2005 issued by Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem in the sum of $3,136,045 for “marketing, selling and administration fees” including GST of $285,095 (TB 022.15).

    (b) “Tax invoice – number 0624-03 Cinema City Commercial & Apartments – Hay Street, Perth” dated 20 December 2005 issued by Westpoint Constructions in the sum of $9,064,000 for “value of work completed to date” including GST of $824,000 (TB 022.14).

    (c) Invoice Number 085 – 03 dated 16 January 2006 issued by Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem in the sum of $3,767,500 for “marketing, selling and administration fees” including GST of $342,500 (TB 022.17).

    (d) “Tax invoice – number 0624-04 Cinema City Commercial & Apartments – Hay Street, Perth” dated 14 January 2006 issued by Westpoint Constructions in the sum of $6,187,181 for “value of work completed to date” including GST of $562,471 (TB 022.16).

Documentation relied upon by Cinema City

  1. We will now outline the documentation that Cinema City says should satisfy us that it made creditable acquisitions from Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem and Westpoint Constructions.
  2. All the agreements in question were signed by Mr Carey and Mr Rundle.

Documents said to establish taxable supplies were made by Westpoint Constructions

  1. In the letter dated 30 January 2020 which accompanied the objection, The Trustee’s tax advisors stated they relied upon a “Design & Construct Contract – Lump Sum” made on 1 October 2005 between “Cinema City Developments Pty Ltd ATF Cinema City Development Trust” and Westpoint Constructions (TB 022.12) (‘Cinema City D & C Contract’). This document was not stamped. We note the document also appears to be based on a now-familiar template referred to earlier in these reasons in the Earlmist and Silkchime proceedings.
  2. Under this agreement Westpoint Constructions was engaged to carry out design and construction works. The “brief works description” in Schedule 1 (pdf 33) included 175 residential apartments, 190 one-bedroom serviced apartments, two “retail lots” and 55 car parking bays, at 580 Hay Street Perth (the location of the Cinema City site) for a contract sum of $71,500,000 (TB 022.12, pdf 33, 35). Westpoint Constructions was engaged to carry out and complete the works in accordance with design documents (clause 6.1, pdf 12), however, they are not attached to the Cinema City D & C Contract.
  3. Cinema City also relies upon two documents named, ‘Progress payment – construction works summary: Cinema City Commercial & Apartments Hay Street, Perth’ to support the tax invoices issued by Westpoint Constructions:
    (a) For works up to 20 November 2005 with “this payment value” being a total of $9,064,000, marked progress claim number 1 (TB 030.21); and

    (b) For works up to 14 January 2006 with “this payment value” being a total of $6,187,181, marked progress claim number 2 (TB 030.22).

  4. Cinema City also relies on worksheets that contain a breakdown of works they say are recorded in the project payment summaries (TB 030.24). For works up to 14 January 2006, they are $1,861,924 for design (pdf 1), $4,325,257 for construction (pdf 2) and $674,385 for preliminaries (pdf 3). There is another worksheet for preliminaries up to 20 December 2005 for $1,088,798 (pdf 4). These do not appear to add up to the amounts in the progress payments or tax invoices, but it is unclear anything turns on the discrepancy.

Documents said to establish taxable supplies were made by Westpoint Realty

  1. Two agreements are relied upon by Cinema City. Both agreements were entered into by Westpoint Realty, and not Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem. One was referenced in the 30 January 2020 letter, and the other was referred to by Mr Carey much later.
  2. In the 30 January 2020 letter, The Trustee’s tax advisors submitted The Trustee relied upon an agreement titled ‘Sales & Marketing Agreement “Cinema City” Residential & Hotel Apartment Development’ made on 11 December 2003 between Westpoint Realty and “Cinema City Developments Pty Ltd ATF Cinema City Development Trust” (TB 022.13). We will refer to this agreement as the ‘Agency Agreement’.
  3. Under the Agency Agreement, “Cinema City Developments Pty Ltd ATF Cinema City Development Trust” (incorrectly described in the document as the “Agent” when they should be the “Principal”) appointed Westpoint Realty as a selling and marketing agent (incorrectly described as the “Principal” when they should be the “Agent”) for each unit in the development defined as the “hotel and residential development at Hay Street, Perth, Western Australia” (TB 022.13, pdf 4). The Agent was entitled to a total four percent commission upon the settlement of the sale of each property (clause 5.2, pdf 9) and could invoice the Principal for advertising and marketing fees of $500,000 from time to time (clause 5.2.2, pdf 9, Annexure B, pdf 17).
  4. The Agency Agreement was for an Agency Period of 60 days after the date of the deed, or the date the deed was terminated under clause 7 (which provided for 14 days’ notice of termination to be served on a defaulting party). In a witness statement dated 5 July 2021, (A5, para [132(a)]), Mr Carey referred to a variation which extended the term of the Agency Agreement, even though it had expired. The variation is on Cinema City Development Pty Ltd letterhead, is addressed to Westpoint Realty, is dated 12 May 2005, and signed on behalf of both entities by Mr Carey. It reads (TB 030.30):
    It is hereby agreed that the following variations to the agency agreement dated 11/12/2003.
  5. The term of the agreement be extended by 5 years.
  6. The agent should be entitled to invoice the full commission that will be paid 100% of commissions in relation to the hotel and residential apartments sales.
    (As original.)
  7. Much later, in a witness statement dated 5 July 2021 (A5, para [119]), Mr Carey referred to another agreement between “Cinema City Developments Pty Ltd ATF Cinema City Development Trust” and Westpoint Realty. It was a “Property Management Service Agreement Cinema City” made on 8 June 2005 (TB 044). This document was not stamped. We will refer to this agreement as the ‘Management Agreement’.
  8. Under the Management Agreement, Westpoint Realty was appointed as sole manager and agent to carry out management services at the Cinema City Property for a period of five years commencing on 8 June 2005. The duties of Westpoint Realty as manager under the agreement included negotiating agreements to lease, lease and licence documents, negotiating rent reviews, instructing and employing contractors, lodging applications or objections on behalf of the owners and making insurance claims on behalf of the owner (TB 044 clause 3.2, pdf 4). This Management Agreement is in substantially the same terms and has the same regime for fees and charges as the Management Agreements for Earlmist and Silkchime.
  9. Cinema City also relies upon two project cost summaries for the “Cinema City Apartments” Project which correspond to the amounts set out in the tax invoices. Each has a breakdown of the costs in the tax invoices issued to Westpoint Realty. The costs are for marketing (with numerous sub-categories), selling, leasing and administration. They are titled ‘Westpoint Realty – Project Cost Summary’ and are:
    (a) dated 20 December 2005 for a total (including GST) of $3,136,045 (TB 030.25); and
    (b) dated 16 January 2006 for a total (including GST) of $3,767,500 (TB 030.29).

Has Cinema City established it made creditable acquisitions?

  1. Like the other applications, there are doubts raised by the evidence before us such that it is difficult to ascertain what was supplied, if anything, and the identity of the entity to whom it was supplied. Our view is that this means Cinema City has not discharged its burden under s 14ZZK of the TAA.
  2. One issue arises out of the evidence regarding the ownership of the land. Cinema City never owned the land, and it only had a licence to occupy it from 1 October 2005, which was a period of two and three months prior to the issue of the tax invoices in December 2005 and January 2006. If that is so, there must be some doubt over whether any work that may have been carried out was carried out at the behest of Cinema City or another entity - perhaps Goldtag Pty Ltd (ACN 085 432 239) (‘Goldtag’), which was also mentioned in the documents as having an ownership interest in the project (see below).
  3. Cinema City submitted it was “the active property developer of stage 2 of the project, not Goldtag”. They say that, from 2003, Cinema City had an option and was developing stage 2 with Goldtag retaining the residual property. They say that whilst a supply was made to Goldtag, a supply was also made to Cinema City as the entity entrusted with developing the property and as the entity who was to pay, and did pay, for the works by Westpoint Constructions (Applicant’s closing submissions in reply, para [92]).
  4. As we stated above for Earlmist, we agree it may be possible that creditable acquisitions could be made by someone other than the owner of land, for example another entity appointed or engaged to undertake a development on the land. However, one would expect to see clear evidence of the arrangement pursuant to which that occurred. The short timeframe is also an issue: a licence to occupy and develop the land that covers a two-to-three-month period calls into question what could have been supplied in such a short timeframe. We deal with this in more detail below.

Who made the creditable acquisitions?

  1. In this section, we address who made the creditable acquisitions. We also touch on what was supplied, which we address in further detail under the next sub-heading.
  2. The starting point is that the land being developed as part of the Cinema City project was the land comprised in lot 32 on plan 12711 and more particularly described in Certificate of Title volume 1657, folio 653 (TB 030.17). Goldtag became the registered proprietor of the land on 12 April 1999.
  3. Mr Carey was a director of Goldtag from 8 December 1998 to 26 May 2013 when the company was deregistered. Mr Rundle was a Company Secretary from 8 December 1998 until 27 January 2006 (TB 031.31, pdf 2-3).
  4. Goldtag was the trustee of a trust named the Cinema City Property Trust. This was shown on an application for approval to commence development made on 22 June 2001 (TB 030.35, pdf 66-67). Westpoint Corporation was described in some documentation as also being a trustee. An authority dated 26 June 2003 given to the town planning firm who made the development applications for the project was signed for and on behalf of Westpoint Corporation and Goldtag as trustees for Cinema City Property Trust (TB 030.35, pdf 203). However, in his evidence at the hearing Mr Carey was asked if Westpoint Corporation was a trustee from time to time, to which he responded “no”. He did not know why Westpoint Corporation was cited as an owner of the land (transcript/224), but later thought it should be a reference to Goldtag care of Westpoint Corporation (transcript/225).
  5. Correspondence from the town planning firm says their client (TB 030.35, pdf 65):
    ... has instructed us to pursue a two-staged approval process (Stage 1 being the refurbishment of the existing building/ cinema complex) and Stage 2 being the hotel / residential component.
  6. An approval to commence development was given to Goldtag as trustee for Cinema City Property Trust by the City of Perth on 26 July 2001. The approval was valid for two years and stated, “If development is not substantially commenced within this period a fresh approval must be obtained before commencing or continuing the development” (TB 030.35, pdf 82-83).
  7. An application for approval to commence development of Stage 2 (described as “hotel, residential, cinema and retail shopping arcade, with basement and tenant car parking”) was made on 31 July 2001. The owner of the land was described on the development application as Westpoint Corporation and Goldtag as trustees for Cinema City Property Trust (TB 030.35, pdf 87-88).
  8. An amended development application was made on 12 September 2001 (TB 030.35, pdf 117). Amended plans were lodged with the City of Perth on 30 November 2001 (TB 030.35, pdf 128) which were considered at the Council meeting on 11 December 2001 although the Council did not decide the application on that occasion (TB 030.35, pdf 132 and 134). Further amended plans were sent to the City of Perth on 20 December 2001 (TB 30.35, pdf 134).
  9. Approval for a 23-level building to be constructed above the existing Cinema City Complex in accordance with the 20 December 2001 plans was subsequently given to Westpoint Corporation and Goldtag as trustee for Cinema City Property Trust by the City of Perth, subject to numerous conditions, on 23 January 2002. Again, the approval was for a two-year period with the caveat that a fresh approval would be required if the development was not substantially commenced within that period (TB 30.35, pdf 136-139).
  10. Mr Carey’s evidence at the hearing was that before construction commenced, he applied to have a “3D subdivision” approved (transcript/225). This was to allow for the construction of a tower which included a hotel complex, residential apartments, and commercial premises on the lower level. The town planners had recommended the 3D subdivision which involved the creation of each separate freehold title above another freehold title lot (A5, para [159]).
  11. An application was made to the Western Australian Planning Commission to create two such lots in approximately August 2002. However, on 12 December 2002, the Western Australian Planning Commission resolved that the application be refused (TB 030.35, pdf 160). An appeal was lodged in the Town Planning Appeal Tribunal of Western Australia on 10 February 2003 (TB 030.35, pdf 165).
  12. On 2 September 2003, a Call Option Agreement (TB 022.10) was entered into. Again, this document was not contemporaneously stamped. The Call Option Agreement was between Goldtag (as Grantor and registered proprietor of the land) and CCD Company as trustee for the Cinema City Development Trust (as the Grantee). The document was executed by Mr Carey and Mr Rundle in their capacities as Director and Company Secretary of both companies (TB 022.10, pdf 7).
  13. Under this agreement, in return for a “call option fee” of $5000, Goldtag granted the Grantee or the Grantee’s Nominee a call option to purchase the “Development Lot” from the Grantor for the purchase price of $12,000,000. The call option could be exercised during the option period from 31 January 2002 and expiring on 30 January 2007 (clauses 1.1, 2.1, 4).
  14. The “Development Lot” was defined as “the proposed lot which is to be constructed as part of a strata titled development and is to be created after the Subdivision and which is referred to as lot 2 the Proposed Plan of Subdivision” (clause 1.1). The “Subdivision” was defined as “the subdivision of the Land into two separate lots for the purpose of the Town Planning and Development Act 1928 (WA) in connection with the Project”. The “Land” was the location of the Cinema City site at 580 Hay Street Perth, which was described in the agreement with reference to its registered description, “part of Lot 32 on Plan 12711, the whole of the land in Certificate of Title Volume 1657 Folio 653”.
  15. Clause 3 of the Call Option Agreement governed the exercise of the call option. To exercise the call option, clause 3.1 provided the Grantee or the Grantee’s Nominee needed to deliver an executed Notice of Exercise of Call Option (in the form set out in Annexure A to the agreement) by 5.00pm on 30 January 2007 (the last day of the call option exercise period). If the call option was being exercised by the Grantee’s Nominee, a Notice of Nomination executed by the Grantee and the Grantee’s Nominee was required. Clause 3.2 provided that if the Grantee complied with clause 3.1, a binding contract for the sale and purchase of the Development Lot was immediately created.
  16. Clause 5.1 also provided:

“At any time before the exercise of the Call Option, the Grantee may nominate a Nominee to assign the whole of its interest under this agreement by delivering to the Grantor a Notice of Nomination signed by both the Grantee and the Grantee’s Nominee”.

  1. Importantly, the Call Option Agreement was conditional upon an application for subdivision of the Development Lot being lodged with the Western Australian Planning Commission within three months of the date of the agreement (that is, within 3 months of 2 September 2003) (clause 7(a)) and the Western Australian Planning Commission granting approval for the subdivision of the Development Lot within six months after the date of the agreement, or within a longer period agreed between the Grantor and the Grantee (clause 7(b)). At the hearing Mr Carey said the six-month period in clause 7(b) was extended due to delays in the subdivision being approved (A5, para [115(a)]), and that he recalled signing a document to that effect but said he had not been able to locate it (transcript/237).
  2. Mr Carey’s evidence was that a Town Planning Appeal Tribunal hearing was set down for 21 June 2004 (A5, para [166]).
  3. His evidence was that in the interim, given planning approval from the City of Perth would expire on 22 January 2004 “and has not been implemented”, on 15 July 2003, the town planners’ lodged a development application with the Council to renew the planning approval granted on 22 January 2004 (A5, para [167]; TB 030.35, pdf 198). There is no evidence of the renewal of the planning approval being granted.
  4. Mr Carey’s evidence was that in June 2004, a decision was made to withdraw the proceedings before the Town Planning Appeal Tribunal and to revise the plans for the development (A5, para [168]).
  5. Thus, as well as there being no documentary evidence that the time frames in clause 7 were extended to allow for delays in the subdivision approval, Mr Carey conceded the condition precedent to the Call Option Agreement was never satisfied (transcript/237). It follows the subdivision of the land into two separate lots was never approved by the Western Australian Planning Commission. Additionally, we accept the Commissioner’s submission that there is no evidence, in the form of the Notice of Exercise of Call Option contemplated by clause 3.1, that the call option was ever exercised by CCD Company as trustee for the Cinema City Development Trust (or any nominee, as well as there being no evidence of any Notice of Nomination). There is also no evidence of a purchase price ever being paid. We infer this is probably because the contract never became conditional.
  6. At the hearing Mr Carey’s evidence was that, at the time of the renewal application “there had been substantial works carried out in the basement of that building and also the cinemas had been demolished” (transcript/225-226).
  7. At the hearing Mr Carey was asked about the ‘Project Payment – Construction Works Summary’ for 20 November 2005 (TB 030.21). He agreed a substantial amount of the work evidenced in that progress payment was carried out prior to 1 October 2005, being the date the Cinema City D & C Contract was made (transcript/239). However, it is problematic that the Cinema City D & C Contract is not expressed to be retrospective.
  8. We agree with the Commissioner’s submission that the work described by Mr Carey was likely related to the development approval for Stage 1 because the application to commence development referred to basement parking and the refurbishment of the existing cinemas (TB 030.35, pdf 66; Respondents closing submissions, para [337]).
  9. It is also problematic for Cinema City that the evidence suggests Stage 2 did not progress past the design stage. This calls into question whether any supplies were made, and if so, what was supplied.
  10. Plans for the development were being prepared from approximately October 2004, when preliminary concept plans had been prepared (TB 030.35, pdf 204). Revised schematic designs were prepared by 15 April 2005 whereby the town planners had “deleted the proposed high-rise hotel component and worked within the building’s existing structural grid to achieve residential apartments with an average area of 60 square metres each” (pdf 217). Plot ratio calculation options were provided in May 2005 (pdf 219). A design brief and project design assessment were prepared in July 2005 (pdf 221-228).
  11. In September 2005, the town planners informed the Western Australian Planning Commission of their intention to lodge a development application by requesting a pre-lodgement meeting (TB 030.35, pdf 232).
  12. Email communications between the town planners and Westpoint show design options were being discussed in October 2005 (TB 030.35, pdf 234-236; 240-241). A briefing note dated 11 October 2005 records meetings with the City of Perth on 23 September 2005 and 3 October 2005 having occurred and that the architects had prepared two new designs (pdf 238). Project Control Group meeting minutes dated 14 October 2005 discuss the proposed development application (pdf 242). A letter to the City of Perth dated 25 October 2005 from the town planners refers to “a residential and retail development” (pdf 250).
  13. As part of The Trustee’s objection, they produced another ‘Deed of Agreement’ between Goldtag and CCD Company as trustee for the Cinema City Development Trust, defined as ‘CCD’. It is dated 1 October, but the year is obscured (TB 022.11). A further copy was produced in these proceedings which shows the date to be 2005 (TB 043). The Deed of Agreement was signed by Mr Carey and Mr Rundle in their capacity as Director and Company Secretary respectively for both companies.
  14. The first recital incorrectly states CCD has purchased the Cinema City Land from Goldtag pursuant to the Call Option Agreement dated 2 September 2003. However, the operative terms of the Deed of Agreement provide that Goldtag grants a licence to CCD to occupy and commence development on the land which included “the pre leasing, pre selling and design & construction of the project” (clause 1). Mr Carey confirmed in his evidence at the hearing that the recital should have said CCD was entitled to purchase the land pursuant to the call option (transcript/237). The licence commenced on 1 October 2005 and was “until settlement of the land which is contracted to be 30 January 2007” (clause 3).
  15. During cross-examination Mr Carey agreed that without that licence, CCD Company did not have a right to occupy and commence development of the land. The full exchange is relevant (transcript/238):
    MR DAVIES: And then, clause 1 of the operative part of the agreement is that Goldtag does in fact grant a licence to Development to occupy and commence development of the land. And I take it that was necessary, because without that licence, Development didn't have the right to occupy and commence development of the land?

    MR CAREY: Yes.

    MR DAVIES: And can I also take it that that means that prior to 1 October 2005, developments had not commenced development?

    MR CAREY: There’d been minor words and things on site, but, no, not the main structure.

    MR DAVIES: Well, prior to receiving a licence to commence development, developments in fact had no right to commence development?

    MR CAREY: Yes, sure, in the legal sense, but I owned both entities and I controlled both entities.

    MR DAVIES: But the purpose of this agreement was, wasn’t it, Mr Carey, that the purpose of it was to give development what it didn’t have, and that was a licence to commence development?

    MR CAREY: Yes, that's right, it was formalised, the situation, yes.

  16. Conditional planning approval was given to Goldtag by the City of Perth on 9 November 2005 to change the use of an existing retail shop to a sales office and showroom (TB 030.35, pdf 244-247). A Project Meeting Agenda dated 30 November 2005 shows the development approval for the revised design had not yet been applied for because it references subdivision and architectural plans not having been finalised and completed (pdf 261).
  17. The hotel development approval appears to drop out of the documentation after the reference to it being “deleted” on 15 April 2005 (TB 030.35, pdf 217-218). However, on 1 September 2005, Westpoint Corporation (Development Manager) and CCD Company as trustee [for] Cinema City Development Trust (Owner)entered into an agreement with Pacific International Suites Perth Pty Ltd (Lessee) and Pacific International Hotels Pty Ltd (Hotel Operator) and Parkes Street Pty Ltd (Guarantor) whereby the Owner agreed to lease the hotel apartments to the Lessee, with the hotel to be operated by the Hotel Operator (TB 030.27, pdf 2). The agreement was executed by Mr Carey and Mr Rundle for the Development Manager and Owner (pdf 6). There was another agreement between those same parties made on 27 October 2005 whereby it was contemplated that the Owner would develop the property (including 190 hotel rooms and serviced apartments) which would be leased to the Lessee, and the apartments would be sold subject to the apartment leases (TB 030.28, pdf 3). Both agreements are unstamped.
  18. Mr Carey’s evidence at the hearing was that he intended to go ahead with the hotel apartment approval, and that the “all apartment development” which did not involve a hotel was being pursued as an alternative (transcript/227-228). He insisted that, as at 11 October 2005, he was “absolutely committed” to the hotel development and he accepted the agreement for Pacific Hotels to lease the hotel was legally enforceable (transcript/231-232). He stated there was a possibility the hotel could relocate to another site he was developing and so obtaining both approvals was a type of “insurance policy” (transcript/230-231). In our view, Mr Carey’s explanation, and his evidence that the Project Meeting Agenda on 30 November 2005 related to the “all apartments” scheme is plausible. This is supported by the fact that the hotel agreements were clearly entered into as they were executed by the parties.
  19. Ultimately, regardless of whether there were one or two development applications being planned and progressed, they were not finalised or approved, and neither was the subdivision. Mr Carey’s evidence was that “Goldtag granted the option and entered into the Deed of Agreement so that the Cinema City Trust could develop the airspace above the existing building that was owned by Goldtag” (A5, para [115]). However, as is evident from the evidence above, the subdivision of the airspace was never approved.
  20. There are several difficulties for Cinema City that arise from this morass of evidence. Firstly, there is doubt about the identity of the entity making creditable acquisitions. The entity was most likely Goldtag who was the registered proprietor of the land. More specifically, the recipient was most likely Goldtag as trustee for the Cinema City Property Trust, as contemplated in the correspondence from the town planners who had applied for development approval and subdivision approval on their behalf. It is also possible Westpoint Corporation was a trustee, as per the authority for the town planners that Mr Carey signed on 26 June 2003 (TB 030.35, pdf 203).
  21. Additionally, Cinema City did not acquire any interest under the Call Option Agreement because the condition precedent was not satisfied, and the Notice of Exercise of Call Option was never given. If we accept the Deed of Agreement made on 1 October 2005 granted a licence to Cinema City to develop and occupy the land, it was only for a two-to-three-month period prior to the periods of the tax invoices, and there was no development approval under which they could commence the development work. Additionally, the Cinema City D & C Contract which appointed Westpoint Constructions to carry out design and construction works was only entered into on 1 October 2005.
  22. The evidence suggests the development did not proceed past the planning stages. At the time of the tax invoices in December 2005 and January 2006, the existing development approval granted for two years on 23 January 2002 had expired. If we accept Mr Carey’s evidence that the licence in the Deed of Agreement was granted so Cinema City could develop the airspace as part of Stage 2, nothing was done on Stage 2 because no development or planning approval for the subdivision of the land into two lots had been granted. Mr Carey referred to “substantial works” to the basement and demolition of the cinema, however, those appear to relate to Stage 1. Coupled with Mr Carey’s admission that a substantial part of the work in the construction works summary for 20 November 2005 was carried out prior to 1 October 2005, it is unclear to us what work could have been carried out from 1 October 2005.

What was supplied by Westpoint Constructions?

  1. In addition to those detailed in the section above, there are further issues raised by the Cinema City D & C Contract that create doubt about what (if anything) was supplied.
  2. If (as we discussed above), Mr Carey was “absolutely committed” to the hotel development as at 11 October 2005, why did the Cinema City D & C Contract made on 1 October 2005 omit reference to the hotel component of the development? Instead, the brief works description in Schedule 1 described the works as the “all apartments” option (TB 022.12, pdf 33).
  3. We have already mentioned above that the Cinema City D & C Contract was not expressed to be retrospective, and yet Mr Carey conceded a substantial amount of the work referred to in that progress payment (supported by the 20 November 2005 construction works summary) was carried out prior to 1 October 2005. Indeed, clause 9.1 titled, “date for building commencement” provided for certain conditions to be satisfied before Westpoint Constructions was to commence carrying out the works. It provided (TB 022.12, pdf 21-22):
    The Contractor must commence the carrying out of the Works within 20 days of the

    last to occur of the:

    9.1.1 Principal producing evidence of the Principal’s title to the Site as required by

    clause 1.4;

    9.1.2 Principal producing evidence of the Principal’s capacity to pay the Contract

    Sum as required by clause 1.5;

    9.1.3 granting, by Authorities, of all Approvals necessary to commence the Works;

    9.1.4 signing of this Agreement; or

  • 9.1.5 granting, by the Principal, of possession of the Site to the Contractor.
  1. Clause 1.4 provided:

“Not later than 10 days after the date of this Agreement the Principal must supply reasonable evidence of the Principal’s title to the Site or authority to construct the Works on the Site”.

  1. However, there was no requirement for Westpoint Constructions to commence the work because CCD Company did not have title to the land which was owned by Goldtag. Even if the Deed of Agreement granted a licence to Cinema City on 1 October 2005 to develop and occupy the land, as we pointed out above, there was no development approval to commence the works given by the City of Perth.
  2. Cinema City also relies upon a letter on Westpoint Consulting Group letterhead dated 15 January 2006 (TB 030.23). The letter stated a site visit had been undertaken on 15 January 2006 and Westpoint Consulting had assessed the value of the work in progress. Westpoint Consulting recommended the payment of the second progress claim of $6,187,181, being the amount in the invoice dated 14 January 2006. The costs are stated, but apart from reference to a site visit it is unclear how the costs were assessed, and there is a disparity between this letter and the Cinema City D & C Contract. The letter refers to the development as the “580 Hay Street Perth Hotel/ Apartment Project”, whereas the hotel was not included in the Cinema City D & C Contract which instead referred to an “all apartments” arrangement. In a summary of the works and progress that had been completed to date, the letter refers to “development approval issued and amended development approval lodged”. As we discussed above, the development approval had expired, and the amended development approval had not yet been lodged. The list refers to the hotel, namely that the “interior design of hotel advanced in collaboration with hotel operator” (pdf 2). As was stated for Earlmist, the letter was signed but it is not clear who the signatory was. In the circumstances, it is unclear what we should make of this letter.
  3. Ultimately, we are unable to make a finding about what work was done under the Cinema City D & C Contract, who it was done for, and whether the work and any payments were authorised under the Cinema City D & C Contract.

What was supplied by Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem?

  1. Cinema City submitted the two tax invoices issued by Westpoint Realty were authorised under the Agency Agreement. They rely on the two project cost summaries (dated 20 December 2005 and 16 January 2006) which we referred to above. The project cost summaries contain a breakdown of marketing costs, selling commissions and a leasing commission for the “Pacific International Hotel” (TB 030.25; 030.29). In the 20 December 2005 project cost summary, there is a selling “commission – apartments” of $999,137. In the 16 January 2006 project cost summary there is a selling “commission – apartments” of $2,402,305.
  2. Annexure B of the Agency Agreement stated the Agent’s commission was four percent of the contract price for the property, or such other rate as agreed (TB 022.13, pdf 17).
  3. Mr Carey’s evidence was that the $999,137 selling commission in the 20 December 2005 project cost summary represented two percent of the value of contracts exchanged for the hotel apartments. In support, he referred to a status report for Cinema City dated 20 December 2005 which stated there were 140 contracts exchanged in September 2005 for $49,956,850, with two percent of that amount being $999,137 (A5, para [131(a)]; TB 030.26; STB 142.10).
  4. With respect to the selling commission of $2,402,305 stated in the 16 January 2006 project cost summary, Mr Carey’s evidence was that it represented four percent of 105 contracts exchanged for the residential apartments for $60,057,625 (TB 030.26). Mr Carey said the four percent was payable due to the variation dated 12 May 2005 (which we referred to above) which extended the term of the Agency Agreement by five years and entitled the Agent to charge the full commission (TB 030.30).
  5. In any event, we agree with the Commissioner’s submission that the preconditions to the Agent becoming entitled to remuneration under clause 5.2 of the Agency Agreement (TB 022.13, pdf 9) were not satisfied. Clause 5.2 provided, in part:
    5.2.1 Commission payable to the Agent on the sale of a Property shall be

    paid as follows:

  • (1) on the date upon which the Contract for the sale of that Property becomes unconditional and a deposit of at least 10% of the purchase price for the Property has been paid by the purchase, the Agent shall be entitled to invoice the Principal for payment of 50% of the commission;
  • (2) on the date upon which settlement of the sale and purchase of the Property pursuant to the contract is effected, the Agent shall be entitled to invoice the Principal for payment of the balance of the commission; ...
  1. However, the contracts for sale for the residential apartments were conditional upon titles being issued (see, for example, A12, Annexure B, clauses 10.1(a), 10.2(a)(ii)). Mr Carey agreed this was correct in his evidence at the hearing (transcript/253). The contracts for sale also contemplated the contract could be terminated if the Strata subdivision was not approved and registered (clauses 6.3). No titles were ever issued, and the subdivision was never approved. Additionally, although CCD Company as trustee for Cinema City Development Trust was expressed to be the vendor, Goldtag remained the owner, and therefore the vendor had no title it could sell. Mr Carey conceded the sales were never finalised (transcript/251).
  2. The status report dated 20 December 2005 refers to contracts being exchanged for 140 “Hotel apartments – PRD” for $49,956,850 (TB 030.26). These 140 contracts were also referenced in a Westpoint Realty Marketing & Sales Plan for Cinema City dated December 2005 as the “total sold” (TB 030.37, pdf 3). This plan continued to state that of 190 hotel apartments, 140 had been sold “through the SE Asian Introducer AUSTPAC PRD Realty (Singapore) Pte Ltd” (TB 030.37, pdf 4). Mr Carey confirmed the reference to these contracts being exchanged was a reference to a Put Option Deed (transcript/251).
  3. The Put Option Deed was dated 18 September 2005 and was between CCD Company (Developer) and Austpac Prd Realty (Singapore) Pte Ltd (‘Austpac’) and David Yuen Sing Chi (Guarantor) (STB 142.9). The background to the Put Option Deed stated Austpac had agreed to introduce purchasers to purchase the Units or to purchase the Property. In summary, ‘Property’ was defined to include units that are not the subject of a sale, and the ‘Units’ as those listed in Annexure E (although there is no Annexure E). As such, it is not a contract of sale, but rather a type of agency agreement. The Put Option Deed contemplates that further work and approvals were required before the development could proceed. For example, the ‘Background’ section states, “The Developer proposes to construct the Development and wishes to establish both the viability of the Development and security for construction and development finance”. Clause 19 provides for what happens if the Developer does not complete the Development. If that were to happen, the Developer was liable to pay Austpac two percent of the price “in full and final settlement of all claims, costs and damages of Austpac resulting from the Development not being completed by the Developer”. Under cross-examination Mr Carey agreed the Put Option Deed was conditional upon titles being issued (transcript/253). Consequently, we agree with the Commissioner that, firstly, the Put Option Deed is not a contract of sale under which the liability to pay commission could arise, and even if it was, the fact it was conditional meant that no commission was payable under clause 5.2 of the Agency Agreement.
  4. Cinema City claims the leasing commissions of $1,625,000 in the December 2005 and $1,072,500 in the January 2006 tax invoices were charged under the Management Agreement (TB 030.25; 030.29). Mr Carey’s evidence was that leasing commissions were charged with respect to agreements made with Pacific International Hotels Pty Ltd (A5, para [131(b)] and [132(b)]. He described a somewhat convoluted calculation method as to how these amounts were arrived at: “The commission was based on the net rent of $3,250,000, multiplied by 9 months, multiplied by 9% and adjusted for incentives, and then paid in part” (A5, para [132(b)(iii)]).
  5. We have already referred to the agreements with Pacific International Suites Perth Pty Ltd (Lessee) and Pacific International Hotels Pty Ltd (Hotel Operator) above. The first was dated 1 September 2005 (TB 030.27) and the second was dated 27 October 2005 (TB 030.28). In his evidence at the hearing Mr Carey described the first agreement as “more or less heads of agreement” which led to the second agreement which was more comprehensive (transcript/245). The second agreement could be regarded as an agreement for lease, given clause 2.1 (TB 030.28, pdf 7) which provides:
    The Owner grants and the Lessee accepts the Apartment Leases and the Common Property Lease in the form of the lease annexed with:
  • (1) any amendments as may be agreed; and
  • (2) the latest form of the Plan of Subdivision and the latest form of the Rules attached.
  1. There are, however, no attachments to the document that was provided to us.
  2. Additionally, recital C of the 1 September 2005 agreement stated the Owner had obtained development approvals from the City of Perth (TB 030.27, pdf 2), but as we explained above, the development approval had expired, and no additional approval application had been lodged for Stage 2 at that time.
  3. A more fundamental problem for Cinema City, in our view, is that the agreements describe CCD Company as trustee [for] Cinema City Development Trust’ as being the registered proprietor of the land. It was not at that time, nor was it ever entitled to become the registered proprietor and so there was no apparent legal basis upon which it could grant a lease over any part of the Cinema City land.
  4. Our conclusions regarding marketing and legal fees purportedly charged under the Agency and Management Agreements are substantially the same as for Earlmist and Silkchime. Substantial marketing costs are listed in the project cost summaries. In the 20 December 2005 project cost summary (TB 030.25), the marketing costs total $469,999 and comprise sums for “market research”, “advertising & marketing – general”, “consultants – marketing & PR Telemarketing”, “telemarketing”, “legal costs – advertising & marketing”, “Westpoint multimedia expenses” and “advertising – print”. In the 16 January 2006 project cost summary (TB 030.29), the marketing costs total $271,387 which comprise sums for “market research”, “advertising & marketing – general”, “consultants – marketing & PR telemarketing”, “telemarketing”, “Westpoint multimedia expenses” and “advertising – print”. To the extent those costs are listed in the project cost summaries, it is unclear to us what exactly was supplied that gave rise to those sums. Again, there is no documentation to support or substantiate how those specific sums were arrived at. The evidence of Ms Rigg was that marketing work took place, but she was not aware of how the amounts were determined (transcript/382-383).

Issues regarding the payment of the tax invoices via set-off

  1. As with the other applications before us, the Applicants (in this instance Cinema City) allege the tax invoices were paid by way of a mutual set-off of book entries. Cinema City also relies on unstamped loan agreements between each of the entities, drawdown notices, journal entries and trial balances.
  2. The specific loan agreements and drawdown notices on which Cinema City relies to evidence the mutual set-off are:
    (a) A loan agreement dated 1 July 2002 between CCD Company as borrower and Westpoint Corporation as lender for a commitment of $35,000,000, for the period 1 July 2002 to 1 July 2008 (TB 030.18). Mr Carey’s evidence was that this loan agreement was entered into by CCD Company as trustee for the “Cinema City Trust” (A5, para [116]), but the agreement states CCD Company is the borrower. Also, the Cinema City Development Trust was not established until 31 October 2002 (TB 031.25, pdf 5), after the loan agreement was entered into.
Clause 2 states “The Borrower shall use the net proceeds of all Loans for the purpose specified in Item 9 and for no other purpose” (pdf 10). Item 9 states the purpose, as contemplated by clause 2, is “To provide funds to assist with the acquisition and development of land located at 45 Bay St, Port Melbourne” (pdf 30). It seems clear this agreement does not apply to the Cinema City Development. There is also no evidence of any drawdown by CCD Company under this agreement.
(b) A loan agreement dated 20 August 2003 between Westpoint Constructions as borrower and CCD Company as lender for a commitment of $20,000,000 (TB 030.19).

On 20 December 2005, Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Westpoint Constructions wished to draw $9,064,000 on 20 December 2005 (TB 030.31), being the GST inclusive amount of the tax invoice of the same date.

On 14 January 2006, Mr Carey signed another drawdown notice stating Westpoint Constructions wished to draw $6,187,181 on 14 January 2006 (TB 030.33) being the GST inclusive amount of the tax invoice of the same date.

(c) A loan agreement dated 20 August 2003 between Westpoint Realty as borrower and CCD Company as lender for a commitment of $10,000,000 (TB 030.20).

On 20 December 2005 Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Westpoint Realty wished to draw $3,136,045 on 20 December 2005 (TB 030.32), which again corresponded to the GST inclusive amount in the Westpoint Realty invoice of the same date.

On 14 January 2006, Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Westpoint Realty wished to draw $3,767,500 on 14 January 2006 (TB 030.34), which corresponded to the GST inclusive amount in the Westpoint Realty invoice of the same date.

  1. Mr Carey agreed the purpose of the loan drawdowns on 20 December 2005 and 14 January 2006 was to enable the payment of the tax invoices to be offset against the loans (transcript/265).
  2. Like the other applications we discussed above (Earlmist and Silkchime), Cinema City also relies upon incomplete handwritten journal entries to substantiate a set-off. In each entry, the entity is stated as ‘(61) Cinema City Development’. As with Earlmist and Silkchime, the “journal number”, “date prepared”, “prepared by”, “entered by”, “authorised by” and “date entered” are blank. They are:
    (a) A journal entry form with an “effective journal date” of 20 December 2005 (TB 022.19, pdf 1). The amounts in the 20 December 2005 tax invoices are set-off against the respective loan accounts of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty.

    (b) A journal entry form with an “effective journal date” of 14 January 2006 (TB 022.19, pdf 2). The amount of the Westpoint Constructions tax invoice 0624-04 is set-off against the loan account for Westpoint Constructions.

    (c) A journal entry form with an “effective journal date” of 16 January 2006 (TB 022.19, pdf 3). The amount of the Westpoint Realty tax invoice 085 – 03 is set-off against the loan account for Westpoint Realty.

  3. A Cinema City Development Trust trial balance as at 31 January 2006 (TB 022.18) shows a credit to “Loan – Westgem Unit Trust” of $3,767,500 and a credit to “Loan – Westpoint Constructions” of $6,187,181. A trial balance was not provided for December 2005. There are no other accounting records for the Cinema City Development Trust (such as general ledgers) before us.
  4. There are also no corresponding accounts from Westpoint Corporation or Westpoint Realty to substantiate the mutual set-off. As we explained earlier in these reasons, the GSTD 2004/4 persuasively suggests there would ordinarily be an express or implied agreement between the entities that set-off can be used. There is no evidence of this, nor is there any evidence of whether the companies’ end of financial year statements reflect that an obligation to pay has been replaced with a set-off arrangement.
  5. Further doubts are raised by the unfortunate timing of the tax invoices. The invoices were issued in December 2005 and January 2006. As we have previously noted, the Westpoint group was on the verge of collapse. A receiver was appointed to Westpoint Constructions on 2 February 2006, followed by a liquidator on 22 February 2006 (TB 012.36, pdf 5). A receiver and manager was appointed to Westpoint Realty on 20 April 2006 (TB 012.38, pdf 5).
  6. As we found for Earlmist and Silkchime, the reports as to affairs made by the receivers, managers and liquidators are inconsistent with the inter-company debts and mutual set-offs alleged by Cinema City. These included:
    (a) A report by the administrators of Westpoint Realty dated 2 February 2007 listed monies owed to related entities at clause 5.1.2. Cinema City Property Trust was listed as being owed $420.00 and there was no mention of CCD Company (TB 012.43, pdf 17).

    (b) A report as to affairs dated 11 August 2006 from the liquidator of Westpoint Constructions, which was verified by Mr Rundle on 9 August 2006, did not list CCD Company, or CCD Company as trustee for the Cinema City Development Trust as a debtor or unsecured creditor (TB 031.A2, pdf 7 and 9).

    (c) A report as to affairs dated 27 February 2006 by the liquidator of Westpoint Corporation, signed by Mr Rundle and Mr Nairn on 23 February 2006 lists “Loan – Cinema City Development Trust” as a debtor with the amount owing being $1,070,535.75; and “Loan – Cinema City Property Trust” as a debtor with the amount owing being $671,962.32 (TB 031.A1, pdf 10 and 11).

  7. As was the case with Earlmist and Silkchime, Mr Carey’s said inconsistencies could be explained by the fact the receiver and manager and the liquidator did not have up-to-date financial information. Again, we note there is no mention of a lack of up-to-date financial information in any of the reports and the external managers were not called as witnesses to identify the information (or shortcomings in the information) before them. We also note the evidence of Mr Nairn that the accounts within the Westpoint group were properly kept to correctly record the financial position of the companies, including Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty (transcript/366-367). That evidence suggests the receivers and managers and the liquidators would have had the correct and up-to-date financial information.
  8. In summary, there are simply too many issues and inconsistencies, together with a lack of corroborating evidence, such that we cannot be satisfied, on the balance of probabilities, that a mutual set-off occurred.

Conclusion for Cinema City

  1. In summary, similar issues and concerns arose for Cinema City as for Earlmist and Silkchime. For the reasons we have outlined above, we find Cinema City has not established on the evidence that it made creditable acquisitions from Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty. Those reasons included:
    (a) Firstly, and fundamentally, we are not persuaded Heca Nominees was validly appointed as an additional trustee to the CCD Trust. No steps were taken to vest the trust property in the additional trustee, nor was the Federal Court or the receivers and managers ever advised an additional trustee was appointed. The failure to stamp the Additional Trustee Deed and Ratification Deed further adds to this doubt because contemporaneous stamping would have given an indication as to when these documents were created.

    (b) It was unclear who made any creditable acquisitions from Westpoint Constructions. We concluded it was most likely any work was carried out for Goldtag, who was the registered proprietor of the land, or more particularly, Goldtag as trustee for the Cinema City Property Trust. This is evidenced by the correspondence with the town planners, the Stage 1 development approval and the proposed development approval being in that name. To further complicate matters, there was also reference to Westpoint Corporation and Goldtag being trustees for the Cinema City Property Trust.

    (c) It was also unclear to us what, if any, work was carried out by Westpoint Constructions. It appeared to us the work undertaken on the basement and existing cinema related to Stage 1 of the development. This work pre-dated the Cinema City D & C Contract which was not retrospective. The evidence suggests the work on Stage 2 did not progress past the planning stages. Indeed, Cinema City only had a licence to develop and occupy the land from 1 October 2005, which was a period of two and three months prior to the issue of the tax invoices in December 2005 and January 2006. Also, there was no development approval for Stage 2 and the subdivision of the 3D lot was never approved.

    (d) We also found that, with respect to any taxable supplies being made by Westpoint Realty, there was no contractual basis upon which sales commissions could be charged because the contracts for the sale of the 105 residential apartments and 140 hotel apartments never became unconditional and so no commission was payable under clause 5.2 of the Agency Agreement. With respect to the two agreements with Pacific International Suites Perth Pty Ltd (Lessee) and Pacific International Hotels Pty Ltd (Hotel Operator), there was no clear legal basis upon which Cinema City could contract or agree to enter into any future leases. This is because it was not the registered proprietor, whereas it appears Goldtag was. We also found it was unclear as to how the amounts stated in the project cost summaries for marketing were calculated.

    (e) There are similar issues with the evidence concerning set-off that we found for Earlmist and Silkchime. These included incomplete journal entries, a lack of evidence as to whether there was any express or implied agreement between Cinema City and Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty and a lack of evidence of mutual book entries, a lack of corresponding records from Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty showing corresponding loan drawdowns and the amounts payable under the tax invoices and inconsistent reports as to affairs from the receiver and manager and liquidator of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty.

    (f) Similarly, the failure to stamp key documents including the Cinema City D & C Contract, Agency Agreement, the variation dated 12 May 2005 and Management Agreement raise some doubts about provenance, which further add to the overall concerns that we have.

  2. Again, there are so many doubts raised by the evidence that we find Cinema City has not discharged the burden of proving its net amount for the December 2005 and January 2006 tax periods is less than zero. That is, Cinema City has not established on the evidence that it made creditable acquisitions under s 11-5 of the GST Act from Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty whereby Cinema City was entitled to input tax credits for GST of $1,109,095 and $904,971, in whole or in part, for the relevant tax period. Overall, Cinema City has not discharged the burden of proving that the assessment was excessive or otherwise incorrect and what it should have been.
  3. Consequently, we find that the Cinema Reviewable Decision should be affirmed.

BAYSHORE

The entity

  1. The ‘Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust’ (‘Bayshore Trust’) was established on 15 December 1999 by a Deed of Trust (‘Bayshore Trust Deed’) (TB 031.11, pdf 49).
  2. The Trustee was Skypark Holdings Pty Ltd (ACN 090 795 734). The company changed its company name to Bayshore Port Melbourne Pty Ltd ‘(Bayshore Company’) on 10 August 2001 (TB 025.17, pdf 2).
  3. As was the case with the Cinema City Development Trust (‘CCD Trust’), the Quartz Company held all units in the Bayshore Trust (031.11, pdf 44).
  4. The Bayshore Trust Deed was stamped in August 2001 (although the precise date within that month is obscured on the document). (TB 031.11, pdf 2).
  5. Like the CCD Trust, Mr Carey (as Director) and Mr Rundle (as Company Secretary) executed the CCD Trust Deed on behalf of both entities (TB 031.11, pdf 49-50).
  6. As we mentioned above for the Cinema City application, on 3 May 2020, the Quartz Company changed its name to ACN 008 859 103 Pty Ltd (TB 031.A6, pdf 1).
  7. We have already explained (but repeat here so there is no confusion) Mr Carey was a Director and Company Secretary of the Quartz Company from 5 December 1988. Mr Allan Carey was a Director from 5 December 1988 until 12 April 2006. Mr Grant Dodd was a Company Secretary from 28 October 2003 until 1 December 2020 (TB 031.A6, pdf 3-4). Mr Rundle was Company Secretary from 30 June 1997 to 27 January 2006 (TB 031.A6, pdf 3-5).
  8. Mr Carey was the sole Director of the Bayshore Company from 10 December 1999 (TB 025.17, pdf 2-3).
  9. Receivers and managers were appointed to the Bayshore Company on 22 December 2005 until 3 March 2011 (TB 025.17, pdf 4). The receivers and managers completed the property development (A4, para [23(a)]), finalised the settlement of 21 apartments and realised the remaining assets (TB 025.20, pdf 5; 025.21, pdf 5).
  10. A liquidator was appointed on 9 July 2008 until 8 July 2011. The Bayshore Company was deregistered on 19 September 2011 (TB 025.17, pdf 4).
  11. The Applicant in these proceedings is Heca Nominees as trustee for the Bayshore trust. Heca Nominees claims to be entitled to the input tax credits that arose in connection with this development on the same basis that it asserted an entitlement in the Cinema City development. As with Cinema City, an additional document called a ‘Deed of Appointment of Additional Trustee: Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust’ (‘BPM Additional Trustee Deed’) dated 7 December 2005 (TB 025.16) was produced by the Applicant in these proceedings. The parties to the BPM Additional Trustee Deed are the Quartz Company as the Unit Holder and Heca Nominees. The Deed was executed by Mr Carey on behalf of both parties (pdf 3).
