The Deed purported to appoint Heca Nominees as an additional trustee from the date of execution (7 December 2005).
  12. This BPM Additional Trustee Deed is unstamped. We express the same concerns about the failure to stamp this document that we expressed for the equivalent document in the Cinema City application. One of the benefits of the stamping process is that it would provide some evidence of when the document was in existence.
  13. By virtue of the BPM Additional Trustee Deed and a Ratification Deed which we discuss in a moment, the Applicant in these proceedings is Heca Nominees as trustee for the Bayshore Trust. For convenience, we will refer to the applicant entity as ‘Bayshore’. Where the relevant documentation refers more generally to the trustee for the Bayshore Trust, we will refer to the entity as ‘The Trustee’.
  14. Like Cinema City, the Commissioner submitted we should not accept the BPM Additional Trustee Deed as evidence of the appointment of Heca Nominees as an additional trustee of the Bayshore Trust. He argues any purported appointment was ineffective (Respondent’s written closing submissions, para [430]). For reasons very similar to those we gave in the Cinema City application, we agree with the Commissioner.
  15. Clause 56.5 of the Bayshore Trust Deed provides, in part, that “a continuing trustee shall take such action as is necessary to cause title to any assets of the Trust Fund to be amended to include any additional trustee” (TB 031.11, pdf 40). However, Mr Carey conceded at the hearing that no steps were taken to transfer the title of the assets in the Bayshore Trust into the joint names of the two trustees (transcript/344). The Commissioner correctly points out subsequent sales of property in the development by the receivers and managers could only have been carried out by them on the basis the properties were held solely by the Bayshore Company (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [427]).
  16. Also, clause 46 of the Bayshore Trust Deed provides if there is more than one trustee, they “shall act jointly” (TB 031.11, pdf 36). Mr Carey’s evidence at the hearing was that there were two trustees, but they were not acting jointly (transcript/344).
  17. Mr Carey could not recall whether Heca Nominees took any action as trustee prior to 3 September 2009 when Mr Carey wrote to the Commissioner to state Heca Nominees as trustee for the Bayshore Trust was not able to complete the lodgement of BAS for December 2005 due to the appointment of receivers (TB 025.22). We infer from this evidence that no action was taken by Heca Nominees as trustee prior to that time. We agree with the Commissioner’s submission that this effectively means the purported appointment was ineffective because Heca Nominees never held any assets on trust pursuant to the Bayshore Trust (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [430]).
  18. Also, despite Heca Nominees apparently being appointed as an additional trustee on 7 December 2005, the tax invoices in question (which we discuss below), contain no reference to supplies being made to Heca Nominees in any capacity. They were instead issued to the Bayshore Company as trustee for the Bayshore Trust (TB 025.12; 025.13; 025.14). The Commissioner correctly submits there is no evidence of any entity dealing with Heca Nominees as trustee, nor any evidence that a third party such as receivers, managers or liquidators were ever advised of Heca Nominees appointment as an additional trustee (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [429]).
  19. The Commissioner has questioned whether the Bayshore Trust survived the receivership and winding up of the Bayshore Company (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [565]). As we mentioned above, the Bayshore Company was deregistered on 19 September 2011. Despite clause 57 of the Bayshore Trust Deed stating: “The Trustee shall keep a complete and accurate record of all receipts and expenditure on account of the Trust Fund” (TB 031.11, pdf 40), Mr Carey did not produce any current books of account to show the trust fund continued to exist after the Bayshore Company was deregistered (transcript/344). The only records that have been produced, which we discuss below, are a trial balance and incomplete journal entries. Mr Carey insisted the Bayshore Trust still exists (transcript/345). However, in our view, the Commissioner’s criticisms are valid because there are no recent financial records of the trust before us, or any other documents, such as from the liquidators, to suggest the trust assets were not all realised and distributed.
  20. Mr Carey produced a resolution dated 31 August 2021 which purported to approve a Deed of Ratification and authorised Mr Carey to execute it (TB 041.16). We note Mr Carey was the only person in attendance at the meeting in question.
  21. On the same day, 31 August 2021, Mr Carey executed a ‘Deed of Ratification for the Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust’ (TB 041.17) between the Quartz Company (notwithstanding that the company had already changed its name to ACN 008 859 103 Pty Ltd) as Unit Holder, and Heca Nominees as the additional trustee (‘Ratification Deed’). Mr Carey executed the document on behalf of both companies. The fact the document came into existence some 15 years after the Additional Trustee Deed and after these proceedings had been commenced raises questions about the probative value of this document.
  22. The Ratification Deed purported to confirm and ratify the appointment of Heca Nominees as Trustee of the Bayshore Trust. It also purported to confirm and ratify all acts or omissions of Heca Nominees done in its capacity as a Trustee of the Bayshore Trust since the date of its appointment as Additional Trustee on 7 December 2005 (TB 041.17, pdf 3). However, apart from writing to the Commissioner on 3 September 2009 to claim substantial GST refunds were due, and otherwise pursuing the GST claim, there were no apparent acts or omissions to ratify. The Ratification Deed does little to assist Bayshore even if we accepted it according to its terms.
  23. In summary, the evidence leaves us with serious doubts over whether Heca Nominees was appointed as an additional trustee: As we have explained, none of the trust assets were ever transferred into the joint names of the Bayshore Company and Heca Nominees, and Bayshore Company and Heca Nominees never acted jointly as was required by the Bayshore Trust Deed. Additionally, if Heca Nominees was a trustee, it did nothing in that capacity, other than to notify and pursue GST claims with the Commissioner. There is no evidence of Heca Nominees being recognised as a trustee in any third party or related entity dealings involving the Bayshore Trust. Overall, the assertion that Heca Nominees was appointed as a trustee is not substantiated; most of the evidence of that appointment is self-serving evidence provided by Mr Carey that is conveniently timed (from the point of view of the Applicant), inconsistent with some of the known facts, and impossible to verify.

The assessment

  1. The Bayshore Trust was registered for GST on a non-cash accruals basis from 1 July 2001 to 5 December 2007. It lodged monthly BAS statements from September 2001 to November 2005 (TB 025.8; 025.1, pdf 8; A4, para [21]).
  2. Except for three months during this time, the Bayshore Trust reported a refund position and was paid those refunds. The total refunds were $533,194 for September 2001 to June 2002; $744,630 for the 2002 to 2003 income year; $1,728,506 for the 2003 to 2004 income year; $305,007 for the 2004 to 2005 income year; and $341,478 for July 2005 to November 2005. The highest monthly refund claimed was $1,528,563 for the September 2003 tax period (see table at A4, para [21]). The sum of all input tax credits claimed totalled $3,652,815.
  3. Mr Carey’s evidence was that this was consistent with the usual process of developments where there would be significant claims for input tax credits during the development phase and few, if any, sales until the development was completed and the lots were sold (A4, para [22]).
  4. On 18 January 2006, the receivers and managers lodged a BAS for the Bayshore Trust for the December 2005 tax period. They claimed non-capital purchases of $445,327 for which $40,484 of input tax credits were claimed (TB 025.3; 025.19). They continued to do so during the term of their appointment until November 2010 (TB 086, pdf 2).
  5. As we mentioned above, and like the other property developments we discussed above, Mr Carey claims to have sent a letter to the Commissioner dated 3 September 2009 (TB 025.22) claiming “substantial GST refunds are due” to Heca Nominees as trustee for the Bayshore Trust for December 2005. Again, the Commissioner claims to have no record of receiving the letter.
  6. The letter confirmed receivers were appointed without warning on 22 December 2005. It also advised that ASIC and the receivers had taken the entities’ books and records and were refusing to provide access. As a consequence, the letter said a BAS could not be completed immediately but the BAS would be lodged once Mr Carey could gain access to the books.
  7. Again, a statutory declaration from Ms Gray confirms she typed and posted the letter (TB 025.23). We have no reason to doubt Ms Gray’s evidence. However, as we pointed out above, this evidence is consistent with Mr Carey’s holding the contemporaneous view that “substantial refunds” were due, but the evidence does not of itself demonstrate whether, and to what extent, any creditable acquisitions were made by Bayshore.
  8. In an application dated 22 August 2014, declared on 9 September 2014, tax advisors applied for a private ruling for “The Trustee for Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust” (TB 013.8, pdf 1, 2 and 5). They asked the Commissioner to confirm the Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust (of which Heca Nominee was trustee, according to a table located in the body of the accompanying letter at TB 013.8.1, pdf 2) was entitled to input tax credits for the December 2005 tax period, and that their entitlement had not ceased (TB 013.8, pdf 2).
  9. On 26 November 2014, the Commissioner requested further evidence to confirm creditable acquisitions were made (TB 031.A5).
  10. On 22 August 2018, the Commissioner declined to rule on the application because The Trustee had been unable to provide the information the Commissioner required to progress the private ruling (TB 025.25).
  11. On 27 November 2018, The Trustee wrote to the Commissioner to request that the Commissioner issue a nil assessment for the December 2005 tax period (TB 022.25).
  12. On 7 March 2019, the Commissioner declined to issue the nil assessment and stated that the appropriate way to generate review rights under Part IVC of the TAA would be for the trustee to request the Commissioner issue an assessment in relation to the BAS lodged on 18 January 2006 for December 2005 (TB 025.25).
  13. On 18 December 2019, the Commissioner issued a notice of assessment for the December tax period, showing a negative net amount of $40,484 (TB 025.26).

The Reviewable Decision

  1. The tax advisors for ‘The Trustee for the Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust’ lodged an objection dated 31 January 2020 (TB 025.5) to the 18 December 2019 notice of assessment. The grounds for the objection were that the taxpayer was entitled to claim input tax credits of $1,863,429 for the December 2005 tax period and that the taxpayer’s entitlement to refunds or credits had not ceased (TB 025.6).
  2. While the heading to the letter referred to the Trustee for the Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust, the advisers only made plain in a table in the body of the letter that Heca Nominees was the taxpayer. We note Mr Carey signed an authority for the tax advisors to act for Heca Nominees as trustee for the Bayshore Trust on 30 January 2020 (TB 025.7).
  3. The Commissioner disallowed the objection in full on 22 May 2020 (TB 025.27). We will refer to this as the ‘Bayshore Reviewable Decision’. That decision refers to Heca Nominees as the trustee for the Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust (TB 025.1, pdf 5).
  4. On 27 May 2020, ‘The Trustee for the Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust’ sought review of the Bayshore Reviewable Decision in this Tribunal (TB 025.1).

The tax invoices

  1. In a letter dated 30 January 2020 which accompanied the objection, the Trustee’s tax advisors described the Bayshore project (TB 025.6):
    The Taxpayer acquired a major development site in Port Melbourne early 2002 located at 45 Bay Street, Port Melbourne. The Taxpayer undertook a major development of the site into 306 residential apartments and 18 commercial units. The apartments were pre-sold on a strata-titled basis.
  2. Mr Carey described the project in similar terms in his witness statement. He confirmed the project covered three lots at 45 Bay Street, 162 Rouse Street and 50-52 Nott Street, Port Melbourne (A4, paras [12] and [17]).
  3. The objection letter explained The Trustee had engaged Westpoint Constructions “to design and construct the project”; Westpoint Realty “to carry out the marketing and sales of the project” and Westpoint Corporation “to provide development management services to manage the project” (TB 025.6, pdf 2).
  4. Bayshore relies upon the following tax invoices issued to “Bayshore Port Melbourne Pty Ltd ATF Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust”:
    (a) “Tax invoice – number 0324-14 Bayshore Apartments – 45 Bay Street, Port Melbourne” dated 20 December 2005 issued by Westpoint Constructions in the sum of $7,840,065 for “value of work completed to date” including GST of $712,733 (TB 025.12).

    (b) Tax invoice numbered 049-13 dated 20 December 2005 issued by Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem in the sum of $5,462,314 for “marketing, selling and administration Fees” including GST of $496,574 (TB 025.13).

    (c) “Tax invoice – number 1105-078 Bayshore Apartments Development” dated 20 December 2005 issued by Westpoint Corporation in the sum of $7,195,347, including GST of $654,122 (TB 025.14). The work was described as:

    Fee for professional services rendered in respect of the Bayshore Apartments Development, as follows:

    • Development management services up to and including 20 December 2005

    • Service fee for achieving budgeted development approval

    • Ongoing building & development services

    • Ongoing work on the marketing scheme for the residential component of

    the development

Documentation relied upon by Bayshore

  1. We will now outline the documentation that is said to support Bayshore’s claims it made creditable acquisitions from Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem, Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Corporation.

Documents said to establish taxable supplies were made by Westpoint Constructions

  1. The objection letter stated the Trustee relied upon a building contract between the Bayshore Company and Westpoint Constructions to establish Westpoint Constructions made taxable supplies to the Trustee (TB 025.6, pdf 3). There are three versions of the building contract before us.
  2. In written closing submissions, the Commissioner argued, “one of the material documents proffered and relied upon by Mr Carey is a construction contract between Bayshore and Westpoint Constructions that has clearly been doctored for the purpose of the proceedings” (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [77]). Specifically, the Commissioner argued the version of the building contract we refer to below as the First Version (which was incomplete and accompanied the objection) and the version we refer to below as the Third Version were “deliberately doctored for the purposes of these proceedings” (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [465]).
  3. We will now outline the First Version, Second Version and Third Version of the building contract. We note the building contracts in this case were not prepared using the same template we encountered previously.
  4. The First Version was a building contract that was attached to the objection letter as Appendix 4 (TB 025.9). We note:
    (a) This version appears to be incomplete, and there is no evidence on the document to indicate it was stamped.

    (b) The parties are “Bayshore Port Melbourne Pty Ltd” as Principal (although the “Pty” is struck through with two lines on the cover page and on a page titled “This Formal Instrument of Agreement” where the parties are identified) and Westpoint Constructions as Contractor.

    (c) There is an extensive index showing 68 clauses and listing numerous Annexures, followed by a page with the heading “Cooling Off Notice To The Principal” signed by Mr Carey and Mr Rundle. The page after that is headed “Checklist To Be Signed By The Principal” although we note it is unsigned and the checklist has not been completed. The next page is titled “This Formal Instrument of Agreement” and is undated, apart from the year, being 2005.

    (d) At the footer of the second page of the index (pdf 3), there is a reference to the name the document was saved under, “stage2buildingcontract0205.DOC” and on subsequent pages (pdf 4-10).

    (e) Pages one to three of the building contract appear next. Clauses numbered 1 through to 8(2)(e) appear on those pages.

    (f) Clause 3 “The Works”, provides:

    The Works and the work under the Contract comprise the design, construction, commissioning and completion of:

    Domestic Work

    306 residential apartments, and related works as set out in the Contract Documents; and ...

The rest of the clause is left blank. The ASIC bar code on this page is “KOR.1175.001.0214”.

(g) Page four is missing. Page five, which is the last page of this document, appears to be a title page for Schedule 1. It states, “Schedule 1 Conditions of Contract for Design and Construct (AS4300-1995) (as amended)”, but nothing is attached.

(h) There is an ASIC bar code at the bottom right-hand corner of each page. For example, the cover page is bar coded “KOR1175.001.0200”. What we have described here is the totality of the First version of the building contract.

  1. The Second Version of the building contract was produced by Bayshore’s solicitors on 14 April 2021 (TB 031.24; A18, affidavit of Ms Quirk, para [92], attachment CLQ-29).
    (a) This building contract appears to be complete because it contains 68 clauses. The last page is numbered “Page 152”.

    (b) Each page of the building contract is stamped with an ASIC bar code. The bar codes match those on the same pages as the First Version.

    (c) The name the document was saved under, “stage2buildingcontract0205.DOC” appears from the same page of the index as for the First Version (pdf 4-11). At the bottom of the footer of the page titled, “Checklist To Be Signed by the Principal”, the footer reads (pdf 13):

    Bayshore Port Melbourne

    DXC/stage2buildingcontract0205.DOC

    (d) The first five pages are the same as the First Version, including “Pty” being struck through on the cover page and the “This Formal Instrument of Agreement” page. However, there is an additional page after the cover page titled, “Post-it note from page number 200” where a post-it note with “Bayshore” underlined has been scanned by ASIC. Also, page four, which was omitted from the First Version, contains the execution clauses (signed by Mr Carey and Mr Rundle on behalf of both entities). The next page, page five, is the same title page for Schedule 1 that was in the First Version. A document called “Amended General conditions of contract for design and construct” (‘General Conditions’) is attached to Schedule 1.

    (e) The Annexures referred to in the index are included in this version of the building contract.

    (f) Clause 3 is slightly different to the equivalent clause in the First Version. The number of apartments is different and there is an additional last sentence which refers to Stage 2. It states (TB 031.24, pdf 15):

    The Works and the work under the Contract comprise the design, construction, commissioning and completion of:

    Domestic Work

    126 residential apartments, and related works as set out in the Contract Documents; and

    Known collectively as “Stage 2” of the Project.

The ASIC bar code on this page is the same as for the First Version.

(g) Clause 2.1 of the General Conditions defines the “Contract Sum” as “the fixed lump sum amount set out in Annexure Part A ...” (TB 031.24, pdf 24). In “Annexure Part A – Contract Details” the contract sum, as per clause 2.1, is stated to be $17,500,000.00. The ASIC bar code on this page is “KOR.1175.001.0325” (TB 031.24, pdf 126).

(h) In his further witness statement dated 1 November 2021 (A10, para [8], Annexure NPC-28), Mr Carey produced a copy of the building contract as part of his response to the Respondent’s submission that the Third Version of the building contract (discussed below) was “deliberately doctored” to include a higher contract price. The copy he produced is the same as this Second Version.

  1. The Third Version of the building contract was annexed to Mr Carey’s witness statement dated 5 July 2021 (TB 030.3; A5, para [10(c)], attachment BPM-3).
    (a) There is no scan of the post-it note on this version.

    (b) The ASIC bar code on the page where clause 3 appears is the same as in the Second Version (pdf 14).

    (c) This is a complete version of the building contract. The Annexures stated in the index are included in this version of the building contract.

    (d) The footer starts from the same index page as the First Version and the Second Version, and is the same, “stage2buildingcontract0205.DOC” (pdf 3-11). Like the Second Version, on the page titled, “Checklist To Be Signed by the Principal”, the footer reads (pdf 12):

    Bayshore Port Melbourne

    DXC/stage2buildingcontract0205.DOC

    (e) Clause 3 is the same as the First Version of the contract (TB 030.3, pdf 14). It provides:

    The Works and the work under the Contract comprise the design, construction, commissioning and completion of:

    Domestic Work

    306 residential apartments, and related works as set out in the Contract Documents; and

The ASIC bar code on this page is the same code as the First Version and the Second Version.

(f) In “Annexure Part A – Contract Details” the contract sum, as per clause 2.1, is $40,000,000.00 more than the sum of $17,500,000.00 stated in the Second Version. It stated to be $57,500,000.00 (TB 030.3, pdf 124). The ASIC code on this page is slightly blurry and appears to be one digit different from the bar code in the Second Version. In the Second Version it is “KOR.1175.001.0325”. In the Third Version it is “KOR.1175.001.0326” (TB 030.3, pdf 124).

(g) We agree with the Commissioner’s submission that the Third Version appears to be a complete copy of the First Version (that is, the two versions are likely copies of the same document albeit that the pages missing from the First version appear to be included in the Third Version) (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [466(c)]).

(h) In his witness statement dated 15 July 2021, Mr Nairn stated he was aware Westpoint Constructions was carrying out design and construction works on the Bayshore apartment development and that the works were contracted through the building contract. He annexed a copy of the building contract as Annexure GJN-4 to his witness statement (TB 018.4). We agree with the Commissioner’s submission that this version is a duplicate of the Third Version of the building contract (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [466(d)]).

  1. We note all three versions of the building contract have a page titled, “Cooling Off Notice to the Principal” that bear the same handwritten note at the top right-hand corner of the page (TB 025.9, pdf 11; 031.24, pdf 12; 030.3, pdf 11):
    18/2/05

    ACCEPTED BY

    CASTLE PARTNERS

    AAR

    MFS

  2. The bottom of that page has the handwritten words “Bayshore Port Melbourne Pty Ltd” next to the typed words: “Signed for and on behalf of the Principal”. Mr Carey has signed next to his initial “NPC” and Mr Rundle has signed next to his initial, “GJR”.
  3. In summary, the First Version is an incomplete copy of the Third Version. We are satisfied the Second Version and the Third Version are the same document with one important difference: the Third Version of the building contract has been altered to delete reference to Stage 2 of the project from clause 3, and to insert a different contract sum.
  4. To clarify the issues regarding the various versions of the building contract, on 21 October 2021, a summons was issued to ASIC for production of “A copy of the building contract between Bayshore Port Melbourne Pty Ltd and Westpoint Constructions Pty Ltd which bears the barcode KOR.175.001.0200 on the cover page” (TB 129).
  5. ASIC produced a copy of the building contract on 22 October 2021 which was the same as the Second Version of the building contract before us (TB 130).
  6. The building contract that is apparently for Stage 1 of the project was attached to Mr Carey’s witness statement dated 1 November 2021 (A10, para [6], Annexure NPC-27). The building contract is between “Bayshore Port Melbourne Pty Ltd” as Principal and Westpoint Constructions as Contractor. “Volume 1” appears on the cover page. The date of the contract is not inserted, but 2002 is stated as the year. The works are described in clause 3 as including 186 residential apartments and 16 retail units that are collectively called “Stage 1”. The contract sum is stated as $47,500,000.00 (142.4, pdf 1, 4-5, 111).
  7. We will address the submission that the building contract was “doctored” below, when we consider what was supplied by Westpoint Constructions.

Documents said to establish taxable supplies were made by Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem

  1. In the objection letter, the tax advisors stated “The Trustee for the Bayshore Port Melbourne Trust” relied upon an ‘Agency Agreement’ to establish Westpoint Realty made taxable supplies to the Trustee (TB 025.6, pdf 2). The Agency Agreement was attached as Appendix 5 to the objection letter (TB 025.10).
  2. The Agency Agreement was dated 1 August 2003. The parties were the Bayshore Company as trustee for the Bayshore Trust (Principal) and Westpoint Realty (Agent) (TB 025.10). It was executed by Mr Carey as Director and Mr Rundle as Company Secretary on behalf of both parties. The Agency Agreement is not stamped.
  3. Under the Agency Agreement, Westpoint Realty was appointed “to market and sell Properties in the Development during the Agency Period” in accordance with a marketing strategy and plan (clause 2.1, pdf 3; 3.1, pdf 3). Westpoint Realty was required to use its best endeavours to promote the development and effect sales (clause 3.3, pdf 4).
  4. The “Property” was defined as “each unit notified by the Principal to the Agent after the date of this agreement as available for sale from time to time and forming part of the Development” (clause 1.1, pdf 3). The ‘Properties in the Development’ was described as “Property at 45 Bay Street, Port Melbourne, VIC” (Annexure A, pdf 13). The Principal was to provide the Agent with a schedule of initial sales prices for the properties (clause 3.2, pdf 4).
  5. Clause 5 concerned the Agent’s Remuneration. Commission was payable to the Agent at four percent of the contract price (clause 5.1; Annexure B). That commission was payable in accordance with clause 5.2 which provided (pdf 7):
    Except as may be varied in Annexure B, commission payable to the Agent on the sale of a Property shall be paid as follows:
(a) the Agent shall be entitled to invoice the Principal and be paid for payment of 100% of the commission, when:-
(i) a deposit has been paid by the purchaser in accordance with a contract; or

(ii) a Deposit Power Bond has been received by the Principal or the Agent on account of the deposit payable by the Purchaser.

(b) the Agent shall be entitled to invoice the Principal and be paid the balance of the commission on the date upon which settlement of the sale and purchase of the Property pursuant to the Contract is effected.

(c) The Agent shall be entitled to issue and present to the Principle [sic] an Irrevocable Authority pertaining to each recognized sale in the development

The expression, “Deposit Power Bond” is not defined.

  1. Clause 5.3 provided “Any commission is exclusive of any GST” and added the Principal was entitled to any input tax credit in relation to a supply made under this agreement (pdf 7).
  2. The term of the agreement was for the “Agency Period” (clause 1.1). The Agency Period commenced on the date of the agreement (1 August 2003) and ended 36 months after that date, or on the date the deed was terminated under clause 8 (which should be a reference to clause 7 which covers termination if either party defaults), whichever was the first to occur.
  3. In his witness statement dated 31 August 2021 (A6, para [18(c)]), Mr Carey attached a letter on Bayshore Company letterhead dated 12 May 2005, executed by Mr Carey on behalf of Bayshore Company and Westpoint Realty (TB 041.4) which extended the term of the agreement and entitled the agent to invoice 100% commission. It stated:
    It is hereby agreed that the following variations to the agency agreement dated 01/08/2003.
  4. The term of the agreement be extended by 24 months.
  5. The agent should be entitled to invoice the full commission that will be paid 100% of commissions in relation to the residential apartments sales.
  6. Bayshore relies upon a ‘Westpoint Realty – Project Cost Summary’ for “Bayshore Apartments” dated 20 December 2005 (TB 030.9) for $5,462,314 which comprises:
    (a) Marketing costs of $547,688. The breakdown of these costs is shown as $86,178 for “Advertising & Marketing – General”; $32,440 for “Consultants – Marketing & PR Telemarketing”; $324,675 for “Telemarketing”; $67,624 for “Westpoint Multimedia Expenses”; and $36,771 for “Advertising – Print”.

    (b) Selling costs of $4,914,626 comprising $4,873,631 for “Commission – Apartments” and $40,995 for “Legal”.

Documents said to establish taxable supplies were made by Westpoint Corporation

  1. The objection letter also said the Trustee relied upon a Development Management Agreement to establish Westpoint Corporation made taxable supplies to the Trustee (TB 025.11). The Development Management Agreement was attached to the objection letter as Appendix 6 (TB 025.6, pdf 2).
  2. The Agreement is titled, ‘Development Management Agreement (Bayshore Apartments Development)’ (‘DM Agreement’). The parties are the Bayshore Company as Trustee for the Bayshore Trust (Developer) and Westpoint Corporation (Manager) (TB 025.11). Westpoint Corporation’s ACN is incorrectly stated in item 3 of the Schedule. The date of the agreement is 14 July 2004 (pdf 6). The DM Agreement was executed by Mr Carey as Director and Mr Rundle as Company Secretary of both entities (pdf 8). It is unstamped.
  3. Recital C states it is a condition of a Loan Agreement between the parties that the Developer enter into the DM Agreement. In his witness statement dated 5 July 2021 (A5, para [12(b)]), Mr Carey stated it was not an express term of the Loan Agreement (which was identified as a loan agreement dated 1 July 2003 at TB 030.6) that the DM Agreement was entered into, but the intention of the parties was to enter into it.
  4. Under clause 1 of the DM Agreement, Bayshore appointed Westpoint Corporation to provide management services from the commencement date of 14 July 2004, which was also the date of the agreement. Westpoint Corporation was appointed as the sole and exclusive manager (clause 5).
  5. The management services that Westpoint Corporation was to perform were listed in clause 2, which stated (pdf 2-3):
    The Manager shall, by and with the consent of The Developer:
  1. assess, investigate and report to The Developer within a reasonable time after the commencement of its investigation on the various needs and requirements of The Developer insofar as they relate to the Land and in this respect assess the potential of the Land (in conjunction with its surrounding amenities) to support the Project (the “proposed development”);
  2. authorise the commissioning of limited or full feasibility studies of the proposed development;
  3. instruct and appoint qualified architects, engineers and other specialist consultants as required to perform all of the services of work necessary to originate, design, plan for, inspect and generally administer work on the proposed development;
  4. present to The Developer in properly documented form the result of all and any feasibility studies, all working drawings, recommendations and cost and time estimates relating to the proposed development;
  5. seek to obtain all and any approvals not already held from any local, statutory or other governmental authority having jurisdiction over or relating to the proposed development;
  6. manage and control the proposed development schedule to ensure that all work therein is carried out and completed in proper sequence;
  7. obtain the advice of the architect and specialist consultants specified in Item 6 of the Schedule on all matters which they may be competent to advise on in relation to the proposed development; and
  8. generally exercise, in conjunction with builder specified in Item 7 of the Schedule (“the Builder”), and to the point of practical completion and commissioning of the proposed development all those powers that The Developer might itself exercise in relation to the proposed development.
  1. The Manager’s duties and obligations included employing or engaging responsible and skilled personnel as agents; keeping proper books of account and records of all financial transactions relating to the proposed development and making them available to the Developer; using reasonable endeavours to obtain approvals in connection with the development; regularly reporting to the Developer; and providing other services and assistance to the developer “whether or not in connection with the proposed development” at the rate specified in item 9 of the Schedule which was stated to be “by negotiation” (clause 4).
  2. Clause 3 stated Westpoint Corporation was entitled to charge the Developer a management fee as Manager for the “proposed development” of no more than the amount specified in item 8 of the Schedule. The management fee is stated in item 8 to be:
    Two (2) percent of project costs, payable as follows:

    - 0.5 percent on issue of development approval

    - 0.5 percent on commencement of construction

    - 0.5 percent on practical completion

    - 0.5 percent on settlement of all sales

    Plus

    Success fee of two (2) percent of project value to achieve budgeted development approval payable on this approval.

Has Bayshore established it made creditable acquisitions?

  1. For reasons we will now explain, there is too much uncertainty raised by the evidence for us to be satisfied about what was supplied, and the value of anything supplied, such that we do not think Bayshore has succeeded in establishing it made creditable acquisitions.

What was supplied by Westpoint Constructions?

  1. The different versions of the building contract raise doubts about which building contract was the actual building contract. This is significant because there is a large disparity (in the amount of $40,000,000) in the value of what was proposed to be supplied. As we outlined above, the Second Version had a contract price of $17,500,000.00, but the Third Version had a contract price of $57,500,000.00. Mr Carey’s evidence was that the building contract was worth $65,000,000 but it had been split into two contracts for the two stages of the project with $17,500,000.00 being for Stage 2. We outline this evidence in more detail below.
  2. The total value is not the only difference between the versions of the contract. Clause 3 of the First Version and the Third Version states there would be 306 residential apartments, while the same clause in the Second Version refers to 126 residential apartments.
  3. More problematic, as was conceded by Mr Carey and Mr Nairn, is that the First Version and the Third Version appear to have been changed by someone altering the number of residential apartments to the increased number of 306 and by deleting the words “known collectively as “Stage 2” of the Project”. Under cross-examination, Mr Carey was asked about the inconsistencies in the documentation. He agreed the Third Version had been changed in this manner. He denied he changed the document and denied it was a deliberate fabrication (transcript/329-331 and 336). Mr Carey could not, however, explain the existence of the Third Version of the building contract (transcript/336-337).
  4. Mr Nairn, who annexed the Third Version of the building contract to his witness statement, was also asked about the inconsistencies between the Second Version and the Third Version. He could not recall where he obtained the Third Version of the building contract that he annexed to his affidavit. He did not recall obtaining it from searching the compactus in the Westpoint offices where signed and sealed documents were filed (transcript/371). Mr Nairn agreed clause 3 in the Second Version and the Third Version were different. He did not have any explanation for the differences. He also denied changing the document (transcript/376).
  5. We agree at least one of the documents appears to have been changed, but we do not have sufficient evidence to identify who made the change, much less why and when the change was made. However, the fact changes were undoubtedly made calls into question the integrity of the documents that are said to evidence what was supplied by Westpoint Constructions and its value. At a minimum, there is doubt over which version of the contract was in force.
  6. In his witness statements, Mr Carey gave detailed evidence about the contract sum for the Bayshore project. In his statement dated 5 July 2021, he stated $17,500,000.00 was not the correct total contract sum. In support of his evidence that the actual contract sum for the Bayshore project was significantly higher than a total of $17,500,000.00, Mr Carey referred to a “Recommendation of Progress Payment No.11” dated 21 December 2005 by ACSM Pty Ltd, the project manager appointed pursuant to the building contract and named at Item 16 of Annexure Part A. This document referred to a construction budget of $86,764,867 (A5, para [10(c)] and [18], TB030.8, pdf 3), an original contract sum of $65,000,000.00, with an adjusted contract sum of $86,431,534.00 after approved variations of $21,431,534.00 were included (TB 030.8, pdf 11). However, in his witness statement, Mr Carey relied upon the Third Version of the building contract that had a contract sum of $57,500,000 (A5, para [10(c)]).
  7. In his statement dated 1 November 2021, Mr Carey stated the building contract was first prepared in 2002. That contract did not contain a contract sum, but Mr Carey recalled the contract price was to be $57,500,000, based on a preliminary cost estimate prepared by quantity surveyors (A10, para [5]). We note the amount of this estimate was $59,894,499 (TB 142.2, pdf 2).
  8. Mr Carey’s evidence was that the financier, a private lender called MFS, engaged a company called Project Planning and Management Pty Ltd to provide independent advice on the building cost. The estimate provided was $65,772,782 (TB 142.3, pdf 1). Based on that estimate, Mr Carey’s evidence was that the contract price was agreed to be $65,000,000.00 (A10, para [6]).
  9. Mr Carey said MFS had limits on the amounts it would lend, so the contract was split into two building contracts to accommodate the lender (A5, para [10(c)]). These were the building contract for $47,500,000 for Stage 1 and another building contract for $17,500,000 for Stage 2, with the total contract sum for the two projects being $65,000,000.00. This is consistent with the Second Version of the building contract being the contract that was produced by ASIC under summons.
  10. The evidence as to which contract was the correct one is contradictory. Mr Carey initially relied upon the Second Version of the building contract with a contract sum of $57,500,000. We note this was supported by a document titled ‘Valuation summary prepared by CB Richard Ellis for MFS Structured Finance Pty Ltd’ dated 27 April 2005 (TB 041.3) which refers to a building contract for $57,500,000 and there being 306 apartments in total for Stage 1 and Stage 2. Specifically (TB 041.3, pdf 3), this valuation states:
    In addition we have been provided with a building contract for the total building work between Bayshore Port Melbourne Pty Ltd and Westpoint Constructions Pty Ltd for $57,500,000. There are approved variations of $21,431,534.50.
  11. Given the contradictions in the versions of the building contracts and the change in Mr Carey’s evidence over time, in our view, the contract sum for the project is unclear. That lack of clarity is exacerbated by the changing of clause 3 by unknown persons for unknown reasons, and Mr Carey’s inability to explain the existence of the Third Version of the building contract.
  12. If we accept Mr Carey’s evidence that the building contract was split into two Stages with the Second Version being the applicable contract for Stage 2, there is also doubt as to the work that was completed under that building contract. Mr Carey’s statement dated 17 December 2020 (which attached the First Version) says the building contract was entered into in February 2004 (A4, para [19(a)]). He said the work was for the design, commissioning, and completion of 306 residential apartments. However, he agreed during cross-examination that the building contract is dated 2005. His evidence was that “there was substantial building ... there was substantial substructure work occurring in 2004” (transcript/326). The Commissioner points out the building contract is dated 2005 but there is no other evidence as to when it was entered into apart from Mr Carey’s confusing account (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [471]). We agree it is not possible to determine whether the building contract had been entered into when any work was carried out. We should add that if the contract – whichever one was in force – had been stamped in the ordinary course, some of this confusion might have been avoided as we would at least have clear evidence of the date of the operative document.
  13. There is a final issue that muddies the evidentiary waters. As we outlined above, all three versions of the building contract have a page titled, “Cooling Off Notice to the Principal” which contains a “Notice that Contract is Ended”. In these notices, Mr Carey and Mr Rundle have signed under the following statement that is addressed “To [the] (Contractor)”: “I/We give notice under our Contract with you that the Contract is ended. Please refund the deposit less $100 and any out-of-pocket expenses incurred by you which I have previously approved”. There is also a handwritten annotation at the top right-hand corner of that page which states: “18/2/05 accepted by Castle Partners, AAR, MFS”.
  14. Mr Carey’s evidence about this in his witness statement dated 5 July 2021 was that it is mandatory under Victorian legislation to have a cooling off period accompanying a building contract. His evidence was that he signed as an acknowledgment that the cooling off period existed but that no notice of termination was served, and that Westpoint Constructions carried out work under the building contract (A5, para [10(a)]). That evidence does not sit comfortably with the fact Mr Carey (and Mr Rundle), an experienced Company Director and property developer, have signed on the part of the page that gives notice that the contract is ended. This further adds to our doubt about which (if any) of these contracts was operative.
  15. After considering the above evidence, we find that although the Applicant is relying on the Stage 1 building contract and the Second Version of the building contract (and not the First Version and the Third Version which have been changed), there are too many doubts raised by the evidence concerning these building contracts for us to be satisfied Bayshore has met its burden. We are not satisfied there is sufficient evidence to substantiate the transactions which are recorded in the tax invoices. There is conflicting evidence as to the contract sum and the number of apartments. It is also unclear whether some of the work was carried out before whichever version of the building contract was entered into, and whether notice was given that the building contract had ended.

What was supplied by Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem?

  1. The Commissioner submitted the preconditions for Westpoint Realty becoming entitled to remuneration under the Agency Agreement were not satisfied with respect to the services for which the tax invoice purportedly charged (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [500]).
  2. As we have outlined above, Bayshore relies on the project cost summary dated 20 December 2005 (TB 030.9), which states the ‘Commission – Apartments’ to be $4,873,631. Mr Carey gave evidence about how this commission was calculated (A5, para [22(a)]. He referred to a ‘Bayshore Status Report’ dated 20 December 2005 which showed the value of contracts exchanged for the apartments to be a total of $129,432,100 and $9,043,400 for retail contracts exchanged. Mr Carey’s evidence was that (A5, para [22(a)]:
    The commission set out in the tax invoice was for a part of the commission payable to WPR [Westpoint Realty], which was $5,539,020 based on the contracts exchanged to date. The balance of the commission was to be recovered in later months.
  3. However, as the Commissioner points out (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [503]), this is inconsistent with the project costs summary which states the commission is for the apartments.
  4. A further problem for Bayshore is that it started entering into contracts of sale for the Bayshore project as far back as 2001. Therefore, Bayshore was entering into contracts of sale over a period of several years before the tax invoice of December 2005, and before the Agency Agreement was entered into on 1 August 2003. Despite this, Bayshore relies upon the Agency Agreement to authorise commissions in relation to contracts of sale exchanged prior to the Agency Agreement being entered into (transcript/315-316). This raises doubt as to whether those commissions had previously been charged. Mr Carey’s evidence was that, from his own knowledge, they had not been charged previously and that the selling commission in the project cost summary represented four percent of the contract price of all properties in the development (A5, para [22(a)]; transcript/316). There is no documentary evidence to substantiate this. Mr Carey gave the following explanation for the temporal gap (transcript/316):
    MR DAVIES: But why was it that you waited until December 2005 to charge remuneration that had – if it had been enlivened by the agency agreement, had been enlivened over time from at least 2003 onwards?

    MR CAREY: Well, there's a number of factors. One was that once these contracts were in place, they've got to be administered. So a lot of these deposits were by way of deposit bonds and bank guarantees that had to be administered, they had to be updated, they had to be renewed, there was a lot of administration with the contracts. So that was an ongoing process. And as I said ... as well, one of the factors was that I was managing the timing when income was brought to account.

    MR DAVIES: So you though [sic] that it was appropriate to bring this income to account in December 2005?

    MR CAREY: Well, I thought it was appropriate and so did KPMG who were auditing the group.

    MR DAVIES: And you had not thought that it was appropriate to bring the income to account, as and when the entitlement arose from 2003 onwards?

    MR CAREY: No.

  5. Overall, we have difficulty accepting Mr Carey’s explanation that commissions owed before December 2005 and before the Agency Agreement was entered into were not charged. There was no obvious commercial explanation for deferring commissions which were due and payable in previous income periods for up to four years.
  6. Also, as the Commissioner points out (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [507]), the charging of commission under clause 5.2 of the Agency Agreement was when a deposit, or a deposit power bond was paid by the purchaser. There is no evidence regarding if, and when, deposits were paid and received.
  7. With respect to marketing and legal costs, Bayshore relies on the Agency Agreement to demonstrate Westpoint Realty’s entitlement to charge marketing costs. The Commissioner submitted there was no entitlement to recover those costs under the Agency Agreement (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [513]). We agree with that analysis, for reasons we will now explain.
  8. As we outlined above, the Agent’s Remuneration is governed by clause 5 of the Agency Agreement. Clause 5.1 provides for the Agent to be paid commission on the sale of each property calculated at the rate in Annexure B. The amount of commission in Annexure B is four percent of the contract price of the Property. As we have also mentioned, the entitlement to invoice for the commission arises under clause 5.2 when a deposit has been paid by the purchaser or a Deposit Power Bond (not defined) is received (TB 025.10, pdf 6-7 and 14). There is no other provision in the Agency Agreement that would permit additional marketing expenses to be charged. Mr Carey agreed Annexure B says nothing about reimbursement for marketing expenses (transcript/314-315). He explained he understood the agent was to carry out marketing and to be reimbursed for the cost of doing so (transcript/314). He stated (transcript/315):
    Well, I was a party to the agreement. I understood what that agreement meant. And that’s the basis on which I acted.
  9. When asked why he did not vary the agreement to reflect that marketing and legal costs could be charged, Mr Carey stated (transcript/315):
    It didn’t enter my mind, because it was plain, when I made the agreement, that Westpoint Realty was to carry out the marketing, incur the cost, and it would be reimbursed by Bayshore. As with all the other projects.
  10. However, the problem remains that Bayshore relies upon the Agency Agreement to substantiate the marketing and legal costs, and yet there is no express term of that agreement which would entitle Westpoint Realty to charge the $547,688 of marketing costs shown in the project cost summary (TB 030.9).
  11. Additionally, we are unsure as to how the amounts set out for marketing and legal costs stated in the project cost summary were reached. We note Mr Carey attempted to provide further details about the nature of the marketing work he says was undertaken in his witness statement dated 5 July 2021 (A5, paras [52]-[57]). Mr Carey said the nature of the marketing work was similar to Cinema City and referred to the Marketing & Sales Plan for that project (A5, para [53]; TB 030.37).
  12. He explained significant costs were incurred for telemarketing as part of the Bayshore project (A5, para [55] and [57]). For example, a document titled ‘Telemarketing Wages and Commissions – VIC’ spreadsheet shows telemarketing costs of $5,897 for the period 1 June 2005 to 14 June 2005 (TB 030.15, pdf 393). Another such spreadsheet shows costs of $3,026.00 for the period 23 May to 31 May 2005 (TB 030.15, pdf 394). Another spreadsheet for the period 8 November 2003 to 21 November 2003 shows telemarketing wages of more than $33,000 (although the exact amount is unclear due to the quality of the copy) (TB 030.15, pdf 395-396). A telemarketing report dated 9 February 2002 shows a total expenditure to date of $76,765.24, with some of the costs comprising that amount having been paid in full (TB 030.15, pdf 397).
  13. Mr Carey also referred to various brochures and other materials to demonstrate marketing activity (A5, para [56]). These materials included an ‘Introducer Pack’ prepared by the project’s architects that included floorplans of the development (TB 030.15, pdf 192); documentation suggesting a display apartment that was open seven days a week (pdf 205); an investor update published in April 2004 (pdf 212); other brochures and advertising materials (starting from pdf 216 to 353); a proposed public relations campaign in July 2004 (pdf 353); and a marketing meeting on 9 December 2005 (pdf 362), of which Mr Carey said there had been regular meetings.
  14. Despite the welter of materials, the documents before us do not show how the very specific amounts set out in the project cost summary were calculated. As we stated above, the evidence of Ms Rigg was that marketing work took place, but she was not aware of how the amounts were arrived at (transcript/382-383). Some of the marketing documents referred to by Mr Carey pre-date the Agency Agreement, and as we have stated, the Agency Agreement does not contain an express provision under which these costs could have been charged. We continue to be uncertain as to whether these amounts, which would have been incurred from at least 2001, were charged in previous tax periods when they fell due.

What was supplied by Westpoint Corporation?

  1. The Commissioner argued the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Corporation in December 2005 does not accord with the provisions of the DM Agreement dated 14 July 2004 (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [523]).
  2. In his witness statement dated 17 December 2020, Mr Carey said Westpoint Corporation issued a tax invoice to Bayshore for “professional fees rendered in respect of the Bayshore project” on 20 December 2005. Mr Carey said these fees were authorised by the terms of the DM Agreement whereby Westpoint Corporation was engaged to provide management services for the project (A4, para [28]).
  3. In his witness statement dated 5 July 2021, (A5, para [26]), Mr Carey stated the amount in the invoice was reflected in a Development Management Cost Summary. This was a document titled ‘Westpoint Corporation – Development Management Cost Summary’ dated 20 December 2005 for the ‘Bayshore Apartments’ project for a total of $6,541,225 (excluding GST) (TB 030.11). That document specified amounts for:
    (a) Item 1.1 – a $600,000 fee for “Issue of development approval”, calculated at 0.5% of $120,000,000.

    (b) Item 1.2 – a $600,000 fee for “Commencement of construction”, calculated at 0.5% of $120,000,000.

    (c) Item 2.1 – a $2,700,000 fee for “Achieving budgeted development approval”, calculated at 2% of $135,000,000.

    (d) Item 3.1 – a $1,820,000 fee for “Day to day management of the integrated property development process for this project from 01/07/2000 to 20/12/2005”.

    (e) Item 4.1 – an $821,225 fee for “Day to day management of marketing and selling process for this project from 01/07/2000 to 20/12/2005”.

  4. Our initial observation is that clause 3 and item 8 of the Schedule contemplate the categories of fees charged under items 1.1, 1.2 and 2.1.
  5. As with the Agency Agreement, there are doubts raised by the tax invoice being issued in December 2005 in circumstances where the entitlement to payment arose prior to that time. The date of the DM Agreement was 14 July 2004. Under cross-examination, Mr Carey agreed development approval had been given (although he said there was a subsequent amended approval), meaning the 0.5 percent fee (in item 1.1) was payable from 14 July 2004. He also agreed construction had commenced by that date, meaning the further 0.5 percent fee (in item 1.2) was payable from 14 July 2004 too. Mr Carey further agreed that by the end of 2004, the two percent fee in item 2.1 also would have been payable (transcript/317).
  6. Mr Carey confirmed those amounts were not charged for until December 2005, which we note was approximately eighteen months to a year after they were payable. Mr Carey referred to managing the timing of the income due to the Westpoint group gearing up for an initial public offering (‘IPO’). His evidence was (transcript/317):
    MR DAVIES: And again your explanation for the delay in charging in relation to those entitlements, what’s that?

    MR CAREY: Well, I suppose I was running the overall project and incurring the cost and, you know, one of the factors was, to some extent, match the cost with the – as the revenue was being produced, and also the issue that I raised before about managing the timing of income arising in accordance with a plan for the IPO.

  7. For the same reasons as we gave with respect to Westpoint Realty and the Agency Agreement above, we are not satisfied these amounts were not charged earlier when liability to charge those commissions arose. While Mr Carey referred generally to the possibility of an IPO, he did not offer any convincing commercial explanation for the delay.
  8. Mr Carey was also asked which part of the DM Agreement authorised the payment of additional fees for “day to day management”, as per items 3.1 and 4.1. Mr Carey said those amounts were authorised by item 9 of the Schedule (transcript/318), which provided for a ‘Rate of Remuneration For extra Services to be “By negotiation” (TB 025.11, pdf 7). He confirmed the contemplated negotiation took place with himself and he made the decision alone (transcript/318). Other than the sums stated in items 3.1 and 4.1, there is no documentation before us to support what was negotiated or which confirms anything negotiated was meant to operate retrospectively.
  9. Items 3.1 and 4.1 of the cost summary refer to “day to day management” of the property development and marketing occurring from 1 July 2000. However, the DM Agreement is not expressed to be retrospective. The commencement date is the date of the agreement, which is 14 July 2004. Mr Carey was asked under cross-examination whether the DM Agreement contemplated charging for work completed prior to the agreement commencing. The following exchange is relevant (transcript/318-319):
    MR DAVIES: What provision are you relying upon in the agreement behind tab 25.11 to demonstrate the entitlement of Corporation to work carried out before that agreement was entered into?

    MR CAREY: Because from the time that we purchased this property, I had agreed between Westpoint Corporation and Bayshore – because Bayshore didn’t have any employees so it couldn't actually do anything – so I engaged or came to an agreement with Westpoint Corporation that it would manage the project from that point in time.

    MR DAVIES: So in order to support the charge for the work carried out by Corporation prior to July 2004, you’re relying upon a different agreement to the one set out in 25.11?

    MR CAREY: Well, the formal agreement that was put in place is consistent with the agreement that I came to when we first purchased the property.

    MR DAVIES: So are you relying upon an agreement other than the one that’s set out – other than the written agreement of 14 July 2004, or are you not relying upon an agreement other than in the written agreement?

    MR CAREY: I’m relying on both agreements. The agreement that I made between Bayshore and Westpoint Corporation when we purchased the property in 2000 which then became the formal agreement in 2003.

    MR DAVIES: That prior agreement, was that an agreement that – had that been an agreement that had been committed to writing?

    MR CAREY: I hadn’t really turned my mind to this. I think there may have been a letter of engagement. There wasn’t a formal agreement but I haven’t really searched for that. It was certainly the case from the time we purchased the property that a significant number of Westpoint Corporation employees were engaged in an [sic] wide range of management services to give rise to this project because Bayshore had no employees.

    MR DAVIES: Mr Carey, what evidence do we have that demonstrates that for the work carried out under this earlier agreement that you saw was existence, hadn’t been charged for and paid for before July 2004?

    MR CAREY: Well, because I ran the project on a day to day basis and I also managed Westpoint Corporation on a day to day basis and I know of my own knowledge that there were no invoices issued for this work.

    MR DAVIES: So you're saying, Mr Carey, that from as early as 2001 in relation to this development, there was an agreement on foot whereby [Westpoint] Corporation would carry out work for Bayshore for remuneration and it charged for that work carried out, as and from 2001 onwards, was not made until December 2005?

    MR CAREY: Yes.

  10. We do not have a copy of the additional, earlier agreement that Mr Carey referred to, and relies upon, although Mr Carey did concede “There wasn’t a formal agreement”. It is unsatisfactory that the existence of this agreement was only disclosed during cross-examination. We note the submission of Bayshore that “The arrangement between WPCorp [Westpoint Corporation] and Bayshore was not formally documented until 2003” (Applicant’s closing submissions in reply, para [122(d)]). This appears to be a reference to the document referred to during cross-examination which has not been filed in these proceedings (transcript/318). Also, as the question from Mr Davies suggests, without seeing that additional agreement and knowing its precise terms, it is difficult for us to ascertain whether work carried out under the earlier agreement had already been invoiced. It is plausible that it was, given the DM Agreement commenced on 14 July 2004 and was not expressed to operate retrospectively.
  11. Bayshore submitted “it is clear from the material that WPCorp [Westpoint Corporation] carried out work with respect to the project” (Applicant’s closing submissions in reply, para [122]). Bayshore refers to documents including two facsimile communications from the designer and architects in September and October 2001 which refer to Westpoint Corporation as the “client” (TB 030.15, pdf 5-6 and 7-12). We also note other documents such as plans titled ‘Staging Drawings Westpoint Corporation’ prepared by the architects for the project (pdf 92) which indicates Westpoint Corporation’s involvement. We do not think it is material that documents which were referred to in support of Westpoint Corporation’s involvement at that time related to a different property development with a similar name, ‘Bayview’ (see, for example pdf 105 and 107). Westpoint Corporation may have been working on the project from 2001, but this does not address our concerns. The DM Agreement was not retrospective, we do not have a copy of the other agreement Mr Carey referred to under cross-examination, and we do not know if the work was previously invoiced, given the delay which was not satisfactorily explained.

Issues regarding the payment of the tax invoices via set-off

  1. There are similar issues regarding the payment of the tax invoices by way of a mutual set-off of book entries as there were for the other applications.
  2. Like the other applications, Bayshore relies upon unstamped loan agreements and drawdown notices to substantiate a mutual set-off in which payments of the tax invoices were offset against the loans:
    (a) A loan agreement dated 1 July 2002 between Bayshore Company as Borrower and Westpoint Corporation as Lender for a commitment of $35,000,000 (TB 030.2).

There is no evidence of any drawdown by Bayshore Company under this agreement.

(b) A loan agreement dated 1 July 2003 between Westpoint Corporation as Borrower and Bayshore Company as Lender for a commitment of $20,000,000 (TB 030.6).

On 20 December 2005 Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Bayshore wished to draw $7,195,347 on 20 December 2005 under the loan agreement (TB 030.14). This was the GST inclusive amount of the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Corporation on the same date.

(c) A loan agreement dated 1 July 2003 between Westpoint Constructions as Borrower and Bayshore Company as Lender for a commitment of $30,000,000 (TB 030.4).

On 20 December 2005 Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Westpoint Constructions wished to draw $7,840,065 on 20 December 2005 under the loan agreement (TB 030.12). This was the GST inclusive amount of the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Constructions on the same date.

(d) A loan agreement dated 1 July 2003 between Westpoint Realty as Borrower and Bayshore Company as Lender for a commitment of $10,000,000 (TB 030.5).

Again, on 20 December 2005, Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Westpoint Realty wished to draw $5,462,314 on 20 December 2005 under the loan agreement (TB 030.13). This was the GST inclusive amount of the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Realty on the same date.

  1. Each of these loan agreements was signed by Mr Carey as Director and Mr Rundle as Company Secretary.
  2. Again, like the other applications, Bayshore relies upon an incomplete handwritten journal entry to substantiate that a set-off occurred (TB 025.15, pdf 2). The entity is stated as “(51) Bayshore Port Melb”. Like the other applications, the “journal number”, “date prepared”, “prepared by”, “entered by”, “authorised by” and “date entered” are blank. The “Journal Details” box had been completed to state, “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices”.
  3. Bayshore also relies on a trial balance as at 31 December 2005 to show the debiting and crediting of the loan amounts to pay the invoices (TB 025.15, pdf 1). That trial balance shows:
    (a) A debit of $18,634,297 for “WIP – Bayshore”, which Bayshore submits comprise the GST exclusive amounts payable by Bayshore in respect of the tax invoices dated 20 December 2005 (opening submissions, para [28(a)(i)] of Annexure 1 Bayshore).

    (b) A debit of $1,863,429 for “GST Paid”, which Bayshore submits is the GST payable under the tax invoices (opening submissions, para [28(a)(ii)] of Annexure 1 Bayshore).

    (c) A credit of $7,195,347 for “Loan Westpoint Corporation P/L”, which Bayshore describes as the reduction of the loan in the amount necessary to pay the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Corporation (opening submissions, para [28(a)(iii)] of Annexure 1 Bayshore).

    (d) A credit of $7,840,065 for “Loan Westpoint Constructions P/L”, which Bayshore describes as the reduction of the loan in the amount necessary to pay the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Constructions (opening submissions, para [28(a)(iv)] of Annexure 1 Bayshore).

    (e) A credit of $5,462,314 for “Loan – Westgem Unit Trust”, which Bayshore describes as the reduction of the loan in the amount necessary to pay the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Realty (opening submissions, para [28(a)(v)] of Annexure 1 Bayshore).

  4. Mr Nairn was asked about the trial balance at the hearing, including about the trial balance which demonstrated that after the credit of $7,195,347 for “Loan Westpoint Corporation P/L”, there was a remaining year-to-date debt balance of $8,896,195.87 (TB 025.15, pdf 1). The following evidence from Mr Nairn suggests the trial balance is not reliable (transcript/369):
    MR DAVIES: Can I ask you to have a look at the document 25-15 which is a trial balance of one of the special purpose vehicles; this one's Bayshore or Melbourne trust. ...

    To clarify this, on the face of it, it’s a trial balance of Bayshore or Melbourne trust. I take it you weren't involved in the preparation of this document?

    MR NAIRN: December 2005 I was the general manager of financial accounting. This would've been produced by accounting staff employed by the group who probably sat underneath me, so, yes.

    MR DAVIES: Can I take your eye to about five or six items down the column. There's an item for the loan with the Westpoint Corporation Pty Ltd?---

    MR NAIRN: Yes.

    MR DAVIES: And do you see there that that records Westpoint Corporation as owing $8.8 million to special purpose vehicle?

    MR NAIRN: Yes, it does.

    MR DAVIES: Does that come as a surprise to you?---

    MR NAIRN: Yes, it does, it would indicate to me that this is a preliminary trial balance and that the accounting for December 2005 is incomplete. That's what it would suggest to me. Whether that's true or not I can't say.

    MR DAVIES: On the face of it though – - -?

    MR NAIRN: On the face of it, yes.

    MR DAVIES: On the face of it that the veracity, or accuracy of that document would require further investigation before one could accept that as a reliable – - -?

    MR NAIRN: Yes, I would agree with that comment.

  5. While the reliability of this trial balance has been called into question, there are no other financial records of Bayshore before us, save for the tax invoices and the incomplete journal entry.
  6. Also, as with the other applications, there are no corresponding accounts for Westpoint Corporation, Westpoint Constructions or Westpoint Realty AFT Westgem that evidence a mutual set-off, nor any evidence that these companies accepted the mutual set-off, as suggested in GSTD 2004/4, by way of any express or implied agreement.
  7. As with the other applications, the timing of the tax invoices in December 2005 is questionable given the Westpoint group was on the verge of collapse. Winding up proceedings against Westpoint Corporation were commenced in the Federal Court on 19 December 2005 (TB 031.4). Receiver managers and an administrator were appointed on 24 January 2006. A liquidator was subsequently appointed on 16 February 2006 (TB 012.44, pdf 5). A receiver was appointed to Westpoint Constructions on 2 February 2006, followed by a court appointed liquidator on 22 February 2006 (TB 012.36, pdf 5). On 20 April 2006 receivers and managers were appointed to Westpoint Realty, followed by the appointment of liquidators on 13 April 2007 (TB 012.38, pdf 6).
  8. Like the other applications before us, the reports as to affairs made by the receivers and managers and liquidators are inconsistent with the inter-company debts and mutual set-offs alleged by Bayshore.
  9. A report as to affairs as at 8 February 2006 prepared by the receiver and manager of the Bayshore Company stated Westpoint Corporation was an unsecured creditor for the amount of $8,039,517. Westpoint Realty and Westpoint Constructions were not listed as creditors. The report as to affairs also lists a fixed and floating charge (TB 031.14) to a creditor called Bayshore Mezzanine Pty Ltd with the amount of $36,644,470 owing to them (TB 031.15, pdf 8). The report as to affairs appears to have been certified by Mr Carey on 1 February 2006 because his name is printed and the certification is signed (TB 031.15, pdf 9). However, Mr Carey’s evidence at the hearing was that this was not his handwriting and that he believed the certification had been signed by someone else. He thought his signature may have been put on the form electronically (transcript/339). We also note there is a letter dated 7 February 2006 to ASIC from the receiver and manager which states they “are unable to certify the accuracy of the information” in the report as to affairs (TB 031.15, pdf 10). Mr Carey stated in his evidence that he thought this qualification was “very important” because the same firm of receivers were appointed to Westpoint Corporation (transcript/340 -341). Thus, Bayshore argued the fact the receiver and manager stated he could not verify the accuracy of the information in this Bayshore Company report as to affairs meant the receiver and manager would not have been able to verify the accuracy of the financial information for Westpoint Corporation (Applicant’s closing submissions in reply, para [124(a)]).
  10. A subsequent report as to affairs dated 21 February 2006 by the same receiver and manager listed the Bayshore Mezzanine Pty Ltd fixed and floating charge as well as three others (TB 031.16, pdf 7). There was a substantial change from the 8 February 2006 report as to affairs in the amount owing to Westpoint Constructions which is only listed as an unsecured creditor for $262,294 (TB 031.16, pdf 8). There is no letter or other notation indicating the receiver and manager had any concerns about the accuracy of the information in this report as to affairs.
  11. A document titled ‘presentation of accounts and statements’ dated 22 December 2005 prepared by the receivers and managers shows that in the period 22 December 2005 to 21 June 2006, $28,240,718.14 in pre-appointment construction costs were paid directly to the persons who undertook the work such as payments to subcontractors (TB 031.17, pdf 5 and 7). We note Bayshore submitted this was incorrect because direct payments were inconsistent with Mr Nairn’s evidence which (in summary) was that the work was carried out by Westpoint Constructions under a building contract and invoiced by Westpoint Constructions to Bayshore and paid by way of reductions in inter-company loans. However, there are approximately eleven pages of these payments (TB 031.17, pdf 8-19) which we think lends weight to the Commissioner’s submission that the fact of the receivers and managers paying these pre-appointment costs directly was inconsistent with Westpoint Constructions having incurred those costs. We infer Bayshore incurred them directly.
  12. A report by administrators of Westpoint Realty did not list Bayshore as one of the related entities to whom it owed money under outstanding related party loans (TB 006.47, pdf 17).
  13. The report as to affairs for Westpoint Constructions dated 11 August 2006 that was verified by Mr Rundle on 9 August 2006 (TB 031.A2, pdf 1-2) listed the Bayshore Trust as being owed $4,767,888 as at 22 February 2006 (pdf 5 and 7). That is different to the amount showing in the ‘Trial Balance as at 31 December 2005’ as being owed under the loan to Westpoint Constructions which was $9,865,932.22 (TB 025.15, pdf 1).
  14. The report as to affairs dated 27 February 2006 for Westpoint Corporation, certified by Mr Rundle and Mr Nairn on 23 February 2006, stated Westpoint Corporation owed the sum of $8,896,195.22 to “Loan Bayshore PM Trust” (TB 031.A1, pdf 1, 5, 10). We note this figure is substantially similar to the trial balance which shows an amount of $8,896,195.87 being owed under the loan (TB 025.15, pdf 1).
  15. Apart from this one exception, we find these reports are inconsistent with the amounts of inter-company debts and mutual set-offs alleged by Bayshore.
  16. As in the other applications, the GST reporting by the receivers and managers of Bayshore, Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty are inconsistent with the input tax credit entitlements Bayshore is claiming given:
    (a) Westpoint Corporation did not lodge a BAS for December 2005 (TB 019.5, pdf 1).

    (b) As we mentioned above, on 18 January 2006, the receivers and managers lodged a BAS for the Bayshore Trust for the December 2005 tax period claiming non-capital purchases of $445,327 and $40,484 of input tax credits (TB 025.3; 025.19).

    (c) Westpoint Constructions lodged a BAS with a net amount of nil and no taxable supplies for the December 2005 tax period on 2 March 2006 (TB 012.32).

    (d) Westpoint Realty lodged a BAS for the period 1 October 2005 to 31 December 2005 on 27 February 2006 which reported total sales of $302,711 and non-capital purchases of $785,046 (TB 012.34).

  17. We agree with the Commissioner’s submission that these BASs do not show taxable supplies which correspond with the creditable acquisitions Bayshore claims to have made.
  18. The Commissioner makes a similar submission here to the one it made in relation to Silkchime, which is that Westpoint Realty’s BAS, lodged on 27 February 2006 occurred before the receiver and manager was appointed to Westpoint Realty on 20 April 2006 (TB 012.38, pdf 1). If we accept Mr Carey’s evidence that the books of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty were seized by ASIC in January 2006, there is still a lack of any corroborating evidence from the perspective of Westpoint Corporation, Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty that they made taxable supplies to Bayshore.

Conclusion for Bayshore

  1. In summary, similar issues and concerns arose for Bayshore as for Cinema City. For the reasons we have outlined above, we find Bayshore has not established on the evidence that it made creditable acquisitions from Westpoint Constructions, Westpoint Realty and Westpoint Corporation. Those reasons included:
    (a) Doubt as to whether Heca Nominees was validly appointed as an additional trustee to the Bayshore Trust. None of the trust assets were ever transferred into the joint name, the alleged trustees never acted jointly, Heca Nominees did nothing as a trustee other than to notify and pursue GST claims with the Commissioner, and there is no evidence Heca Nominees was recognised as a trustee by any third party.

    (b) There is doubt as to whether the Bayshore Trust continued to exist after the Bayshore Company was deregistered. For example, there are no books of account or other records that show the trust fund continued to hold trust property and to exist.

    (c) With respect to whether taxable supplies were made to Bayshore by Westpoint Constructions, the three different versions of the building contract raise doubts about which building contract was the actual building contract. Further doubt is created by the First and Third Versions having been changed to alter the number of residential apartments, to delete reference to Stage 2, and to insert a higher contract sum. We found the evidence as to which building contract was the correct one to be confusing and contradictory. This meant the contract sum for the Project and the number of apartments were unclear to us. Doubts were also raised about the actual work that was completed under the building contract, given Mr Carey’s evidence that substantial work was carried out before the contract was entered into. The signed ‘Cooling Off Notice to the Principal’ clause in all three versions of the building contract also suggests those building contracts had ended. Overall, there is too much doubt raised by this evidence for us to be satisfied as to what was supplied by Westpoint Constructions.

    (d) With respect to any taxable supplies being made by Westpoint Realty, there were too many inconsistencies and doubts raised by the documentation which meant we could not be satisfied about what was supplied under the Agency Agreement or whether the commissions charged, some of which would have been in relation to contracts of sale that predated the Agency Agreement, had been charged previously. We were not satisfied by Mr Carey’s explanation that commissions were deferred for up to four years instead of being invoiced when they fell due. We also found there was doubt about whether additional marketing expenses could be charged under the Agency Agreement in the absence of an express term. Despite Mr Carey’s evidence about the nature of the marketing activities, including significant costs for telemarketing, we found the evidence and documents before us did not show us how the specific amounts set out in the project cost summary were calculated. There is simply too much doubt as to what exactly the creditable acquisitions were, when they were made, and if they were already charged for in previous tax periods.

    (e) The evidence also raises doubt about what was supplied by Westpoint Corporation under the DM Agreement.
