The tax invoice was issued in December 2005, and yet Mr Carey agreed entitlement to the commissions for the first two out of the five items in the Development Management Cost Summary were payable from 14 July 2004, and the third by the end of 2004. We were not satisfied these amounts were not charged earlier when they fell due. We also found the remaining two fees for day-to-day management were problematic, particularly when during Mr Carey’s cross-examination, he sought to rely upon another agreement that was not before us. He also sought to rely on a negotiation he effectively had with himself that these fees could be charged under the DM Agreement which was not documented. Even if Westpoint Corporation may have been working on the project from 2001, the DM Agreement, which came into effect on 14 July 2004 was not retrospective, and we do not have a copy of the other agreement that Mr Carey sought to rely upon.

    (f) There are similar issues with the evidence concerning set-off that we found for the other applications. These included incomplete journal entries, a lack of evidence as to whether there was a mutual set-off or that the related companies agreed to the set-off arrangement, a lack of evidence of mutual book entries, and inconsistent reports as to affairs from the receivers and managers and liquidators of Westpoint Constructions, Westpoint Realty and Westpoint Corporation.

    (g) Similarly, the failure to stamp key documents including the BPM Additional Trustee Deed, the Ratification Deed, the various versions of the building contract, the Agency Agreement and the DM Agreement raise doubts about provenance, adding to our overall concerns.

  2. In summary, there are so many doubts raised by the evidence that we find Bayshore has not established the Commissioner’s assessment of 18 December 2019 that Bayshore was entitled to a negative net amount of $40,484 (TB 025.26) which could be refunded, was excessive or otherwise incorrect. That conclusion flows from our finding Bayshore has not established it made creditable acquisitions under s 11-5 of the GST Act from Westpoint Constructions, Westpoint Realty and Westpoint Corporation whereby Bayshore was entitled to input tax credits for GST of $1,863,429 for the December 2005 tax period.
  3. We therefore find that the Bayshore Reviewable Decision should be affirmed.

MARKET STREET

The entity

  1. The ‘Market Street Trust’ was originally called the ‘Marriott on Market Syndicate’. It was established as a managed investment scheme under a constitution dated 16 June 2000. The responsible entity for the scheme was Westpoint Management Limited (‘Westpoint Management’). The Constitution was executed by two Directors of Westpoint Management, Mr Brian Thomas and Mr Richard Beck (TB 031.1, pdf 1, 8, 46).
  2. Westpoint Management executed a “Deed of Variation of the 60 Market Street Trust” on 4 September 2001 (‘Market Street Trust Deed’). Stamp duty was paid on the deed. The Market Street Trust Deed repealed the Constitution and replaced it with a new one. It was executed by Westpoint Management and the sole unitholder, Quartz Nominees Pty Ltd (ACN 008 859 103) (TB 031.2, pdf 1, 2, 3). We referred to that entity earlier in these reasons as the ‘Quartz Company’. Mr Carey signed as Director of both entities and Mr Rundle signed as Company Director of both entities.
  3. As we mentioned earlier, Mr Carey was a Director and Company Secretary of the Quartz Company from 5 December 1988. Mr Allan Carey was a director from 5 December 1988 until 12 April 2006. Mr Grant Dodd was a Company Secretary from 28 October 2003 until 1 December 2020. Mr Rundle was Company Secretary from 30 June 1997 to 27 January 2006 (TB 031.A6, pdf 3-5).
  4. The definition of ‘Constitution’ in the Market Street Trust Deed refers to “the Constitution establishing the 60 Market Street Trust dated 16 June 2000 ... as amended by a deed of variation dated 29 June 2001” (TB 031.2, pdf 2). We were not provided with a copy of this deed of variation.
  5. Mr Nairn was a Director of Westpoint Management from 19 September 2005 until 18 October 2020 when the company was deregistered. Mr Rundle was a Director from 23 October 2001 to 27 January 2006. Mr Carey was a Director from 28 May 1996 to 21 December 2005. Mr Richard Beck was a Director from 10 June 1998 to 9 September 2005 (TB 031.10, pdf 1, 3).
  6. We understand a receiver was appointed to Westpoint Management as Trustee for the Market Street Trust on 22 December 2005 (A4, para [42]). Receivers and managers were subsequently appointed to Westpoint Management from 24 January 2006, as well as a provisional liquidator on 9 February 2006, and a court appointed liquidator on 11 April 2006 (TB 028.17, pdf 5-6).
  7. The receivers and managers took possession of the Market Street project, completed the development and finalised the sales of the apartments in due course (A4, para [53]).
  8. Like Cinema City and Bayshore, in these proceedings, an additional document called, ‘Deed of Appointment of Additional Trustee: 60 Market Street Trust’ (‘MST Additional Trustee Deed’) executed on 7 December 2005 (TB 028.16) was produced by the Applicant. It purports to add Heca Nominees as an additional trustee from the date of execution.
  9. The MST Additional Trustee Deed was executed by Mr Carey on behalf of both parties. It was also unstamped at the time, and we reiterate the observation we made that contemporaneous stamping would have provided some confirmation as to when it was created, especially as the document was only produced during these proceedings.
  10. By virtue of the MST Additional Trustee Deed and a Ratification Deed which we discuss below, the Applicant entity is claimed to be Heca Nominees as trustee for the Market Street Trust. For convenience, we will refer to the Applicant entity as ‘Market Street’. Where the relevant documentation refers more generally to the trustee for the Market Street Trust, we will refer to the entity as ‘The Trustee’.
  11. Like Cinema City and Bayshore, the Commissioner submitted we should not accept the MST Additional Trustee Deed as evidence of the appointment of Heca Nominees as an additional trustee of the Market Street Trust, and we should find any purported appointment was ineffective (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [583]). We agree with the Commissioner for reasons we will now discuss.
  12. The totality of the evidence raises doubts about whether Heca Nominees was ever a trustee of the Market Street Trust. There is no evidence of any steps taken to transfer title to the assets into the joint names of Heca Nominees and Westpoint Management. Nor is there any evidence Heca Nominees and Westpoint Management ever acted jointly.
  13. Under cross-examination Mr Carey conceded none of the assets were transferred into joint names (transcript/308-309). He could not confirm whether Heca Nominees took any steps as a trustee at any time prior to 3 September 2009, being the date of a letter from Mr Carey to the Commissioner claiming Heca Nominees as trustee for 60 Market Street was owed substantial GST refunds for 1 to 21 December 2005 (TB 028.22).
  14. We are also unaware of evidence the receivers and managers or other entities in the Westpoint group were ever notified Heca Nominees was an additional trustee: Mr Carey could not recall ever informing the receivers and managers of that important development (transcript/309). Indeed, the relevant tax invoices, which we discuss below, were issued to Westpoint Management as trustee for the Market Street Trust. There is no mention of Heca Nominees in the tax invoices.
  15. There is also no evidence Heca Nominees fulfilled any accounting obligations, as required by clause 27 of the Market Street Trust Deed, which included the preparation of annual accounts. We note the following evidence of Mr Carey tends to confirm Heca Nominees did not meet these accounting obligations at the relevant time (transcript/311):
    MR DAVIES: Well, my question to, Mr Carey, is whether at any time HECA has undertaken the accounting required by clause 27?

    MR CAREY: Well, I have a small team of accountants in my office that are bringing these entities up to compliance, but it's difficult because we don't have the original documents, and we need to put in all of the payments and receipts of receivers. But, with Westpoint Management Limited at this time other entities of mine had security over Westpoint Management Limited through rights of subrogation.

    MR DAVIES: So, Mr Carey, is the answer to my question that HECA Nominees has not kept books of account and records that are in relation to the trust fund of 60 Market Street as required by clause 27?

    MR CAREY: Well, as I've said to you that there's a small team of accountants that are reconstructing those accounts.

    MR DAVIES: So, does that mean that those accounts are not yet in existence?

    MR CAREY: They have been reconstructed, Mr Davies.

    MR DAVIES: So, do I take it from that, that at the moment the trust does not have proper records and proper accounts?

    MR CAREY: Well, those records comprise the records that were seized by ASIC, which were recovering, but in particular the receivership has been there on Westpoint Management for 15 years, so there's multiple receiverships and liquidation where we've got to factor in or account for the expenses and receipts during that period of time, which is in progress.

    MR DAVIES: Let's just be clear about it, as we stand at the moment HECA Nominees is not able to produce a book of account that demonstrates that there is an asset which it holds as trustee for the trust?

    MR CAREY: Well, not here and now today because it's being reconstructed.

  16. These ‘reconstructed’ accounts are not before us. Indeed, we have no financial records to confirm the trust continued to exist after Westpoint Management was deregistered.
  17. We note there is evidence that the Market Street Trust was wound up during the process of liquidation. A Report to Creditors of Westpoint Management dated 13 June 2013 by the liquidators stated, with respect to the Market Street Trust, that (TB 031.7, pdf 9, 12):
    The Perpetual receivership has been completed with no surplus funds being available to the liquidator. The trust will be wound up when the liquidation of WML [Westpoint Management] is finalised.
  18. The liquidator also noted part of his role was to “complete the winding up of each of the trusts and MISs [Managed Investment Schemes]” (TB 031.7, pdf 14). The following statement also indicates part of the role of the liquidator was to wind up the trusts for which Westpoint Management was the responsible entity (TB 031.7, pdf 16):
    The Liquidators’ fees which relate to the various entities of which WML is trustee or responsible entity will be payable from the assets of the relevant trusts and managed investment schemes, to the extent that those assets are sufficient. Some of the trusts may not have sufficient assets to pay the Liquidators’ fees. Accordingly, those fees will be payable by WML.
  19. A Report to Creditors by the liquidator of Westpoint Management dated 5 July 2017 (TB 031.8, pdf 4) made similar reference to the Market Street Trust being wound up when the liquidation of Westpoint Management is finalised (pdf 7). Clause 5.5, titled “Market Street Trust” states (pdf 15):
    As advised previously, the Receivership of the Market Street Trust has been completed. The Receivers advised there was a shortfall on the secured creditor’s debt at the completion of the receivership. Accordingly, there was no funds available to the liquidator.

    The trust will be wound up when the liquidation of WML as trustee is finalised.

  20. Based on these reports and the absence of evidence of any accounts of the Market Street Trust, we find it is very likely the Market Street Trust was wound up and its assets distributed during the liquidation and winding up process.
  21. As for Bayshore, Mr Carey produced a resolution by Quartz Company dated 31 August 2021 that he had signed, from a meeting where he was the only person in attendance, which approved a Deed of Ratification for the Market Street Trust and authorised Mr Carey to execute it (TB 041.20). The company name was incorrect because (as we previously noted) Quartz Company changed its name to ACN 008 859 103 Pty Ltd on 3 May 2020 (TB 031.A6, pdf 1).
  22. On 31 August 2021, Mr Carey executed a ‘Deed of Ratification for the 60 Market Street Trust’ (TB 041.21) between the Quartz Company (notwithstanding the change in name) as Unit Holder, and Heca Nominees as the additional trustee (‘Ratification Deed’). Mr Carey purported to execute the document, which was not stamped, on behalf of both companies. We make the same observations as for the Cinema City Ratification Deed and the Bayshore Ratification Deed: we have concerns over the probative value of this evidence given the document was produced approximately 15 years after the MST Additional Trustee Deed - and after these proceedings were commenced.
  23. Like Cinema City and Bayshore, the Ratification Deed sought to confirm and ratify the appointment of Heca Nominees as Trustee of the Market Street Trust. With reference to s 7(5) and (6) of the Trustees Act 1962 (WA), the Ratification Deed also sought to confirm and ratify all acts or omissions of Heca Nominees done in its capacity as a Trustee of the Market Street Trust since the date of its appointment as additional trustee on 7 December 2005 (TB 041.21, pdf 3). However, the Ratification Deed does little to assist Market Street because apart from writing to the Commissioner on 3 September 2009 to claim substantial GST refunds were due, and otherwise pursuing the GST claim, there were no apparent acts or omissions to ratify.
  24. In summary, the evidence falls sort of persuading us Heca Nominees was appointed as an additional trustee. None of the trust assets were ever transferred into the joint names of Westpoint Management and Heca Nominees. They never acted jointly, and there is no evidence that Heca Nominees did anything in its capacity as trustee, other than to notify and pursue the GST input tax credit claim with the Commissioner. There is no evidence of Heca Nominees being recognised as a trustee in any third party or related entity dealings involving the Market Street Trust. The claim that Heca Nominees was appointed as a trustee has not been substantiated, nor have we been provided with a clear commercial explanation for why that would have occurred, save that it served the Applicant’s purposes in these proceedings.

The assessment

  1. The Trustee for the Market Street Trust was registered for GST on a non-cash accruals basis from 1 October 2000 to 17 March 2006 (TB 028.8). It lodged its BAS monthly from 1 October 2000 (TB 028.1, pdf 8, para [21]).
  2. The Trustee for the Market Street Trust was due to lodge a BAS for the December 2005 tax period on 23 January 2006 (TB 028.3). Mr Carey said that BAS could not be lodged because records and documents had been seized by ASIC and relevant staff who had worked for the Market Street Trust and for other entities in the Westpoint group had their employment terminated when the receiver was appointed (A4, para [43]).
  3. On 3 September 2009 Mr Carey said he sent a letter to the Commissioner in similar terms to the other applications. The letter confirmed receivers were appointed without warning and that they were refusing to provide access to the books and records. The letter also claimed Heca Nominees as trustee for the Market Street Trust was owed substantial GST refunds for 1 to 21 December 2005 (TB 028.22). As for the other applications, we accept Mr Carey and Ms Gray’s evidence that the letter was sent (TB 028.23) even though the Commissioner has no record of receiving it.
  4. On 2 September 2014, we understand the Trustee for the Market Street Trust applied for a private tax ruling to confirm they were entitled to input tax credits for the December 2005 tax period (TB 028.1, pdf 11, para [36]).
  5. The Commissioner declined to make a private tax ruling on 22 August 2018 because the Commissioner said the Trustee had been unable to provide the information the Commissioner required to progress the ruling (TB 028.25).
  6. On 27 November 2018, the Trustee wrote to the Commissioner to request that the Commissioner issue a nil assessment for the December 2005 tax period (TB 028.25).
  7. On 7 March 2019, the Commissioner declined to issue the nil assessment and stated the appropriate way to generate review rights under Part IVC of the TAA would be for the Trustee to lodge a BAS and request the Commissioner make an assessment (TB 028.25).
  8. A nil BAS for the December 2005 tax period was lodged for the Market Street Trust on 12 December 2019 (TB 028.3, pdf 1-3).
  9. On 18 December 2019 the Commissioner issued a notice of assessment to the Trustee for the December 2005 tax period, with the assessed amount being nil (TB 028.26).

The Reviewable Decision

  1. On 30 January 2020 Mr Carey signed an authority authorising his tax advisors to prepare and submit an objection on behalf of Heca Nominees as Trustee for the Market Street Trust (TB 028.7).
  2. An objection dated 31 January 2020 was made by the tax advisors on behalf of ‘The Trustee for the 60 Market Street Trust’ (TB 028.5). The grounds of the objection were that the Trustee was entitled to input tax credits of $2,018,480 for the December 2005 tax period, and that the taxpayer’s entitlement to the refund or credit had not ceased (TB 028.6).
  3. The Commissioner disallowed the objection in full on 22 May 2020 (TB 028.27; 028.2). We will refer to this as the ‘Market Street Reviewable Decision’.
  4. On 27 May 2020, the Trustee sought review of the Market Street Reviewable Decision in this Tribunal (TB 028.1, pdf 1-3).

The tax invoices

  1. In a letter dated 30 January 2020 that accompanied the objection, the Trustee’s tax advisors described the Market Street project as follows (TB 028.6, pdf 2):
    Tower 1

    The Taxpayer acquired a re-development site in Melbourne CBD in 2000 located at 60 Market Street, Melbourne.

    The Taxpayer undertook a major re-development of the vacant office building converting it into a 21 storey building consisting of 280 hotel apartments, 24 residential apartments, commercial units and car bays.

    Tower 2

    The Taxpayer undertook a major development of this site converting the building into a 25 storey building consisting of 120 residential apartments, 3 commercial units and 42 car bays with a walkway link into the adjacent building on Level 1 (Stage 1).

    The Apartments were to be sold on completion on a strata-titled basis.

    Both projects will be referred to as the Market Street Project in this ruling.

  2. In his witness statement dated 17 December 2020 (A4, para [46]-[47]), Mr Carey described the Market Street project in this way:
    The Market Street project involved the development of two towers at 60 Market Street, Melbourne. The development was on the land contained in Certificate of Title Volume 10165 Folio 251, with the title having been subdivided into Lots A and B on Subdivision 3366050 which was registered in September 1995.

    Tower 1 involved a major re-development of the vacant office building by converting it to a 21 storey building consisting of 280 hotel apartments, 24 residential apartments, commercial units and car bays. Tower 2 involved converting the building into a [sic] storey building consisting of 120 residential apartments, 3 commercial units and 42 car bays with a walkway link to the adjacent building. ...

  3. The works were described with fewer residential apartments in a ‘Valuation Summary’ dated 20 January 2005 (TB 030.55, pdf 1):
    Upon completion of the proposed development, the building (fronting Market Street and known as stage 1) will have been converted into a Four Star Hotel, comprising 280 hotel rooms and suites over 21 levels with 37 residential apartments to be included over three new levels to be added to the existing structure.
  4. There is also reference to the Panorama Sky Apartments at 60 Market Street, Melbourne comprising a “Residential Apartment complex comprising 37 apartments” in a finishes schedule (TB 030.55, pdf 40), as well as in the building contract (TB 031.3; 028.9) which we discuss below.
  5. The objection letter explained the Trustee had engaged Westpoint Constructions to “design and construct the project”; Westpoint Realty “to carry out the marketing and sales of the project”; and Westpoint Corporation “to provide development management services to manage the project” (TB 028.6, pdf 2).
  6. Market Street relies on the following tax invoices dated 20 December 2005 issued to “Westpoint Management Limited ATF 60 Market Street Trust” for the December tax period:
    (a) “Tax invoice – number 0201-15 Pacific International Hotel Apartments – 60 Market Street, Melbourne” issued by Westpoint Constructions in the sum of $11,909,977 for “Value of Work Completed to Date” including GST of $1,082,725 (TB 028.12).

    (b) Tax invoice Number 038-14 issued by Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem in the sum of $5,306,862 for “Marketing, Selling and Administration Fees” including GST of $482,442 (TB 028.13).

    (c) “Tax invoice – number 0805-076 60 Market Street Development” issued by Westpoint Corporation in the sum of $4,986,439, including GST of $453,312 (TB 028.14). The work was described as:

    Fee for professional services rendered in respect of the 60 Market Street Development, as follows:

    • Development management services up to and including 20 December 2005

    • Service fee for achieving budgeted development approval

    • Ongoing building & development services

    • Ongoing work on the marketing scheme for the residential component of

    the development

Documentation relied upon by Market Street

  1. We will now outline the documentation that Market Street relies upon to support its claims that it made creditable acquisitions from Westpoint Constructions, Westpoint Realty and Westpoint Corporation.
  2. We note all the agreements were signed by Mr Carey and Mr Rundle on behalf of each party.

Documents said to establish taxable supplies were made by Westpoint Constructions

  1. In the objection letter dated 30 January 2020, the Trustee’s tax advisors stated they relied upon a building contract at Appendix 4 (TB 028.6, pdf 2). This was a ‘Building Contract’ for ‘Project: 60 Market Street’ between Westpoint Management (Principal) and Westpoint Constructions (Contractor) dated 10 July 2003 (TB 031.3; 028.9) (‘Building Contract’). The Building Contract is not stamped.
  2. Under the Building Contract, Westpoint Management appointed Westpoint Constructions to undertake the design, construction and completion of “The Work”’ described in clause 2, (TB 031.3, pdf 13). That clause provides:
    The Works and the work under the Contract comprise the design, construction and completion of:
(a) Domestic Work – the 37 residential apartments at Levels 22 to 24 Mezzanine of 60 Market Street. Melbourne more fully described in the Contract Documents.

(b) Commercial Work – the 280 hotel apartments at Levels 2 to 21 and the hotel service areas and common areas at basement to level 1 of 60 Market Street Melbourne work more fully described in the Contract Documents.

  1. We note the project is described in clause 2 with fewer domestic apartments in the Building Contract than in the objection letter (which referred to 120 residential apartments). In his evidence at the hearing, Mr Carey agreed the project essentially comprised 37 residential apartments and 280 hotel apartments (transcript/277).
  2. Clause 7 of the Building Contract acknowledges Westpoint Constructions had been undertaking works on site prior to the Building Contract being entered into, and that any work and payments made prior to the Building Contract being entered into are agreed to be part of this Building Contract (TB 031.3, pdf 15).
  3. Clause 3 provided the work was to be done for the contract sum of $37,779,000. This sum comprised $10,578,120 for the Domestic Work and $27,200,880 for the Commercial Work. The contract sum was not fixed and could be altered under the General Conditions of Contract (TB 031.3, pdf 13).
  4. Clause 3.1 of the ‘General Conditions of Contract for Design and Construct’ which formed part of the Building Contract concerns “Performance and Payment” (TB 031.3, pdf 23-24). It provides:
    The Contractor shall execute and complete the work under the Contract in accordance with the requirements of the Contract.

    Subject to the performance by the Contractor of its obligations under the Contract and to the satisfaction of any pre-conditions to the Contractor’s entitlement to receive payment, the Principal shall pay the Contractor –

(a) for work for which the Principal accepted a lump sum, the lump sum, and

(b) for work for which the Principal accepted rates, the sum ascertained by multiplying the measured quantity of each section or item of work actually carried out under the Contract by the rate accepted by the Principal for the section or item,

adjusted by any additions or deductions made pursuant to the Contract.
  1. Clause 27.1, which is headed “Access to and Possession of the Site”, provides (in part) that Westpoint Management shall give Westpoint Construction access to the site to enable the contractor to commence and carry out their design obligations, and to commence further work under the Building Contract by the time stated in Annexure Part A (TB 031.3, pdf 48). Curiously, the time specified for giving possession of the site to enable the commencement of further work was stated in Annexure Part A, item 36, as 1 October 2002 (TB 031.3, pdf 91), approximately nine months prior to the date of this Building Contract. We think this discrepancy is explained by clause 7 (outlined above) which contemplated Westpoint Construction starting work prior to the Building Contract being entered into.
  2. Clause 42.1, which is headed “Payment Claims, Payment Schedules, Calculations and Time for Payment”, is a large block of text referring to times for payment in Annexure Part A. It also sets out specific requirements to be satisfied before any payments are made. These include the requirement for an accompanying certificate of practical completion and evidence of the amount due and the value of the work to be submitted to the Superintendent before the payment claim will be approved. Upon approval, the Superintendent was required to set out the calculations employed to arrive at the amount of the payment in a detailed payment schedule, which can be adjusted by a quantity surveyor. The import of that clause for these proceedings is clear enough: we would expect to see a substantial paper trail recording the work that was done under the terms of the agreement.
  3. The date for practical completion is stated in item 9 of Annexure Part A as 5 August 2003, approximately a month after the Building Contract was entered into. Again, we think this timing is contemplated by clause 7, which contemplates that Westpoint Constructions started work prior to the Building Contract being entered into (TB 031.3, pdf 88).
  4. Item 46 of Annexure Part A provides the time for payment claims to be made was on the “25th Day of each month” (TB 031.3, pdf 92).

Documents said to establish taxable supplies were made by Westpoint Realty

  1. In the objection letter dated 30 January 2020, the Trustee’s tax advisors also said they relied upon an Agency Agreement at Appendix 5 (TB 028.6, pdf 2). This was an ‘Agency Agreement’ dated 1 June 2002 between Westpoint Management as trustee for the Market Street Trust (Principal) and Westpoint Realty (Agent) (TB 028.10).
  2. The “Properties in the Development” in Annexure A (TB 028.10, pdf 13) are described as:
    PACIFIC INTERNATIONAL APARTMENTS

    60 MARKET STREET, MELBOURNE 3000

    UNITS 1 THROUGH 280

    On proposed plan of subdivision number PS 336605Q and being part of the land now effected by plan of subdivision PS 336605Q & comprises [sic] in certificates of title volume 10255 folios 637, 638, 675 – 693 (inclusive) and 695 – 696 (inclusive)

  3. This Agency Agreement is otherwise the same (that is, it has the same term of 36 months, the same operative clauses and Remuneration Scale in Annexure B) as the Agency Agreement we discussed for Bayshore.
  4. In a further witness statement dated 22 October 2021, Mr Carey attached a letter on Westpoint Management letterhead dated 12 May 2005, executed by Mr Carey on behalf of Westpoint Management as trustee for the Market Street Trust and Westpoint Realty (TB 131, pdf 6) which extended the term of the agreement by 24 months and entitled the agent to invoice 100% commission. It stated:

It is hereby agreed that the following variations to the agency agreement dated 01/06/2002.

  1. The term of the agreement be extended by 24 months.
  2. The agent should be entitled to invoice the full commission that will be paid 100% of commissions in relation to the residential apartments sales.
  3. In his witness statement dated 5 July 2021 (A5, para [65]), Mr Carey referred to another agreement for the first time, a property management service agreement. This was a ‘Property Management Service Agreement 60 Market Street’ dated 8 June 2003 between Westpoint Management as trustee for the Market Street Trust (Owner) and Westpoint Realty (Manager) (TB 052) (‘Management Agreement’). Under the agreement, Westpoint Management appointed Westpoint Realty as the sole manager and agent for the purpose of managing the Market Street property.
  4. Again, this Management Agreement is the same as the equivalent agreements for the Earlmist, Silkchime and Cinema City applications. That is, it has the same operative clauses and the same fees and charges.
  5. Market Street also relies upon a ‘Westpoint Realty – Project Cost Summary’ for the ‘60 Market Street’ project dated 20 December 2005 (TB 030.47) for $5,306,862 which comprises:
    (a) Marketing costs of $476,343. The breakdown of these costs is shown as $58,357 for “Advertising & Marketing – General”; $39,680 for “Consultants – Marketing & PR Telemarketing”; $285,116 for “Telemarketing”; $62,772 for “Westpoint Multimedia Expenses”; and $30,418 for “Advertising – Print”.

    (b) Selling costs of $3,225,514 comprising $3,194,664 for “Commission – Apartments” and $30,850 for “Legal”.

    (c) Leasing costs of $1,605,005 comprising $1,584,000 for “Commission – Pacific International Hotel” and $21,005 for “Legal”.

Documents said to establish taxable supplies were made by Westpoint Corporation

  1. In the objection letter dated 30 January 2020, the Trustee’s tax advisors said they relied upon a Development Management Agreement at Appendix 6 (TB 028.6, pdf 2). This was a ‘Development Management Agreement (60 Market Street Development)’ made on 18 February 2003 between Westpoint Management as trustee for the Market Street Trust (Developer) and Westpoint Corporation (Manager) (TB 028.11) (‘DM Agreement’).
  2. This DM Agreement has the same recitals, operative provisions and management fee as the DM Agreement we outlined above for Bayshore.

Has Market Street established it made creditable acquisitions?

  1. For reasons we will now explain, there is too much uncertainty raised by the evidence for us to be satisfied about what was supplied, and the value of anything supplied, such that we do not think that Market Street has met its burden under s 14ZZK of the TAA.

What was supplied by Westpoint Constructions?

  1. The Commissioner submitted the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Constructions does not accord with and was not authorised by the provisions of the Building Contract (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [658]).
  2. In support of the amount of the supply by Westpoint Constructions in the tax invoice, Market Street produced several additional documents:
    (a) A document Mr Carey refers to in his supplementary witness statement dated 31 August 2021 (A6, para [19]) that he says was a valuation summary prepared by Colliers International dated 20 May 2005. We note there is no date on this document, nor any reference on the document as to the person who prepared it. This document refers to the Building Contract being for $37,779,000 and there being approved variations of $40,191,425, with the total cost of the project being $91,992,644 (TB 041.7, pdf 2). This document provides some confirmation that the contract price was increased but does not shed light on the specific amount of the tax invoice in question.

    (b) A ‘Recommendation for Construction Progress Payment No 10’ dated 21 December 2005 prepared by ACSM Pty Ltd (TB 030.45). We note ACSM Pty Ltd is identified as the Superintendent in item 7 of Annexure Part A of the Building Contract (TB 031.3, pdf 88). The recommendation notes the variations totalling $40,190,425. The recommendation appears to comply with clause 42.1 of the Building Contract. The Commissioner submitted the dates stated in this recommendation appear to be different to those in the contract. For example, the date for possession is stated as 1 March 2004, but in item 36 of Annexure Part A of the Building Contract it is 1 October 2002. The date for practical completion is stated as 28 February 2006, however item 9 of the Building Contract states it is 5 August 2003 (TB 031.3, pdf 88 and 91). However, these latter dates appear to us to be revised dates because the preceding sentence refers to delays in the completion of the building work until the end of February 2006 (TB 030.45, pdf 7). This potentially explains the inconsistency in the dates.

    (c) A ‘Progress Payment – Construction Works Summary’ for works up to 20 December 2005, marked progress payment 10 (TB 030.44). The summary contains a detailed itemised list of the numerous construction-related works with a breakdown of the fee for each. The amount in this summary is $10,827,252 which is the amount in the tax invoice, less the GST. The Commissioner submitted we do not have any details of the other progress payments, so we do not know whether any of the costs were already charged in progress payments one to nine (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [659]). Market Street submitted three documents confirmed the work had not previously been invoiced (Applicant’s closing submissions in reply, para [137]). We do not think this is correct. The documents referred to by the Applicant, being the tax invoice (TB 028.12), the ‘Progress Payment – Construction Works Summary’ (TB 030.44) and the Recommendation by ACSM (030.45, pdf 3) indicate to us $56,507,269 has “previously been claimed” (TB 028.12) under the first nine progress payments.

  3. Even if we accepted this evidence tended to support Westpoint Construction’s entitlement to the amounts invoiced, there are still issues concerning the mutual set-off. We will deal with those below.

What was supplied by Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem?

  1. The Commissioner submitted the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem does not accord with and was not authorised by the terms of the Agency Agreement. Specifically, the Commissioner submitted the preconditions to Westpoint Realty ATF Westgem being entitled to remuneration were not satisfied (Respondent closing submissions, paras [633]-[634]).
  2. Mr Carey’s evidence was that the selling commission in the ‘Westpoint Realty – Project Cost Summary’ of $3,472,896 (TB 30.47) was four percent of the value of contracts exchanged (A5, para [76(a)]). A status report dated 20 December 2005 (TB 030.48), confirms 268 contracts were exchanged in total for the hotel apartments and nil for residential apartments totalling $86,822,400 (TB 030.48). Mr Carey incorrectly stated the number in his witness statement as $88,822,400. That is understandable given the numbers in the status report are slightly blurred. If the value of contracts exchanged was $86,822,400, Mr Carey is correct in his calculation that four percent of that amount is $3,472,896.
  3. However, we are uncertain as to whether deposits were paid for all the contracts exchanged, and if so, when they were paid. In the status report, the section stating the total for the hotel apartments includes sub-headings for “unconditional – deposit received (non-qualifying)” and “unconditional – awaiting deposit”, showing nil for both. The next sub-heading is, “Conditional – awaiting deposit”, with three contracts totalling $1,077,200 being stated. The entitlement to invoice commission, according to clause 5.2 of the Agency Agreement, arises when a deposit has been paid by the purchaser in accordance with a contract or a Deposit Power Bond (an expression that was not defined) has been received. The status report suggests there were only three contracts where a deposit was paid which would entitle Westpoint Realty to invoice for commission.
  4. Even if we assume deposits were paid as contended, another issue for Market Street is when the deposits were paid and whether they had been previously invoiced. Mr Carey conceded many of the contracts of sale were entered into prior to 1 June 2002 when the Agency Agreement was made (transcript/284). In his witness statement dated 5 July 2021 (A5, para [76(a)]), Mr Carey referred to a sample contract of sale for Hotel Apartment A605 dated 22 May 2002 (TB 30.49). At the hearing Mr Carey confirmed he thought the Agency Agreement entitled Westpoint Realty to charge for work carried out before that agreement was entered into (transcript/285). However, the Agency Agreement is expressed to commence on the date of the deed (1 June 2002) and is not expressed to operate retrospectively.
  5. During that part of the cross-examination where he was asked about why he thought Westpoint Realty had an entitlement to charge for work that pre-dated the Agency Agreement, Mr Carey referred to another agreement. This was an unwritten agreement that he effectively negotiated with himself (transcript/285):
    MR DAVIES: Mr Carey, what I’m trying to grapple with is you keep referring to this agreement, what agreement are you referring to?

    MR CAREY: Well, when we bought the property, if I didn’t agree to do anything nothing would have happened. So, I’m saying to you that, when we entered the contract of either property, I engaged the Westpoint Realty and I engage Westpoint Constructions and I engaged Westpoint Corporation to carry out the Realty function of – which is the leasing and selling, and Construction was to design and construct the building, and Corporation to manage the day-to-day development. Because they were the ones that had employees. The vehicle we purchased (indistinct) property had no employees.

    MR DAVIES: This is an engagement that you made before 1 June 2002?

    MR CAREY: Yes.

    MR DAVIES: It’s not an engagement that’s recorded in writing?

    MR CAREY: No.

    MR DAVIES: It’s an engagement that consists of an agreement between yourself and yourself?

    MR CAREY: Well, myself as the managing director of Realty and myself as the managing director of Westpoint Management.

    MR DAVIES: Not reflected in the minutes of any of those entities?

    MR CAREY: Well, Mr Davies, we did – we're doing 20, 30 projects all on this basis. So, I'm just explaining to you the processes that were being carried out and how we conducted ourselves to carry out these projects.

  6. It is difficult to know what to make of an unwritten agreement that Mr Carey effectively negotiated with himself as Director of the two entities which he now says modifies the Agency Agreement and – conveniently for the Applicant - substantiates an entitlement for Westpoint Realty to charge for work under the revised Agency Agreement. The evidence is another example of Mr Carey referring to unwritten agreements or missing documents or un-minuted meetings to buttress the Applicant’s case or explain other inconvenient evidence. In the circumstances, we think this oral evidence should be given limited weight.
  7. Given contracts of sale were being entered into from 2001, there is uncertainty as to whether the commissions were charged when they fell due. Mr Carey’s evidence was that, from his vantage point as the controller of all the entities, he was aware commissions had not previously been charged under 13 invoices which had preceded the December 2005 invoice (transcript/287). Like Bayshore, Mr Carey’s evidence was that he facilitated a delay in invoicing for the work because he was “bringing to account an income” of the Westpoint group entities in preparation for an upcoming IPO (transcript/288):
    MR CAREY: ... Westpoint Group entities were gearing up for an initial public offering, and we were bringing to account an income in accordance with quite a predetermined income build up to the IPO, so I was with the financial controls and things very carefully bringing income to account to match that projection. And I have handed a document to my lawyers which is a corporate plan that shows that IPO, and I'd like to submit it to this tribunal if I can to reinforce what I’m saying.

    MR DAVIES: Are you saying that it was an objective of the IPO in preparing for the IPO not to reveal income in Realty?

    MR CAREY: No, what I'm saying is that I would bring to account the income, because there was a lot of different businesses, not just Realty, to achieve a particular projected earnings leading up to the IPO, and because the group was audited by KPMG, and KPMG were part of that IPO group that was organising it they were very happy with that approach.

    MR DAVIES: So, are you saying that in circumstances in which work had been carried out by Realty for Westpoint Management, which had the consequence that Westpoint Management was obliged under existing agreements to pay a substantial sum of money to Realty, that you massaged matters so as to hide the fact that Westpoint Management owed Westpoint Realty a large sum of money in respect of executed contracts of sale?

    MR CAREY: No, I simply managed when income was brought to account. ...

  8. In support, Mr Carey submitted a document titled ‘Westpoint Corporate Plan 2005/2006 – 2014/2015 Year 1 2005/2006’ but it does not contain any reference to deferring income or otherwise confirm his claim (A14).
  9. As we concluded in relation to Bayshore, we find Mr Carey’s explanation difficult to accept. There is no supporting documentation confirming monies owed would not be invoiced and would be deferred leading up to the IPO. There is no persuasive evidence to suggest commissions that were due prior to the Agency Agreement would not have been invoiced when they fell due.
  10. We now turn to the leasing commission of $1,584,000 (TB 030.47), which Market Street claims was payable under the Management Agreement (TB 052). Clause 4.2 provides “Agreement to Lease fees will be due and payable once the negotiation is completed and all terms are agreed” (TB 052, pdf 9). Mr Carey claimed the leasing commission was charged for a ‘Development Agreement’ entered into in 2001 (the day and month are unclear) between Westpoint Management as trustee for the Market Street Trust (Owner), Pacific Hotel Market Street Pty Ltd (ACN 097 245 095) (Lessee) and Parkes Street Pty Ltd (ACN 076 847 660) and Richland Investment (Australia) Pty Ltd (ACN 076 829 653) (Guarantor) (TB 030.50). The Development Agreement is not stamped. Mr Carey as Director and Mr Rundle as Company Secretary signed the agreement on behalf of Westpoint Corporation (TB 030.50, pdf 22).
  11. The recitals to the Development Agreement explain, amongst other things, that Westpoint Management is the owner of the land and has agreed to develop the property (including 280 apartments), and that the lessee will lease the 280 apartments and part of the common property (TB 030.50, pdf 3).
  12. Clause 2.1 of the Development Agreement (TB 030.50, pdf 7) provides:
    Agreements to Lease

    The Owner grants and the Lessee accepts the Apartment Leases and the Common Property Lease in the form of the lease annexed with:

  • (1) Any amendments as may be agreed; and
  • (2) The latest form of the Plan of Subdivision and the latest form of the Rules attached.
  1. This clause suggests the lease was still being negotiated. In any event, there is no lease, no plan of subdivision and no rules attached to the Development Agreement. We also note clause 2.2, which concerns the execution of the leases, makes clear that no leases had been executed (TB 030.50, pdf 7). It is also unclear what the proposed rent was (clause 4.1, pdf 10) and therefore the amount of the commission cannot be quantified. As such, we do not agree this document constitutes an agreement to lease. The Development Agreement is better characterised as a document that preceded an agreement for lease or a lease. Even if the Development Agreement could be regarded as an agreement for lease, we agree with the Commissioner’s submission that there is no evidence it was negotiated by Westpoint Realty (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [648]).
  2. Mr Carey claimed Westpoint Realty was entitled to charge a leasing commission when the Development Agreement was entered into in 2001 but said he did not invoice the fee until December 2005 (transcript/282). He was asked about whether he was certain the leasing commission had not been charged previously (transcript/282):
    MR DAVIES: What I’m asking you though was that on your scenario the entitlement to charge for it arose when it became an agreement to lease, which was in 2001?

    MR CAREY: Yes.

    MR DAVIES: And it’s true, as you say, that you’ve produced an invoice in December 2005 where a charge is made in respect of it?

    MR CAREY: Yes.

    MR DAVIES: How is it, Mr Carey that you are sure that a charge had not been made for it between 2001 and December 2005?

    MR CAREY: Well, because I’m the managing director of Westpoint Realty and I personally negotiated the agreement, and I approve all of the invoices, so I know that as on my knowledge.

    MR DAVIES: So, what is there that demonstrates to you that the entitlement to charge for the work done in relation to the development agreement was not actually exercised until December 2005?

    MR CAREY: I’m telling you that I am the managing director of Westpoint Realty, the licensee, I was running it on a day-to-day basis, I negotiated this agreement, I entered into the agency agreement, I was overseeing this project day-by-day, and I approved the invoice in December 2005, so I know of my own knowledge. But if you look in the GST integrated account for Westpoint Management you won't find any refund previously in that amount.

  3. The Management Agreement was entered into on 8 June 2003, and yet the Development Agreement was entered into some time in 2001. Mr Carey said the Management Agreement operated retrospectively because this was simply the way the business was run (transcript/280):
    MR DAVIES: You rely upon some clause in that agreement that indicates that work done prior to June 2003, or that is prior to the commencement of that agreement, Realty could charge for it?

    MR CAREY: Yes, well, that’s the way things operated because clearly the work was carried out. This arrangement was negotiated with me as the managing director of Westpoint Realty. And the agreement was put in place, we charge commission on that basis.

    MR DAVIES: Would there have been something in the agreement itself, the written agreement itself, that suggests that it's intended to cover work done prior to June 2003?

    MR CAREY: I don’t know whether there’s an expressed provision but that’s the way things were done. As you know, I owned and controlled, Westpoint Management and also Westpoint Realty. The work was carried out on the basis that the commission was to be paid. That’s the agreement.

  4. While we acknowledge Mr Carey’s evidence about “the way things were done” in the business, the terms of the Management Agreement do not suggest it was intended to operate retrospectively.
  5. Mr Carey also referred to another agreement from approximately 2000 that Westpoint Realty would undertake the work (transcript/281):
    MR DAVIES: But what is there in this agreement, Mr Carey, that authorises Westpoint Realty to charge for work carried out before it was appointed manager?

    MR CAREY: Because that was the agreement between Westpoint Realty and Westpoint Management from the beginning and that's why Westpoint Realty was carrying out that work.

    MR DAVIES: So when you say from the beginning, when is that?

    MR CAREY: From when we contracted to buy the property in – yes, I think 2000.

    MR DAVIES: So, you say there was another agreement on foot from 2000 onwards?

    MR CAREY: Well, that was the agreement I had with Westpoint management that I own because Westpoint management had no employees, the people that had to do the work.

    MR DAVIES: And when you say it was the agreement you had, it was an agreement you had with yourself, was it?

    MR CAREY: Well, between those two entities. That’s why Westpoint Realty carried out that work.

  6. In this evidence Mr Carey appears to be referring to another unwritten agreement which apparently embodied his own personal view or understanding of who would do the work. We do not accept this evidence as being sufficient to substantiate the amount for the leasing commission in the December 2005 tax invoice. For reasons we have already explained in relation to the other applications, we do not think such a self-serving and unverifiable account can be given much weight. Having regard to the evidence as a whole, we think there are too many doubts as to whether the leasing commission was payable, whether it was invoiced previously, and whether it was supported by the Management Agreement which was not retrospective.
  7. We now turn to marketing and legal costs. The Commissioner submits there is no entitlement to recover those costs under the Agency Agreement or the Management Agreement (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [650]).
  8. In his witness statement dated 5 July 2021 (A5, para [107]-[109]), Mr Carey referred to marketing documentation to demonstrate the nature of the marketing work that was carried out for the Market Street project. He stated the activities that fell within the marketing category are illustrated by the Marketing & Sales Plan for the Cinema City project (TB 30.37). For example, Mr Carey observed the project cost summary states that as at 20 December 2005, the sum of $285,116 (out of total marketing costs of $476,343) was incurred for telemarketing (TB 030.47). He referred to telemarketing being an important part of the development and significant costs being incurred in that area (A5, paras [57], [107]). We note by way of example that a telemarketing activity report for the week commencing 15 July 2002 shows over 1000 hours of telemarketing for the Bayshore, Market Street and Cinema City projects (TB 30.15, pdf 372).
  9. Mr Carey also referred to various brochures and other materials to demonstrate marketing activity (A5, para [108]). These materials included: an ‘Introducer Pack’ which refers to the development as ‘Panorama Sky’ which included floorplans of apartments in the development (TB 030.55, pdf 109-152); 3D drawings of the development (pdf 156-162); a brochure for the ‘Panorama Sky Apartments’ (pdf 163-187); advertising materials for the retail space in the development which provided contact details of a person at Westpoint Realty (pdf 188-191); and another promotional brochure which includes a project summary (pdf 192-244, with the project summary at pdf 199).
  10. However, despite Mr Carey’s evidence about the marketing activities that took place, the difficulties for Market Street are similar to those we discussed above for Bayshore. There is no express term in the Agency Agreement that would entitle Westpoint Realty to charge the $476,343 of marketing costs shown in the project cost summary (TB 030.47). Clause 5.1 refers to the Agent being able to charge commission on the sale of each property calculated in accordance with the remuneration scale in Annexure B. Annexure B refers to the commission being four percent of the contract price for the property (TB 028.10, pdf 3 and 14).
  11. The Commissioner also submitted there were no express terms in the Management Agreement that would entitle Westpoint Realty to recover marketing and legal costs. The Applicant did not clearly address this argument in its submissions, but we disagree with the Commissioner about the effect of the agreement on this point. We note Westpoint Realty’s duties under clause 3.2.3 include the responsibility for “marketing of and leasing the property” (TB 052, pdf 6). Clause 4.3 also permits other fees for acting on behalf of Westpoint Management to be paid to Westpoint Realty; and clause 4.4 states Westpoint Management shall be liable for any other costs and expenses paid or incurred by Westpoint Realty on behalf of Westpoint Management (TB 052, pdf 11). We acknowledge the express wording could be more specific, but we think it is sufficiently clear for us to accept marketing and legal fees were recoverable under clauses 4.3 and 4.4.
  12. Notwithstanding that entitlement, the documents before us do not show how the very specific amounts set out in the project cost summary were calculated. Ms Rigg’s evidence does not really assist in this regard. She was a junior marketing officer located in Perth and her evidence suggests she was primarily involved in projects located in Western Australia. She was aware of the Market Street project and had travelled to Melbourne and Sydney to meet with the national marketing team, but she was not responsible for billing or assessing the cost of the marketing work done (TB 017, para [6], [24]; transcript/382-383).

What was supplied by Westpoint Corporation?

  1. As for Bayshore, the Commissioner submitted the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Corporation in December 2005 does not accord with the provisions of the DM Agreement dated 18 February 2003 (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [661]).
  2. In his witness statement dated 17 December 2020, Mr Carey said Westpoint Corporation issued a tax invoice to Market Street for “professional fees rendered in respect of the Market Street project” on 20 December 2005. Mr Carey said these fees were authorised by the terms of the DM Agreement whereby Westpoint Corporations was engaged to provide management services for the project (A4, para [58]).
  3. In his witness statement dated 5 July 2021, (A5, para [81]), Mr Carey stated the amount in the tax invoice was reflected in a Development Management Cost Summary dated 20 December 2005. This was a document titled ‘Westpoint Corporation – Development Management Cost Summary’ dated 20 December 2005 for the ‘60 Market Street’ project for a total of $4,533,127 (excluding GST) (TB 030.51). That document specified amounts for:
    (a) Item 1.1 – a $450,000 fee for “Issue of development approval”, calculated at 0.5% of $90,000,000.

    (b) Item 1.2 – a $450,000 fee for “Commencement of construction”, calculated at 0.5% of $90,000,000.

    (c) Item 2.1 – a $2,200,000 fee for “Achieving budgeted development approval”, calculated at 2% of $110,000,000.

    (d) Item 3.1 – a $918,127 fee for “Day to day management of the integrated property development process for this project from 01/07/2000 to 20/12/2005”.

    (e) Item 4.1 – a $515,000 fee for “Day to day management of marketing and selling process for this project from 01/07/2000 to 20/12/2005”.

  4. As for Bayshore, our initial observation is that clause 3 and item 8 of the Schedule contemplate the categories of fees charged under items 1.1, 1.2 and 2.1.
  5. The Commissioner submitted there is limited evidence to corroborate the amounts of the project costs ($90,000,000) or the project value ($110,000,000) that were referred to in the ‘Westpoint Corporation – Development Management Cost Summary’ of 20 December 2005. The Commissioner pointed out there is no reference to those sums in the DM Agreement (Respondent’s closing submissions, para [663]). Under cross-examination, Mr Carey agreed the DM Agreement did not assist with identifying the amount of the project costs (transcript/277). In his further witness statement dated 1 November 2021 (STB 142, para [16(a) and (b)]), Mr Carey explained the $90,000,000 of project costs was reflected in the trial balance of Market Street as at 31 December 2015 (TB 28.15) in the form of an entry for ‘WIP – Market Street’ showing a debit amount of $90,178,979.60. Mr Carey added the project value of $110,000,000 was “the value the project expected to receive on completion” comprised of $79,890,500 for the 280 hotel apartments and $30,972,000 for the residential apartments. Mr Carey also referred to a “pricing schedule” (STB 142/ [16(b)]) which is consistent with his evidence. We accept there is some evidence that corroborates the Applicant’s claims.
  6. There are also doubts raised by the timing of the tax invoice. It was issued in December 2005 even though the entitlement to payment arose prior to that time. The date of the DM Agreement was 18 February 2003. Under cross-examination, Mr Carey agreed development approval had been issued (in 2001) and construction had commenced by the date of the DM Agreement (with the major construction work starting in 2003) (transcript/278 and 290). When asked about the delay in charging the commissions for the issue of development approval (which could have been charged in 2001) and the commencement of construction (which could have been charged in 2003), Mr Carey again referred to “a predetermined plan how to bring income to account” due to the prospective IPO (transcript/288 and 291). For the reasons we have previously expressed, we are not satisfied that the amounts would not have been invoiced when the liability to pay them arose in 2001 and 2003.
  7. As he did in Bayshore, Mr Carey said he relied on item 9 of the Schedule to the DM Agreement to authorise the payment of additional fees for “day to day management”, in items 3.1 and 4.1. As we have already stated, item 9 of the Schedule provided for a ‘Rate of Remuneration For extra Services’ to be determined “By negotiation” (TB 028.11, pdf 15). Again, he confirmed the contemplated negotiation was a decision he made alone (transcript/293). Other than the sums stated in items 3.1 and 4.1, there is no documentation before us to verify what was negotiated.
  8. Mr Carey confirmed items 3.1 and 4.1 of the cost summary related to a six-year period commencing from 1 July 2000 (when the property was purchased) to December 2005 (when the fees were charged). He agreed he was purporting to charge for those fees under the DM Agreement, even though it was only entered into in February 2003 (transcript/293). Mr Carey also stated the amounts in those items included amounts for salary and wages paid from July 2000 which totalled $918,127 (TB 041, para [26]; 041.13). However, with respect to his calculations, he stated, “this is not a contemporaneous document, it’s something that I’ve done recently to try and illustrate how that was done” (transcript/294). The fact the DM Agreement is not retrospective, and the calculations are not contemporaneous, further adds to the doubt as to how the amounts in these items were arrived at.
  9. Even if we accept the evidence of Mr Carey in relation to the project costs and project value given there is some corroboration for those figures, questions remain over the services billed by Westpoint Corporation in this period. The DM Agreement was made in 2003 but work said to give rise to commissions had already been done. The DM Agreement was not retrospective, so it is not clear how those costs relating to earlier periods were properly included in the December 2005 tax invoice. We also note there is limited evidence that corroborates Mr Carey’s claim he negotiated a right to charge day-to-day management fees under item 9, and it is unclear if the earlier work was previously invoiced.

Issues regarding the payment of the tax invoices via set-off

  1. We observe there are similar issues regarding the payment of the tax invoices by way of a mutual set-off recorded in book entries as there were for the other applications.
  2. Like the other applications, Market Street relies upon the following loan agreements and drawdown notices to substantiate a mutual set-off where the payments of the tax invoices were offset against the loans:
    (a) A loan agreement dated 1 July 2002 between Westpoint Management as trustee for Market Street Trust as Borrower and Westpoint Corporation as Lender for a commitment of $35,000,000 (TB 030.40).

There is no evidence of any drawdown by Westpoint Management under this agreement.

(b) A loan agreement dated 3 February 2003 between Westpoint Constructions as Borrower and Westpoint Management as trustee for Market Street Trust as Lender for a commitment of $30,000,000 (TB 030.41).

On 20 December 2005 Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Westpoint Constructions wished to draw $11,909,977 on 20 December 2005 under the loan agreement (TB 030.52). This was the GST inclusive amount of the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Constructions on the same date.

(c) A loan agreement dated 3 February 2003 between Westpoint Realty as Borrower and Westpoint Management as trustee for Market Street Trust as Lender for a commitment of $20,000,000 (TB 030.42).

On 20 December 2005 Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Westpoint Realty wished to draw $5,306,862 on 20 December 2005 under the loan agreement (TB 030.53). This was the GST inclusive amount of the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Realty on the same date.

(d) A loan agreement dated 3 February 2003 between Westpoint Corporation as Borrower and Westpoint Management as trustee for Market Street Trust as Lender for a commitment of $10,000,000 (TB 030.43).

Again, on 20 December 2005, Mr Carey signed a drawdown notice stating Westpoint Corporation wished to draw $4,986,439 on 20 December 2005 under the loan agreement (TB 030.54). This was the GST inclusive amount of the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Corporation on the same date.

  1. Each of these loan agreements was signed by Mr Carey as Director and Mr Rundle as Company Secretary. None of the loan agreements are stamped.
  2. Again, like the other applications, Market Street relies upon an incomplete handwritten journal entry, with an “effective journal date” of 20 December 2005, to substantiate that a set-off occurred (TB 028.15, pdf 2). The entity is stated as “(46) 60 Market Street”. Like the other applications, the “journal number”, “date prepared”, “prepared by”, “entered by”, “authorised by” and “date entered” fields are blank. The “Journal Details” box had been completed to state, “Transactions to reduce loan by payment of invoices”.
  3. Market Street also relies on a trial balance as at 31 December 2005 to show the debiting and crediting of the loan amounts to pay the invoices (TB 028.15, pdf 1). That trial balance shows:
    (a) A debit of $20,184,799 for “WIP – Market Street”, which Market Street submits comprises the GST exclusive amounts payable by Market Street to Westpoint Constructions, Westpoint Realty and Westpoint Corporation in respect of the tax invoices dated 20 December 2005 (opening submissions, para [28(a)(i)] of Annexure 2 Market Street).

    (b) A debit of $2,018,479 for “GST Paid”, which Market Street submits is the GST payable under each of the tax invoices (opening submissions, para [28(a)(ii)] of Annexure 2 Market Street).

    (c) A credit of $11,909,977 for “Loan – Westpoint Constructions”, which Market Street describes as the reduction of the loan in the amount necessary to pay the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Constructions (opening submissions, para [28(a)(iii)] of Annexure 2 Market Street).

    (d) A credit of $5,306,862 for “Loan – Westgem Unit Trust”, which Market Street describes as the reduction of the loan in the amount necessary to pay the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Realty (opening submissions, para [28(a)(iv)] of Annexure 2 Market Street).

    (e) A credit of $4,986,439 for “Loan – Westpoint Corporation P/L”, which Market Street describes as the reduction of the loan in the amount necessary to pay the tax invoice issued by Westpoint Corporation (opening submissions, para [28(a)(v)] of Annexure 2 Market Street).

  4. There are no other financial records of Market Street before us, save for the tax invoices, the incomplete journal entry, and the trial balance.
  5. As with the other applications, there are no corresponding accounts for Westpoint Corporation, Westpoint Constructions or Westpoint Realty AFT Westgem that evidence a mutual set-off, nor any evidence that these companies accepted the mutual set-off by way of any express or implied agreement. We agree with the guidance in GSTD 2004/4 suggesting those features would ordinarily be present.
  6. The Westpoint group was on the verge of collapse when the tax invoices were issued in late 2005. As we mentioned above in relation to Bayshore and the other applications, winding up proceedings against Westpoint Corporation were commenced in the Federal Court on 19 December 2005 (TB 031.4). Receiver managers and an administrator were appointed on 24 January 2006. A liquidator was subsequently appointed on 16 February 2006 (TB 012.44, pdf 5). A receiver was appointed to Westpoint Constructions on 2 February 2006, followed by a court appointed liquidator on 22 February 2006 (TB 012.36, pdf 5). On 20 April 2006 receivers and managers were appointed to Westpoint Realty, followed by liquidators being appointed on 13 April 2007 (TB 012.38, pdf 6).
  7. Again, like the other applications before us, the reports as to affairs made by the receivers and managers and liquidators are inconsistent with the inter-company debts and mutual set-offs alleged by Market Street.
  8. A report by the administrators of Westpoint Realty dated 2 February 2007 listed related party loans. Neither Westpoint Management nor Westpoint Management as trustee for the Market Street Trust were listed as having monies owed to them under related party loans (TB 012.43, pdf 17).
  9. A report as to affairs for Westpoint Constructions dated 11 August 2006, which was verified by Mr Rundle on 9 August 2006, stated “60 Market Street Trust – Receiver” owed $1,455,679 (TB 031.A2, pdf 1, 3 and 7). In contrast, the trial balance for the Market Street Trust shows that as at 31 December 2005, after the credit of $11,909.977, the balance owing to Westpoint Constructions was $13,596,365.99 (TB 028.15).
  10. A report as to affairs for Westpoint Corporation dated 27 February 2006 by the official liquidator, certified as being true by Mr Rundle, Mr Carey and Mr Nairn on 23 February 2006, stated there was an amount owing of $16,458,041.56 in connection with “Loan – 60 Market Street Trust” with the explanation “no surplus likely from project” (TB 031.A1, pdf 1, 5 and 9). This sum is substantially different from the amount of the loan stated in the trial balance for the Market Street Trust as at 31 December 2005, which shows that after the credit of $4,986,439, the balance owing to Westpoint Corporation was $6,458,041.85 (TB 028.15).
  11. These reports are inconsistent with the amounts of inter-company debts and mutual set-offs alleged by Market Street.
  12. Further, the GST reporting for Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty are inconsistent with the creditable acquisitions Market Street is claiming to have made because the BASs of these entities do not show corresponding taxable supplies.
  13. As we mentioned above for Bayshore, Westpoint Corporation did not lodge a BAS for December 2005 (TB 019.5, pdf 1). Westpoint Constructions lodged a BAS on 2 March 2006 with a net amount of nil and no taxable supplies for the December 2005 tax period (TB 012.32). Westpoint Realty lodged a BAS for the period 1 October 2005 to 31 December 2005 on 27 February 2006 which reported total sales of $302,711 and non-capital purchases of $785,046 (TB 012.34).
  14. We therefore agree with the Commissioner’s submission that these BASs do not show taxable supplies that correspond to the acquisitions which Market Street claims to have made.
  15. The Commissioner makes a similar submission here to the one made in relation to Silkchime and Bayshore. That is, Westpoint Realty’s BAS was lodged on 27 February 2006 before the receiver and manager was appointed to Westpoint Realty on 20 April 2006 (TB 012.38, pdf 1). Again, even if we accept Mr Carey’s evidence that the books of Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty were seized by ASIC in January 2006, there is still a lack of any corroborating evidence from the perspective of Westpoint Corporation, Westpoint Constructions and Westpoint Realty that they made taxable supplies to Market Street.

Conclusion for Market Street

  1. For the reasons we have outlined above, we find Market Street has not established on the evidence that it made creditable acquisitions as contended from Westpoint Constructions, Westpoint Realty and Westpoint Corporation. The doubts we have about this application are similar to those we expressed for Cinema City and Bayshore. In summary, our reasons include:
    (a) As for Bayshore, there is significant doubt as to whether Heca Nominees was validly appointed as an additional trustee to the Market Street Trust. None of the trust assets were ever transferred into the joint name and the alleged trustees never acted jointly. Heca Nominees did nothing as a trustee other than to notify and pursue GST claims with the Commissioner, and there is no evidence Heca Nominees was recognised as a trustee by any third party.

    (b) There is considerable doubt as to whether the Market Street Trust continued to exist after Westpoint Management was deregistered. The reports to creditors prepared by the liquidators clearly refer to the winding up of the Market Street Trust and distribution of its assets to creditors. Other than the trial balance, incomplete journal entry and tax invoice, there are also no financial records, including recent financial records or books of account, that show the trust fund continues to exist. In all the circumstances, we are not satisfied the trust continues to exist.

    (c) With respect to whether taxable supplies were made to Market Street by Westpoint Constructions, we found the evidence was consistent with Westpoint Construction’s entitlement to the amounts invoiced.

    (d) With respect to any taxable supplies being made by Westpoint Realty, we also found there was considerable doubt as to what was supplied by Westpoint Realty as trustee for the Westgem Trust. It was unclear whether deposits (which would have entitled Westpoint Realty to invoice commission under 5.2 of the Agency Agreement) were paid and if so, when they were paid. This was because the Agency Agreement, which was not expressed to be retrospective, was entered into on 1 June 2002 and yet many of the contracts of sale were entered into prior to that time. The uncertainty was further exacerbated by Mr Carey’s reference to an unwritten agreement that he made with himself which he first referred to under cross-examination. We found Mr Carey’s evidence of this other agreement was insufficient to supplement the Agency Agreement, or to otherwise to substantiate an entitlement for Westpoint Realty to charge for work under the subsequent Agency Agreement. The evidence also raised uncertainty about whether sales commission had been previously invoiced. We also did not think the documentation (that is, the Management Agreement) entitled Westpoint Realty to charge a leasing commission upon entry into the Development Agreement because that document fell short of being an agreement for lease. Even if it could be so regarded, the Management Agreement was not retrospective and so we are not satisfied it created an entitlement to any commission that arose prior to it being entered into. Again, Mr Carey referred to another unwritten agreement to buttress his evidence, but we did not think that evidence was sufficient to substantiate the leasing commission. With respect to marketing and legal costs, despite Mr Carey’s evidence about the marketing activities that took place and our finding that the recovery of those costs was possible under the Management Agreement, the documents before us, and the evidence of Ms Rigg, did not show how the specific amounts for marketing and legal fees set out in the project cost summary were calculated.

    (e) The evidence also raises doubt about what was supplied by Westpoint Corporation under the DM Agreement. There was limited supporting evidence as to the amount of the project costs and the project value. Even if we accept Mr Carey’s evidence as to how these sums were arrived at, further doubts are raised by the delay (until December 2005) in invoicing the commissions under the DM Agreement, which were itemised in the Development Management Cost Summary. An entitlement to invoice the first two commissions in that summary (for obtaining development approval and for the commencement of construction) arose in 2001 and 2003, prior to the Development Agreement being made. The DM Agreement is not expressed to be retrospective. Mr Carey also relied upon an undocumented agreement he negotiated himself under item 9 of the Schedule to the DM Agreement to support the final two amounts of commission in the cost summary (for the “day to day management” of the integrated property development process and the management of marketing and selling). However, these included costs incurred for the period from 1 July 2000 to 20 December 2005, approximately three and a half years of which preceded the DM Agreement. There is no evidence that work which preceded the DM Agreement was not previously invoiced. We were also unconvinced by Mr Carey’s explanation that he was deferring charging amounts that could be invoiced as early as July 2000 to December 2005 because he was bringing income to account in preparation for an upcoming IPO.

    (f) There are similar issues with the evidence concerning set-off that we found for the other applications. These included the incomplete handwritten journal entry, a lack of evidence as to whether there was a mutual set-off or that the related companies agreed to the set-off arrangement, a lack of evidence of mutual book entries, and inconsistent reports as to affairs from the receivers and managers and liquidators of Westpoint Constructions, Westpoint Realty and Westpoint Corporation.

    (g) Similarly, the failure to stamp key documents including the Building Contract, Agency Agreement, Management Agreement and DM Agreement raise doubts about provenance, adding to our overall concerns.

  2. Again, there are so many doubts raised by the evidence that we find Market Street has not discharged the burden of proving that the net amount for the December 2005 tax period is less than zero. Specifically, Market Street has not established on the evidence that it made creditable acquisitions under s 11-5 of the GST Act from Westpoint Constructions, Westpoint Realty and Westpoint Corporation whereby Market Street was entitled to input tax credits for GST of $1,082,725, $482,442, and $453,312 (totalling $2,018,480), in whole or in part, for the December 2005 tax period. It follows Market Street has not discharged the burden of proving the assessment was excessive or otherwise incorrect and what it should have been.
  3. Consequently, we find the Market Street Reviewable Decision should be affirmed.

CONCLUSION

  1. For the reasons we have explained in relation to all five developments, we are not satisfied each Applicant has discharged its onus of demonstrating the assessment was excessive or otherwise incorrect and what the assessment should have been. We are grateful to the parties for their assistance in the laborious forensic process of reconstructing the affairs of the Applicants and the Westpoint group. Ultimately, given the state of the evidence – particularly the incomplete and occasionally confusing and contradictory picture painted by the documents, and our hesitations over the reliability of Mr Carey’s assistance – we were not persuaded the Applicants met their burden under s 14ZZK(b) of the TAA. In those circumstances, each of the objection decisions under review must be affirmed.

DECISIONS

  1. The Tribunal affirms the: Earlmist Reviewable Decision; Silkchime Reviewable Decision; Cinema City Reviewable Decision; Bayshore Reviewable Decision; and the Market Street Reviewable Decision.

I certify that the preceding 662 (six hundred and sixty-two) paragraphs are a true copy of the reasons for the decision herein of Deputy President Bernard J McCabe and Senior Member Dr M Evans-Bonner

.....................[SGD]................................

Associate

Dated: 28 April 2023

Date of hearing: 3 November 2021 to 5 November 2021; 15 November 2021 to 18 November 2021; 22 February 2022

Representative for the Applicant: Mr C Sievers of counsel instructed by Mony de Kerloy

Representative for the Respondent: Mr G Davies KC and Ms M Baker of counsel instructed by Jackson MacDonald
